# About the editors

**Dr. Utku Kose** *(Süleyman Demirel University, Isparta, Turkey)*

Dr. Utku Kose received the BS degree in 2008 in computer education from Gazi University, Turkey, as a faculty valedictorian. He received an MS degree in 2010 from Afyon Kocatepe University, Turkey, in the field of computer science and a DS/PhD degree in 2017 from Selcuk University, Turkey, in the field of computer engineering. Between 2009 and 2011, he worked as a Research Assistant at Afyon Kocatepe University. He also worked as a Lecturer and Vocational School—Vice Director at Afyon Kocatepe University between 2011 and 2012, as a Lecturer and Research Center Director at Usak University between 2012 and 2017, and as an Assistant Professor at Süleyman Demirel University between 2017 and 2019. Currently, he is an Associate Professor at Süleyman Demirel University, Turkey. Kose also gave lectures at other higher education institutions such as Gazi University and Istanbul Arel University. He also works as a Visiting Researcher at the University of North Dakota, United States, and holds the Honorary Professor of Artificial Intelligence title at ITM (SLS) Baroda University, India. He has more than 300 publications, including articles, authored and edited books, proceedings, and reports. He is also on editorial boards of many scientific journals and serves as one of the editors of the *Biomedical and Robotics Healthcare* (CRC Press) and *Computational Modeling Applications for Existential Risks* (Elsevier) book series. His research interests include artificial intelligence, machine ethics, artificial intelligence safety, biomedical applications, optimization, chaos theory, distance education, e-learning, computer education, and computer science.

**Dr. Deepak Gupta** *(Maharaja Agrasen Institute of Technology, Delhi, India)*

Dr. Deepak Gupta is an eminent academician and plays versatile roles and responsibilities, juggling between lectures, research, publications, consultancy, community service, PhD and postdoctorate supervision, and so on. With 16 years of rich expertise in teaching and 2 years in industry, he focuses on rational and practical learning. He has contributed to massive literature in the fields of human–computer interaction, intelligent data analysis, nature-inspired computing, machine learning, and soft computing. He is working as an Assistant Professor at the Maharaja Agrasen Institute of Technology (GGSIPU), Delhi, India. He has served as an Editor-in-Chief, Guest Editor, and Associate Editor in SCI and various other reputed journals (IEEE, Elsevier, Springer, Wiley & MDPI). He has actively been organizing various reputed international conferences. He is not only backed with a strong profile, but his innovative ideas, research’s end results, and the notion of implementation of technology in the medical field are, by and large, contributing to society significantly. He completed his postdoc from the Internet of Things research group, National Institute of Telecommunications (Inatel), Brazil, in 2018; eHealth and Telemedicine Group (GTe), University of Valladolid (UVA), Spain, in 2019; Federal University of Piauí (UFPI), Teresina—Pi, Brazil in 2021; and PhD from Dr. APJ Abdul Kalam Technical University, India, in 2017. He has authored/edited 70 books with national/international-level publishers (IEEE Press, Elsevier, Springer, Wiley, CRC, DeGruyter). He has published 330 scientific research publications in reputed international journals and conferences, including 213 SCI-indexed journals of IEEE, Elsevier, Springer, and Wiley. He has also granted/published eight patents. He is the recipient of the 2021 IEEE System Council Best Paper Award and Highly Cited Paper Award in *Applied Sciences* journal. He has been featured in the list of the top 2% scientist/researcher database in the world in 2019, 2020, and 2022, with Rank 51 in AI & Image Processing, Rank 423 in India overall, and Rank 80,604 in the World overall. He has received a grant of Rs 1.31 CR from the Department of Science and Technology against the Indo-Russian Joint call for a project proposal on multiscale modeling of the deformation behavior of carbon fiber–reinforced high-end thermoplastics as CO-PI. He is an Associate Editor of *Alexandria Engineering Journal* (Elsevier), *Computers and Electrical Engineering* (Elsevier), *Expert Systems* (Wiley), *Intelligent Decision Technologies* (IOS Press), and *Journal of Computational and Theoretical Nanoscience*, Honorary Editor of *ICSES Transactions on Image Processing and Pattern Recognition*. He is also a Series Editor of *Biomedical Engineering* and *Computational Modeling for Existential Risks* at Academic Press, Elsevier, *Intelligent Biomedical Data Analysis* at De Gruyter, Germany, *Explainable AI (XAI) for Engineering Applications* and *Artificial Intelligence for Sustainable Engineering and Management* at CRC Press, and *Computational Intelligence for Data Analysis* at Bentham Science. He has been appointed as a Consulting Editor at Elsevier. He is also associated with various professional bodies such as IEEE, ACM, ISTE, IAENG, IACSIT, SCIEI, ICSES, UACEE, Internet Society, SMEI, IAOP, and IAOIP. He has been invited as a Faculty Resource Person/Session Chair/Reviewer/TPC member in different FDP conferences and journals. He is the convener and organizer of the “DoSCI” and “ICCCNet” Springer conference series. He has also been associated with a research-based startup “ADROIDCONNECTZ.”

**Dr. Jose Antonio Marmolejo-Saucedo** *(Universidad Nacional Autonoma de Mexico, Mexico City, Mexico)*

Dr. Jose Antonio Marmolejo is a Professor at the National Autonomous University of Mexico, Mexico. His focus is on operations research, large-scale optimization techniques, computational techniques, analytical methods for planning, operations, control of electric energy and logistic systems, sustainable supply chain design, and digital twins in supply chains. He is a member of the Network for Decision Support and Intelligent Optimization of Complex and Large-Scale Systems, the Mexican Society for Operations Research, and the System Dynamics Society. He is the author of more than 30 research articles in science citation index journals, books, book chapters, conference proceedings, and presentations.

 

# Foreword

The Earth passed through different ages and encountered many transformations since the start of everything. According to the findings, we somehow know about the Ice Age and other massive events, which changed everything radically for living organisms and ecosystems. Most of these events were actually occurrences that appeared slowly but showed high impacts at the end. Currently, we are experiencing a similar case because of the climate change. It seems that intense industrial processes and extreme use of technology are causing more carbon emissions and leading the climate change to speed up. For especially last 10 years, the effects of climate change have been worse and the change become a crisis. So, it is crucial to build effective solutions as well as prevention techniques. To do these, one way is to focus on the underlying factors and analyzing them via mathematical models.

Except from modeling systems and running tests with them, mathematical and logical background of computational methodologies also opens the doors for further analyses and accurate estimations. When we think about current research interests, these efforts can be examined under computational modeling and moved further through advanced methodologies such as data science, data analytics, and artificial intelligence. Basically, system components corresponding to the underlying factors of climate change can be carefully analyzed with computational modeling. In this way, relations among the components can be used accordingly to explain past or present cases and derive estimations for the future states. Eventually, computational modeling is among the useful tools to analyze the climate crisis and enhance ongoing research studies widely.

Considering the focus on computational modeling for climate crisis, this volume, titled *Computational Modeling Applications for Climate Crisis*, gathers the most recent studies and ensures a timely contribution to the literature. The chapters included in the book analyze and discuss about direct or indirect factors having effects on the climate change. I can express that especially review-based chapters enable readers to have information about essential concepts and even further topics covering the issue of climate change from different perspectives. Furthermore, the readers can read about how different areas have connections with global warming, extreme events, and broadly climate crisis and how these connections can be analyzed through mathematical definitions. I find the chapters targeting specific regions of the world remarkable since alternative outputs from such studies are helpful to figure out missing pieces of the whole puzzle of climate change. Chapters generally ensure good discussion points and expose further research ways. This book also has some chapters regarding artificial intelligence applications, which I believe have big potentials in automating the models for successful solutions. Specifically, I can suggest this book as a reference source for researchers and graduate students enrolled in engineering disciplines, mathematics, statistics, and computer science. The science of climatology would also find important findings and discussions from this book. Of course, the book will be very useful for all fields actively working on the climate crisis.

As my final words, I would like to express my deepest gratitude to Dr. Kose, Dr. Gupta, and Dr. Marmolejo Saucedo for giving me the opportunity to write a Foreword for such a timely work. I would like to also congratulate the editors and all authors for their endless efforts to end up this book. I highly suggest all readers to be lost among pages and come out with the innovative ideas in the way of further studies. With all my best wishes for the global efforts against the climate crisis.

**Dr. Jafar Alzubi**

*Al-Balqa Applied University, Jordan*

 

# Preface

Utku Kose, Deepak Gupta and Jose Antonio Marmolejo-Saucedo

Along the long history of humankind, the planet Earth brought many challenges caused by its dynamic nature and evolving biological conditions. It is remarkable that the humankind has been always curious to understand the Earth and deal with the challenges by designing solution ways. These solution ways have been connected with especially inventions and discoveries. It is also important that the technology has been an essential component to understand the conditions deeply and advance the solution ways further. Eventually, today’s modern life seems more controllable thanks to high technology as well as more knowledge and abilities we have. However, more control over the Earth has been associated with more level of human activities which started to affect the biological conditions negatively. We currently face the threat of low natural resources, massive health problems, and extreme events caused by the climate change. Especially climate change is already an existential problem, as it showed many negative impacts within the first quarter of the 21st century.

Climate is known as a critical factor for the well-being and existence of all living organisms. It affects the well-being of generations and the sustainability of natural resources. Throughout the history, the climate has been always in a changing nature in terms of temperature levels and weather patterns. But human activities and industrial revolutions accelerated in the 20th century caused more negative changes to transform the climate change into an existential crisis. Considering especially intense use of fossil fuels and high technology, greenhouse gas emissions started to cause continuous increase in temperature values. Moreover, the climate today brings more extreme events such as storms, tornados, and heat waves. The glaciers are melting and the sea levels are rising at unexpected levels. All these situations are threat for the existence of humans, animals, and the habitat, which are all vital components of the sustainable life. So, there is a red alert for the climate crisis and there has been a worldwide intense effort to find appropriate solutions for lowering the effects of human activities as well as the high technology. Briefly, the climate crisis is an issue, which needs focus from all fields of the life. Among these efforts, there is still hope to build effective research applications, which are targeting optimized activities and use of natural resources to keep the climate change at the stable level for the next time period. At this point, it is important to inform the audience about the latest academic–scientific developments and understand more about the positive outcomes.

This book *Computational Modeling Applications for Climate Crisis* provides a recent collection covering modeling perspectives applied to climate change problems. The motivation of this book has been built over research perspectives, which consider mathematical aspects of climate components to derive descriptive or predictive results at the end. As the life itself is a mixture of mathematical occurrences, it is important to work with alternative computational models, which are useful for evaluating the dynamics of climate. We also believe that computational modeling should be combined with the most recent, emerging technologies so that the capabilities of solution designs can be enhanced. In this context, the book hosts a total of 12 chapters having scientific efforts by the authors around the world. In general, the book covers not only research studies but also extensive review works in the context of computational modeling and climate crisis relation. In detail, the topics include specific research problems such as precipitation forecasting, rainfall forecasting, zero carbon footprint, meteorological drought, and general weather forecasts. This book also provides views about computational modeling for agriculture and tourism. Furthermore, it is also possible to see discussions regarding responsible technology use and sustainability at the time of climate change. Because artificial intelligence reveals innovative computational solution techniques for challenging problems, some chapters are also devoted to it. These chapters include the use of traditional machine learning, quantum machine learning, deep learning, multiagent systems, and optimization-oriented approaches.

It can be seen that the active dynamics covering the life are changing rapidly, which causes the climate crisis to expand accordingly. To deal with climate crisis, one way is interpreting the parameters of systems through models, analyzing the data, and developing advanced technologies. This book accommodates the most recent advancements, which enable readers to learn about current conditions and encourage them to think about further research opportunities. We hope this reference work will be useful for the international audience and help in establishing a better future. We would like to thank all respectful authors, who made this book project a success. We also thank Dr. Jafar Alzubi for his foreword, including valuable discussions about the climate change and kind words regarding this book. Of course, we send our special thanks to all readers, who are interested in climate crisis research and alternative solutions and strategies to deal with it. Thanks for your attention. Stay aware, and responsible!

# Artificial intelligence and optimized computational modelling against climate crisis

Sachin Sharma^([1](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#aff1)), Ranu Tyagi^([1](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#aff1)) and Seshadri Mohan^([2](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#aff2)),    ¹Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Graphic Era Deemed to be University, Dehradun, Uttarakhand, India,    ²Systems Engineering Department, University of Arkansas at Little Rock, Little Rock, AR, United States

 

## Abstract

The climate crisis is one of the first crushing issues standing up to our planet, and addressing it requires inventive courses of action that utilize the control of advancement. Artificial intelligence (AI) and optimized computational showing have developed promising devices for dealing with this complex issue. AI can be utilized to dismember huge amount of climate data, recognize plans, and make estimates around future climate designs. By leveraging machine learning calculations, examiners can make more accurate climate models that can help policymakers make informed choices about climate action. Optimized computational illustration is another competent gadget for addressing the climate crisis. By reenacting climate systems and their intelligence, investigators can better understand the fundamental disobedient driving climate modification and make strategies for calming its influence. Together, AI and optimized computational illustration offer an able approach to understanding and responding to the climate crisis. In any case, there are vital challenges related to their utilization. These join concerns around data security, algorithmic predisposition, and the ethical suggestions of utilizing advancement to make choices that influence people’s lives. The potential benefits of AI and optimized computational display in the fight against climate change are noteworthy. By leveraging these progresses, we will choose a deeper understanding of the climate crisis and make more fruitful procedures for relieving its influence. In any case, it is imperative to carefully consider the ethical and social recommendations of their utilization and to ensure that these developments are sent in a competent and direct way.

 

### Keywords

Artificial intelligence; optimized computational modelling; climate crisis

 

## 1.1 Introduction

Artificial intelligence (AI) might be a division of computer science that deals with making machines that can perform assignments that routinely require human experiences, such as visual acknowledgement, talk affirmation, decision-making, and dialect interpretation ([Kaack et al., 2022](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib1)). AI systems utilize calculations, genuine models, and computational control to analyze gigantic amounts of data, learn from plans and designs, and make estimates or choices based on that information. The climate emergency implies the complex and interconnected challenges related to climate change, counting the world temperatures, extended repeat and earnestness of uncommon climate events, rising sea levels, and the misfortune of biodiversity ([Ajagekar & You, 2022](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib2)). The climate crisis is caused basically by human work, particularly the burning of fossil fuels, deforestation, and farming. Optimized Computational Modeling may be a strategy utilized to reproduce complex systems and expect their conduct underneath unmistakable scenarios ([Filho et al., 2022](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib3)). It incorporates making numerical models that talk to the key components and insights inside a system and utilizing computational control to reproduce how those components modify over time in reaction to different inputs and conditions. AI and optimized computational modeling can be used as accessible tools in the fight against the climate crisis. By analyzing broad entireties of data from sensors, satellites, and other sources, AI frameworks can offer assistance to analysts and policymakers in better understanding how climate modification is impacting assorted regions and natural frameworks ([Entezari et al., 2023](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib4)). AI can also help recognize plans and designs in climate data that might not be immediately clear to individuals and make desires about nearly future changes inside the climate. Optimized computational modeling can help analysts and policymakers reenact the impacts of particular approach choices or mechanical intercessions on the climate. This will provide guidance on which exercises are most likely to have the most prominent influence on decreasing nursery gas surges or diminishing the impacts of climate modification ([Rezaiy & Shabri, 2023](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib5)). By and large, AI and optimized computational modeling are successful devices that can offer assistance to us and address the challenges of the climate crisis. By handling the control of these progresses, we will develop more compelling methods for directing the impacts of climate change and building a more viable future.

 

## 1.2 Overview of climate crisis

The climate crisis could be a complex and interconnected set of challenges related to climate change, which is the long-term move in worldwide climate designs caused basically by human activities such as the burning of fossil fuels, deforestation, and mechanical agribusiness. The resulting changes to the Earth’s climate are having broad impacts on environments, economies, and human social order. The foremost unmistakable effect of the climate crisis is the rise in worldwide temperatures, which is causing more visits and extreme heatwaves, droughts, and fierce blazes ([Himeur et al., 2022](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib6)). Rising temperatures are also causing the polar ice caps to melt, driving rising ocean levels that debilitate coastal communities and foundations. The climate crisis is also causing more visits and extreme climate events, such as typhoons, surges, and storms ([Rane, 2023](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib7)). These events can cause broad harm to property, foundations, and crops, driving financial misfortune and uprooting populations. Another major effect of the climate emergency is the loss of biodiversity, as environments are disturbed by changes in temperature and precipitation patterns. This can have far-reaching results for human social orders, as biodiversity is basic for giving biological system administrations such as fertilization, soil ripeness, and carbon sequestration ([Polo-Mendoza et al., 2023](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib8)). The climate crisis could be a global challenge that requires a facilitated reaction from governments, businesses, and people ([Pekpostalci et al., 2023](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib9)). This reaction must include a move toward more feasible solutions, such as decreasing nursery gas outflows, advancing renewable vitality, and contributing to climate adjustment measures.

 

## 1.3 Proposed methodology

AI and optimized computational roles play vital parts in addressing the climate emergency. Here are a few ways in which these innovations can be proposed and connected:

*   • Upgraded climate models: Utilize AI to move climate models forward by consolidating machine learning calculations that can analyze endless sums of climate information. This will lead to more precise expectations and recreations of future climate scenarios.
*   • Early caution frameworks: Develop AI-driven early caution frameworks for extraordinary climate phenomena, such as typhoons, surges, and dry spells. These frameworks can offer assistance to communities to get ready and react more successfully.
*   • Smart networks: Actualize AI within the administration of shrewd frameworks to optimize vitality conveyance, diminish vitality misfortunes, and coordinate renewable vitality sources more productively.
*   • Building vitality administration: Utilize AI to optimize vitality utilization in buildings through prescient analytics, keen sensors, and mechanized control frameworks.
*   • Grid integration: Utilize AI to upgrade the integration of renewable vitality sources into the control network, adjusting supply and request to guarantee a steady and dependable vitality supply.
*   • Vitality capacity optimization: Utilize AI to optimize vitality capacity frameworks, anticipate request designs, and effectively oversee vitality capacity assets.
*   • Optimized carbon capture: Apply AI to optimize the forms of carbon capture and capacity, making them more proficient and cost-effective.
*   • Monitoring and verification: Utilize AI to screen and confirm the viability of carbon capture and sequestration ventures, guaranteeing that captured carbon remains put away securely.
*   • Accuracy farming: Actualize AI in horticulture for exact cultivating, optimizing asset utilization, decreasing squandering, and minimizing natural effects.
*   • Timberland observing: Utilize AI to monitor and oversee timberlands, recognizing deforestation, surveying biodiversity, and recognizing unlawful logging.
*   • Infrastructure planning: Utilize AI to arrange and plan a framework that is versatile to the impacts of climate change, such as rising ocean levels, extraordinary climate occasions, and changing precipitation designs.
*   • Supply chain resilience: Apply AI to survey and improve the versatility of supply chains to climate-related disturbances.
*   • Situation examination: Utilize AI to analyze different climate change scenarios and their potential impacts, giving policymakers data-driven experiences for decision-making.
*   • Approach optimization: Utilize AI to optimize arrangement mediations and recognize the most compelling techniques for moderating and adjusting to climate change.
*   • Open mindfulness and engagement: Develop AI-driven instructive instruments to raise mindfulness around climate change, its causes, and potential arrangements.
*   • Community engagement: Utilize AI to encourage community engagement and collaboration in tending to nearby climate challenges.

The flowchart consists of the following steps:

*   • Information collection: Collect climate information from different sources, counting satellites, sensors, and climate-checking stations.
*   • Information preprocessing: Utilize AI calculations to preprocess and clean the collected information, dealing with lost values and exceptions.
*   • Climate modeling: Apply optimized computational models, joining AI methods to improve climate models for precision.
*   • Expectation and recreation: Utilize AI to anticipate future climate scenarios and reenact the effects of climate change.
*   • Early caution framework: Coordinated AI calculations to create an early caution framework for extraordinary climate phenomena based on the climate demonstrate expectations.
*   • Vitality productivity: Apply AI to optimizing vitality utilization and conveyance.
*   • Actualize savvy networks and build vitality administration frameworks.
*   • Renewable vitality integration: Utilize AI for effective integration of renewable vitality sources into the control network.
*   • Carbon capture and sequestration: Apply AI to optimize carbon capture forms. Created AI-driven checking frameworks for confirmation.
*   • Common asset administration: Utilized AI for exactness in horticulture to optimize asset utilization and executed AI-based woodland checking for biodiversity and unlawful action location.
*   • Climate change adjustment: Utilize AI to arrange and plan a strong foundation. Optimized supply chain flexibility utilizing AI calculations.
*   • Approach and choice back: Analyze climate change scenarios utilizing AI. Applied AI for optimizing approach intercessions.
*   • Open mindfulness and engagement: Create AI-driven instructive devices. Encouraged community engagement through the AI stage.
*   • Feedback loop: Executed criticism circles to ceaselessly make strides in models based on real-world information and changing conditions.

The proposed optimal computational model includes a computationally scalable algorithm suitable for handling complex real-world problems. The proposed algorithm is a population-based algorithm to solve problems with continuous values. The proposed algorithm can be widely applied as an optimization algorithm to solve complex problems in various engineering fields. The proposed algorithm and meta-heuristic algorithms, such as genetic algorithms, have similar operators, including crossover, mutation, and selection. However, there are some differences between them, such as that there is no preferential local search in the genetic algorithm, while the proposed algorithm supports preferential local search using different operators. In addition, the proposed algorithm is based on mutation operation, while the genetic algorithm is based on hybridization. Like other evolutionary algorithms, the proposed algorithm starts by randomly generating an initial population of 0 without a climate crisis. Then, after initialization, the search space is expanded by mutation. CC[*i*] is the mutation solution vector of EE[*j*] calculated based on [Eq. (1–1)](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#eqn1):

*C**C*[*i*] = EE[*j*1] + *O*[*r*].(*E**E*[*j*3] − EE[*j*2])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">CC</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mtext>EE</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">j1</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>O</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">r</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>.</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">EE</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">j3</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mtext>EE</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">j2</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math> (1–1)

![Equation](images/F000067si1.png)(1–1) 

where O[r] is the optimized scaling factor varying in the range \[0, 1\] and determines the length of the mutation step. EE[j1], EE[j2], and EE[j3] are solution vectors that are randomly selected, with the condition expressed by [Eq. (1–2)](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#eqn2):

$\\begin{array}{l} \\left. {EE}\_{j1},{EE}\_{j2}{and}{EE}\_{j3} \\middle| \\text{j1} \\neq {j2} \\neq {j3} \\neq \\mathit{i} \\right. \\\\ {\\mathit{i} = \\left\\lbrack {1, 2,\\ldots\\ldots.\\ldots,\\text{NP}} \\right\\rbrack} \\\\ \\end{array}$<mml:math><mml:mtable columnalign="left"><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">EE</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">j1</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">EE</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">j2</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">and</mml:mi><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">EE</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">j3</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>|</mml:mo><mml:mtext>j1</mml:mtext><mml:mo>&#8800;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">j2</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8800;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">j3</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8800;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">i</mml:mi></mml:mtd></mml:mtr><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">\[</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#8230;</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#8230;</mml:mi><mml:mo>.</mml:mo><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#8230;</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mtext>NP</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">\]</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mtd></mml:mtr></mml:mtable></mml:math> (1–2)

![Equation](images/F000067si2.png)(1–2) 

where *i* is the index of the current solution. The optimized trial climate vector (C[*ij*]) is produced by mixing the mutated vector and the parent vector in a crossover operation based on [Eq. (1–3)](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#eqn3):

$\\begin{array}{l} {C\_{ij}{= \\text{CC}}\_{ij} A\_{k} \\leq \\text{CR},} \\\\ {or} \\\\ {C\_{ij}{= \\text{EE}}\_{ij} A\_{k} > \\text{CR},} \\\\ {k = 1,\\text{2},\\ldots\\ldots\\ldots\\ldots.., n} \\\\ \\end{array}$<mml:math><mml:mtable columnalign="left"><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mtext>CC</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">A</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8804;</mml:mo><mml:mtext>CR</mml:mtext><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:mtd></mml:mtr><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">or</mml:mi></mml:mtd></mml:mtr><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mtext>EE</mml:mtext></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">A</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#62;</mml:mo><mml:mtext>CR</mml:mtext><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:mtd></mml:mtr><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:mtext>2</mml:mtext><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#8230;</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#8230;</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#8230;</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#8230;</mml:mi><mml:mn>.</mml:mn><mml:mo>.</mml:mo><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mtd></mml:mtr></mml:mtable></mml:math> (1–3)

![Equation](images/F000067si3.png)(1–3) 

where A[*k*] is an arbitrarily chosen real number in the interval between 0 and 1. The CR is a crossover constant. If the A[*k*] is less than or equal to CR, the optimized trial climate vector (C[*ij*]) is inherited from the mutant solution vector, otherwise, the CR is considered equal to EE[*ij*].

 

## 1.4 Advantages

The following are the advantages of artificial intelligence and optimized computational modeling against the climate crisis ([Fig. 1–1](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#f0010)):

*   • Predictive capabilities: AI and computational modeling can analyze huge sums of information from different sources and recreate complex frameworks, permitting us to form more exact expectations about future climate designs, climate occasions, and natural impacts ([Pekpostalci et al., 2023](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib9)).
*   • Faster processing: AI can process large amounts of information in real-time, making it conceivable to respond rapidly to changes in climate patterns and moderate the impacts of extraordinary climate occasions. This may also offer assistance in recognizing unused climate dangers and focusing on interventions to address them ([Alassery et al., 2022](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib10)).
*   • Improved decision making: AI and computational modeling can give decision-makers more comprehensive and exact data, making a difference in their educated choices about climate relief and adjustment measures ([Polo-Mendoza et al., 2023](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib8)).
*   • Identifying trends: AI can offer assistance in recognizing new trends and designs in climate information, which will be troublesome for people to distinguish. This could give modern experiences into the drivers of climate change and offer assistance in distinguishing modern arrangements to address it ([Nishant et al., 2020](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib11)).
*   • Optimizing resource use: Computational modeling can offer assistance to optimize asset utilization, such as renewable vitality, to maximize productivity and decrease squandering. This may offer assistance to quicken the move to a low-carbon economy ([Cowls et al., 2021](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib12)).
*   • Reducing costs: AI and computational modeling can offer assistance in diminishing costs related to climate research and intercessions by streamlining data collection and investigation and recognizing the most successful mediations ([Rane, 2023](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib7)).

![Figure 1–1 Advantages.](images/F000067f01-01-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 1–1](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#Bf0010) Advantages.

 

## 1.5 Disadvantages

The following are the disadvantages of artificial intelligence and optimized computational modelling against climate crisis ([Fig. 1–2](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#f0015)):

*   • Data bias: AI models are only as great as the information they are trained on, and if the information is one-sided or incomplete, the model’s expectations can be wrong or deceiving. This could lead to inaccurate suspicions about the causes and impacts of climate change.
*   • Lack of human input: AI models can be created based exclusively on information inputs without taking into consideration the broader context and human values. This could lead to solutions that prioritize effectiveness over value or fail to consider the total range of social and natural impacts.
*   • Complexity: AI and computational models can be exceptionally complex, making it troublesome for nonexperts to get them and evaluate what comes about. This will make it difficult to communicate the findings to policymakers and the public ([Rezaiy and Shabri, 2023](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib5)).
*   • Technical challenges: AI and computational modeling require noteworthy specialized mastery, frameworks, and assets. This can constrain access to these instruments for smaller organizations or developing nations that will not have the assets to contribute to these advances ([Bliss et al., 2021](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib13)).
*   • Energy consumption: The computational control required to run AI models and simulations can consume a critical amount of energy, contributing to greenhouse gas emissions and worsening the climate crisis.
*   • Ethical concerns: AI and computational modeling can raise ethical concerns related to protection, straightforwardness, and responsibility. These concerns can be particularly critical within the context of the climate approach, where choices have far-reaching impacts on human social orders and environments ([Hernández et al., 2022](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib16)).

![Figure 1–2 Disadvantages.](images/F000067f01-02-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 1–2](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#Bf0015) Disadvantages.

 

## 1.6 Characteristics

The following are the characteristics of artificial intelligence and optimized computational modeling against climate crisis ([Fig. 1–3](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#f0020)):

*   • Machine learning: AI frameworks can learn from information and make strides in their execution over time without being expressly modified.
*   • Natural language processing: AI frameworks can understand and interpret human language.
*   • Computer vision: AI systems can analyze and translate visual data, such as pictures and recordings.
*   • Robotics: AI frameworks can control machines to perform physical assignments, such as assembly line generation and space investigation.
*   • Decision-making: AI frameworks can make choices based on data analysis and logical reasoning.
*   • Climate modeling: Computational models can mimic the Earth’s climate framework, counting the climate, seas, arrival surface, and ice cover, to foresee the impacts of climate change ([Ali & Thakkar, 2023](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#bib15)).
*   • Renewable energy optimization: AI can be utilized to optimize the plan and operation of renewable energy frameworks, such as wind and solar-powered control, to maximize their proficiency and decrease costs.
*   • Carbon capture and storage: Computational models can be utilized to plan and optimize carbon capture and storage frameworks that capture carbon dioxide emanations from control plants and other sources and store them underground.
*   • Sustainable agriculture: AI can offer assistance in optimizing agricultural practices to diminish nursery gas outflows, increase nourishment generation, and preserve common assets.
*   • Disaster prediction and management: AI can offer assistance in anticipating and managing the impacts of normal disasters, such as floods and droughts, which are likely to extend in recurrence and seriousness due to climate change.

## 1.7 Challenges

The following are the challenges of artificial intelligence and optimized computational modeling against the climate crisis ([Fig. 1–4](#Ch001_1-13_B9780443219054000067.xhtml#f0025)):

*   • Information inclination: AI frameworks depend on huge sums of information to memorize and make choices, but if the information is one-sided, the AI system’s yield will also be one-sided.
*   • Need for transparency: Numerous AI frameworks are dark, making it troublesome to understand how they work, and making it challenging to recognize blunders or predispositions.
*   • Security and reliability: AI frameworks can have startling behaviors or make blunders, leading to security concerns and doubt in their reliability.
*   • Direction and morals: There are few controls overseeing the advancement and utilization of AI, and moral concerns, such as protection and segregation, must be carefully considered.
*   • Adaptability: AI frameworks can be computationally serious, making it troublesome to scale them up to handle huge datasets or complex errands.
*   • Limited information: Climate information is regularly deficient, conflicting, or wrong, making it challenging to create precise computational models.
*   • Complexity: The Earth’s climate framework is complex, with numerous forbidding factors and criticism circles, making it troublesome to precisely show.
*   • Uncertainty: Climate modeling includes numerous obscure components and suspicions, driving vulnerability within the exactness of forecasts.
*   • Resource-intensive: Computational modeling requires critical computing control, information capacity, and human assets, making it costly and troublesome to scale.
*   • Policy usage: Indeed, on the off chance that computational models precisely foresee the impacts of climate change, executing arrangements to moderate those impacts requires political will and openness.

## 1.8 Case study

One example of the utilization of artificial intelligence and optimized computational modeling against climate emergencies is the DeepMind extend, in which analysts at Google’s DeepMind utilized AI to make strides in the precision of wind vitality generation figures. The extend utilized machine learning calculations to analyze information from climate figures, control generation, and other sources to foresee wind speed and heading more precisely. This permitted wind ranches to adjust their vitality generation to coordinate the anticipated yield, lessening the need to depend on reinforcement vitality sources and increasing the general productivity of the wind cultivate. The venture has been effective in moving forward wind vitality generation estimates, decreasing costs, and expanding renewable vitality utilization. The AI calculations utilized within the venture are also connected to other renewable vitality sources, such as solar-based and hydroelectric control. Another illustration is the utilization of computational modeling to understand and anticipate the impacts of climate change on horticulture. Analysts at the International Center for Tropical Agriculture have developed a computational show that reenacts the impacts of climate change on crop yields in several districts of the world. They show employment information on temperature, precipitation, soil quality, and other components to anticipate how crops will be influenced by climate change. This data can assist agriculturists and policymakers in making choices about which crops to develop, where to develop them, and how to oversee them to diminish the impacts of climate change on agribusiness. The demonstration has been utilized to anticipate the impacts of climate change on coffee generation in Central America, where rising temperatures and changing precipitation designs are debilitating the region’s coffee industry. The demonstration has been utilized to recognize which coffee assortments are most likely to outlive in a changing climate and to create procedures for adjusting to the changing conditions. By and large, these cases demonstrate the potential of counterfeit insights and optimized computational modeling to address the climate emergency by moving forward the effectiveness of renewable vitality generation and making a difference in agriculturists and policymakers making choices to moderate the impacts of climate change on agribusiness.

## 1.9 Opportunities

 

*   • Climate modeling: AI and computational modeling can assist in making more precise and advanced models of the Earth’s climate framework, counting how the climate, seas, and arrival are associated. These models can assist us in way better anticipating future climate scenarios and educating approaches and activities to moderate and adjust to the impacts of climate change.
*   • Renewable vitality: AI can help optimize the sending and operation of renewable vitality frameworks, such as solar-powered and wind power, by analyzing climate designs and anticipating vitality requests. This will offer assistance to make renewable vitality more productive, solid, and cost-effective.
*   • Farming: AI can offer assistance to agriculturists to optimize trim generation and diminish squandering by giving real-time information on soil health, weather designs, and plant development. This may offer assistance to diminish nursery gas emanations from horticulture and move forward nourishment security.
*   • Climate risk assessment: AI can offer assistance in recognizing and surveying climate-related risks, such as sea-level rise, extreme climate events, and biological system disturbances. This will offer assistance in educated decision-making and arranging to decrease these dangers and make strides in flexibility.
*   • Carbon capture and storage: AI can offer assistance to optimize the advancement and operation of carbon capture and storage innovations, which can expel carbon dioxide from the environment and store it underground. This could offer assistance to diminish greenhouse gas outflows and moderate the pace of climate change.

 

## 1.10 Future perspectives

The future viewpoints of artificial intelligence and optimized computational modeling against the climate crisis are promising, with numerous openings to move forward our understanding of climate change, create more proficient and feasible energy frameworks, and moderate the impacts of climate change on horticulture, normal assets, and human communities. A few potential future improvements incorporate:

*   • Improved predictive capabilities: AI and computational modeling can assist us in better understanding the complex connections between different variables within the climate framework, permitting us to create more exact expectations around the long-standing impacts of climate change.
*   • Increased effectiveness and scalability: As AI and computational modeling innovation proceed to move forward, they will get to be more productive and scalable, permitting us to prepare and analyze larger sums of information and mimic more complex frameworks.
*   • Greater integration with real-world frameworks: As AI and computational modeling become more modern, they can be coordinated more closely with real-world frameworks, such as vitality lattices and transportation systems, to optimize their productivity and diminish their natural effect.
*   • More robust arrangement decision-making: By giving policymakers more exact data about the impacts of climate change and the effectiveness of diverse moderation and adjustment techniques, AI and computational modeling can offer assistance in making more successful approach choices.
*   • Modern applications in preservation and asset administration: AI and computational modeling can be utilized to create more successful preservation and asset administration methodologies, such as recognizing regions of high biodiversity value or anticipating the impacts of land-use change on water assets.

 

## 1.11 Conclusion

AI and optimized computational modeling have incredible potential to address the climate crisis by moving forward our understanding of the complex climate framework, creating more effective and economical energy frameworks, and moderating the impacts of climate change on agribusiness, normal assets, and human communities. In any case, these advances confront noteworthy challenges, such as information predisposition, the need for straightforwardness, restricted information, complexity, and instability. To fully realize the potential of AI and computational modeling against the climate emergency, it is vital to address these challenges and moral contemplations and guarantee that these innovations are created and utilized in a capable and feasible way. This requires collaboration between researchers, policymakers, and the public to guarantee that AI and computational modeling are created and utilized morally and mindfully. With continued innovation and advancement, AI and computational modeling can offer us superior understanding of the impacts of climate change and develop effective moderation and adjustment methodologies, driving us toward a more economical and strong future.
