# Computational analysis of recurrent neural network models for precipitation forecasting: a case study in Burdur, Türkiye

Ahmet Çifci^([1](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#aff1)), İsmail Kırbaş^([2](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#aff2)) and Osman Tayfun Bişkin^([1](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#aff1)),    ¹Department of Electrical-Electronics Engineering, Faculty of Engineering and Architecture, Burdur Mehmet Akif Ersoy University, Burdur, Turkey,    ²Department of Computer Engineering, Faculty of Engineering and Architecture, Burdur Mehmet Akif Ersoy University, Burdur, Turkey

 

## Abstract

Drought, a gradual and predictable disaster resulting from a decline in precipitation and water scarcity, is worsened by climate change, turning it into a local and global natural catastrophe. Addressing the impacts of reduced precipitation necessitates accurate forecasting, emphasizing the importance of anticipating droughts and efficiently managing water resources. Moreover, excessive rainfall leading to floods poses significant economic and social challenges, underscoring the need for precise precipitation predictions. Meteorological stations, satellite imagery, and weather radars are traditional tools employed in precipitation forecasting, relying on statistical methods correlating precipitation with atmospheric variables. However, these methods are computationally expensive and less practical for forecasting due to the nonlinear nature of precipitation amounts. To overcome this problem, machine learning and deep learning algorithms have become popular for precipitation prediction. Since precipitation data include both spatial and temporal information, the challenging nature of the problem requires the use of temporal information. In this chapter, we deployed recurrent models such as recurrent neural networks (RNNs), gated recurrent units, and long short-term memory networks for predicting the precipitation based on meteorological data from 2001 to 2018 in Burdur Province, Türkiye. The results demonstrate that RNNs and their variations are effective for predicting precipitation.

 

### Keywords

Climate change; computational analysis; drought; precipitation forecasting; recurrent models

 

## 2.1 Introduction

Drought is a natural phenomenon that can have devastating impacts on society and the environment ([Haile, Tang, Li, et al., 2020](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib12); [Maleksaeidi et al., 2016](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib17); [Rockström, 2003](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib23); [Smucker, 2012](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib27); [Wilhite et al., 2014](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib33)). It occurs when there is a prolonged period of below-average precipitation, resulting in a shortage of water for human and ecological needs. Drought can lead to water shortages, land degradation, crop failure, and livestock loss, as well as social and economic disruption ([Hermans & McLeman, 2021](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib14); [Miyan, 2015](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib18); [Nguyen et al., 2023](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib20)). In recent years, the frequency and intensity of drought events have increased due to climate change, making it even more important to understand the causes of drought and develop strategies to mitigate its effects. Warmer temperatures caused by climate change are causing increased evaporation, leading to drier conditions in some areas ([Haile, Tang, Hosseini-Moghari, et al., 2020](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib13)). In addition, changes in land use, such as deforestation, can reduce the amount of water available for plants and animals, exacerbating the effects of drought ([Chakravarty et al., 2012](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib5)). It is therefore important to conserve water and protect ecosystems in order to reduce the risk of drought and its associated impacts.

Drought is a recurring and increasingly pressing environmental issue in Türkiye. Situated at the crossroads of Europe and Asia, Türkiye is blessed with a diverse climate and rich water resource potential. However, climate change, population growth, and unsustainable water management practices have made drought a serious and recurrent threat to the country’s ecosystems, agriculture, and water supply. Climate change has disrupted Türkiye’s traditional weather patterns. Rising temperatures and changing precipitation patterns have led to more frequent and prolonged droughts. The country’s southern, southeastern, and central regions are particularly vulnerable ([Serkendiz & Tatlı, 2023](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib25)). Türkiye’s water resources are unevenly distributed geographically, with the majority of the available water resources concentrated in the country’s northeast. This makes water scarcity a significant issue in other regions, such as the Mediterranean and Aegean coasts and the eastern part of Anatolia. Agriculture is a vital sector of the Turkish economy, and it heavily relies on irrigation ([Özerol et al., 2012](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib21)). Droughts have had severe consequences for farmers, leading to reduced crop yields, increased production costs, and economic hardships. To address this, Türkiye has been working on improving water use efficiency and implementing drought-resistant farming practices ([Yavaşlı & Erlat, 2023](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib34)). Droughts also take a toll on Türkiye’s diverse ecosystems, including forests, wetlands, and rivers. Reduced water flow in rivers, for example, can disrupt aquatic habitats and harm fish populations. These environmental consequences have prompted efforts to protect and restore ecosystems affected by drought. Türkiye has been investing in water infrastructure projects, such as dams and reservoirs, to manage its water resources more effectively ([Altinbilek & Hatipoglu, 2020](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib1)). These projects aim to store water during wet periods for use during dry spells. However, these efforts have also raised concerns about their environmental and social impacts, including displacements and disruptions to river ecosystems. Recognizing the seriousness of the drought issue, Türkiye has developed a National Drought Management Strategy and Action Plan for 2017–23 ([MoAF, 2017](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib19)). This plan focuses on improving monitoring, early warning systems, and drought mitigation strategies. Cooperation with neighboring countries on water management is also crucial, as many of Türkiye’s rivers are transboundary. Public awareness and education campaigns are essential for fostering water conservation practices among the general population. Promoting responsible water usage and the importance of water-saving technologies can help reduce the impact of drought.

Burdur, a province located in southwestern Türkiye, is no stranger to the challenges posed by drought. Like many regions in Türkiye, it has faced recurring periods of water scarcity and reduced rainfall, leading to significant impacts on agriculture, ecosystems, and daily life for its residents ([Reihanifar et al., 2023](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib22)). Burdur Province is located in a semiarid region characterized with hot and dry summers. The province is home to several lakes, including Lake Burdur, which is a critical water source for both agricultural and domestic use. However, reduced precipitation in recent years has taken a toll on these water bodies ([Davraz et al., 2019](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib37)). Agriculture is a cornerstone of Burdur’s economy, with a focus on fruit orchards as well as vegetable cultivation. Drought conditions have led to decreased crop yields and increased production costs. Farmers often struggle to secure enough water for irrigation, resulting in economic hardships for the agricultural sector. Burdur relies on its lakes and reservoirs for water supply, and the prolonged droughts have depleted their water levels. This has prompted concerns about the sustainability of the region’s water supply for both agricultural and domestic purposes. Local authorities have implemented water management measures to address this issue, including water rationing during dry periods. Drought has also impacted the province’s ecosystems, including wetlands and bird habitats. Reduced water levels in lakes and rivers can disrupt the balance of local ecosystems, impacting flora and fauna. These environmental consequences highlight the need for conservation efforts and sustainable water management practices. The people of Burdur have had to adapt to changing water availability. This includes adopting more efficient irrigation methods, such as drip irrigation, and exploring drought-resistant crop varieties. Local communities also play an essential role in raising awareness about water conservation and taking measures to reduce water waste.

Predicting precipitation in Türkiye using machine learning (ML) and deep learning (DL) models is an active area of research. Several studies have investigated the application of ML and DL models for precipitation prediction in various regions, including Türkiye. [Bilgili and Sahin (2009)](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib4) successfully utilized artificial neural networks (ANNs) to accurately predict long-term monthly temperatures and rainfall. The outcomes demonstrated errors that fell within acceptable limits. [Saplioglu et al. (2010)](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib24) developed a novel ANN prediction model to estimate daily rainfall in the meteorological stations of Burdur, Eğirdir, and Isparta in Türkiye. The study focused on improving precipitation prediction for effective water resource utilization and planning water structures. The authors compared their model with weighted average and harmonic average methods, revealing the ANN’s superior performance. [Taylan and Küçükyaman (2011)](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib29) also developed an effective precipitation prediction method using ANN. Their study focused on predicting precipitation in Eğirdir, located in the Lake’s Districts of Türkiye, using data from Isparta and Senirkent precipitation stations. The ANN models, with varying numbers of input variables, demonstrated improved accuracy in predicting monthly precipitation compared to historical records. The models successfully estimated missing or unmeasured data and performed well in predicting both low and high precipitation levels, highlighting the utility of the ANN approach for integrated precipitation prediction. [Aydin and Çeli̇k (2013)](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib3) proposed a method using satellite images and artificial intelligence (AI) to detect hazardous weather conditions in Türkiye early. They focused on predicting rainfall-related meteorological phenomena to minimize damages. Utilizing infrared satellite images, the study employed wavelet transform, principal component analysis, and classification algorithms, including ANNs and support vector machines (SVMs). The results showed an 84% prediction accuracy with ANNs and a 93% prediction accuracy with SVMs, highlighting the effectiveness of their approach. [Uzelaltinbulat et al. (2019)](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib30) compared the performance of different AI techniques for predicting monthly rainfall in Northern Cyprus, with the least squares SVM model showing slightly more reliable performance. [Apaydin and Sattari (2020)](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib2) employed a DL and geographic information system (GIS) hybrid method to model precipitation in Türkiye’s coastal zone. The study found that long short-term memory (LSTM) was the most successful method. The results also demonstrated that spatio-temporal data, coupled with the DL and GIS hybrid method, can effectively estimate precipitation and generate distribution maps in areas without direct measurements. [Coban et al. (2021)](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib9) developed a precipitation forecasting model for the Marmara region of Türkiye, predicting rainfall for the next 60 months (up to 2024) in its nine cities. They employed Autoregressive Moving Average, Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average (ARIMA), and Seasonal Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average models based on time series analysis, assuming past conditions persist. The ARIMA model, exhibiting the lowest estimation error, was ultimately selected as the most appropriate. [Soylu Pekpostalci et al. (2023)](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib28) conducted a review of studies from 2010 to 2022 on drought features in Türkiye, emphasizing types of droughts, quantifying indices, indicators, and data sources. Most studies focused on meteorological drought, with a rising trend in ML for short-term prediction. The review identified opportunities for further research in agricultural and hydrological drought monitoring, forecasting, and pattern detection in Türkiye.

While progress has been made in mitigating the impacts of the drought in Burdur, ongoing challenges remain. Sustainable water management, conservation, and long-term planning are essential to ensuring the province’s resilience to future droughts and climate variability. This chapter utilized recurrent models such as recurrent neural networks (RNNs), gated recurrent units (GRUs), and LSTM networks to forecast precipitation based on meteorological data recorded from 2001 to 2018 in Burdur Province, Türkiye. The aim of this chapter was to assess the effectiveness of these recurrent models in capturing the complex temporal patterns of precipitation in Burdur Province. Additionally, comparative analyses among these recurrent models were conducted to identify their strengths and weaknesses in predicting precipitation.

 

## 2.2 Methodology

 

### 2.2.1 Problem statement

In this chapter, our focus lies on utilizing DL techniques to forecast precipitation in Burdur Province. We define forecasting as the process of anticipating future values in a given time-series dataset based on its historical data points. Let **x**\=\[*x*[1] *x*[2]…*x*[*T*]\]<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="bold">x</mml:mi><mml:mo mathvariant="bold-italic">=</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mspace width="1em"/><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8230;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>T</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>\]</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si1.png) denote a sequence representing time series data, where *x*[*t*] ∈ ℝ^(*d*)<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mspace width=".2em"/><mml:mi>&#8712;</mml:mi><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mspace width=".2em"/><mml:mi>&#8477;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si2.png) and *d* represents the number of dimensions at *t*. The goal of forecasting, in the context of this specific data, is to predict the values following at time T, $\\left. \\widetilde{\\mathbf{x}} = \\left\\lbrack {x\_{T}\\quad x\_{T + 1}\\ldots x\_{T + k}} \\right. \\right\\rbrack$<mml:math><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover><mml:mo mathvariant="bold-italic">=</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>T</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mspace width="1em"/><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8230;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>\]</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si3.png). Here *k*<mml:math><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si4.png) is the number of future values to be predicted. This chapter employs simple RNN, LSTM, and GRU models to forecast the precipitation in Burdur Province. Besides, we evaluate the efficacy of these models in terms of various performance metrics.

 

### 2.2.2 Recurrent neural networks

RNN models have been utilized for time series applications that demonstrate temporal dependencies ([Shahid et al., 2020](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib26)). They are one of the classes of neural networks that use previous outputs as current inputs in order to calculate the new output. Besides, RNN has hidden states and utilizes them with previous outputs. It allows processing input of any length, and the size of the model does not increase with the length of the input. On the other hand, RNN models have some drawbacks, such as computation time and accessing historical information. They face a challenge in accessing historical information from a long time ago because long-term temporal dependencies lead to vanishing gradient problems. The architecture of the traditional RNN unit is illustrated in [Fig. 2–1](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0010).

![Figure 2–1 Internal architecture of a recurrent neural network unit.](images/F000018f02-01-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 2–1](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#Bf0010) Internal architecture of a recurrent neural network unit.

The terms *h*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si5.png) and *o*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si6.png) denote the hidden state vector and output vector at the time step *t*<mml:math><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si7.png), respectively. Hidden state vectors in RNN act as memory of the model and is given as follows:

*h*[*t*]\=*a*[1](*W*[*h**h*]*h*[*t* − 1]+*W*[*h**x*]*x*[*t*]+*b*[*a*]),<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>a</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">hh</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">hx</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>a</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math> (2.1)

![Equation](images/F000018si8.png)(2.1) 

where *a*[1]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>a</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si9.png) is the activation function and *b*[*a*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>a</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si10.png) denotes the bias parameter. In [Eq. (2.1)](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#eqn1), *W*[*h**h*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">hh</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si11.png) and *W*[*h**x*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">hx</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si12.png) represent the input-to-hidden and hidden-to-hidden weight matrices, respectively. The output of the RNN network can be given by

*o*[*t*]\=*a*[2](*W*[*o**h*]*h*[*t*]+*b*[*o*]).<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>a</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">oh</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (2.2)

![Equation](images/F000018si13.png)(2.2) 

Here, *W*[*o**h*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">oh</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si14.png) is the hidden-to-output weight matrix and *b*[*o*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si15.png) represents the bias parameter.

 

### 2.2.3 Long short-term memory

Although RNN models can be easily applied to time series data, when dealing with long-term temporal dependencies, RNN networks face the challenge of vanishing gradient problems, which decreases their performance. To address this issue, LSTM was introduced, aiming to mitigate the vanishing gradient problem ([Hochreiter & Schmidhuber, 1997](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib15)). Three gates—input, output, and forget gates—control the flow of information within LSTM layers ([Gers et al., 2000](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib11)). Input and output gates, respectively, are in charge of controlling the input and output activation information. Additionally, forget gates are employed to reset the cell memory when it is no longer necessary ([Gers et al., 2000](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib11)). At a specific time instance *t*, the input time-series data is represented as *x*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si16.png). Here, *W*[*i*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si17.png), *W*[*o*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si18.png), and *W*[*f*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si19.png) denote the input, output, and forget weight parameters, respectively. Consequently, the equations for the input gate (*i*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si20.png)), output gate (*o*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si21.png)), and forget gate (*f*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si22.png)) within the LSTM layer are as follows:

*i*[*t*] = *σ*(*W*[*i*]\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*i*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="\]" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math>

![Equation](images/F000018si23.png)

*o*[*t*] = *σ*(*W*[*o*]\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*o*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="\]" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math> (2.3)

![Equation](images/F000018si24.png)(2.3) 

*f*[*t*] = *σ*(*W*[*f*]\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*f*]).<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="\]" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math>

![Equation](images/F000018si25.png)

Here, the variables *b*[*i*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si26.png), *b*[*o*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si27.png), and *b*[*f*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si28.png) denote the bias parameters. The vectors *h*[*f*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si29.png) and *h*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si30.png) represent the hidden state at time *t*<mml:math><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si31.png). On the other hand, *σ*(.)<mml:math><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mo>.</mml:mo><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si32.png) given in [Eq. (2.3)](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#eqn3) represents the sigmoid activation function. Within the LSTM layer, *h*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si33.png) and *c*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si34.png) are given as follows:

*c*[*t*] = *i*[*t*] ⊙ *c̃*[*t*] + *f*[*t*] ⊙ *c*[*t* − 1]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math> (2.4)

![Equation](images/F000018si35.png)(2.4) 

*h*[*t*] = *o*[*t*] ⊙ tan *h*(*c*[*t*]).<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:mi>tan</mml:mi><mml:mi>h</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (2.5)

![Equation](images/F000018si36.png)(2.5) 

Here, ⊙<mml:math><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si37.png) represents the Hadamard product and the function tan *h*(.)<mml:math><mml:mi>tan</mml:mi><mml:mi>h</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si38.png) Corresponds to the hyperbolic tangent function. The expression *c̃*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si39.png) can be given as

*c̃*[*t*] = tan *h*(*W*[*c*]\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*c*]),<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>tan</mml:mi><mml:mi>h</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="\]" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math> (2.6)

![Equation](images/F000018si40.png)(2.6) 

where *W*[*c*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si41.png) is the weight parameter and *b*[*c*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si42.png) is the bias variable. The architecture of the LSTM unit is shown in [Fig. 2–2](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0015).

![Figure 2–2 Internal architecture of a long short-term memory unit.](images/F000018f02-02-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 2–2](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#Bf0015) Internal architecture of a long short-term memory unit.

 

### 2.2.4 Gated recurrent units

GRU was introduced as an alternative architecture of LSTM to simply reduce the number of parameters ([Cho et al., 2014](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib6); [Chung et al., 2014](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib7); [Shahid et al., 2020](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib26); [Zeroual et al., 2020](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib35)). Unlike the LSTM model, which consists of three gates, the GRU has two gates named update and reset gates. The update gate in GRU performs a function similar to the combination of input and forget gates in LSTM, controlling the memory transferred to the new state. On the other hand, the reset gate in GRU serves a purpose akin to the forget gate in LSTM, responsible for discarding past information that is no longer relevant. The architectural diagram of the GRU unit is depicted in [Fig. 2–3](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0020). The update (*u*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>u</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si43.png)) and reset gates (*r*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si44.png)) in GRU are given as follows:

*u*[*t*] = *σ*(*W*[*u*]\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*u*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>u</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>u</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="\]" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>u</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math> (2.7)

![Equation](images/F000018si45.png)(2.7) 

*r*[*t*] = *σ*(*W*[*r*]\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*r*]).<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="\]" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (2.8)

![Equation](images/F000018si46.png)(2.8) ![Figure 2–3 Internal architecture of a gated recurrent unit.](images/F000018f02-03-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 2–3](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#Bf0020) Internal architecture of a gated recurrent unit.

In [Eqs. (2.7)](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#eqn7) and [(2.8)](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#eqn8), *W*[*u*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>u</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si47.png) and *W*[*r*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si48.png) represent the update and reset weight parameters, respectively. Furthermore, *b*[*u*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>u</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si49.png) and *b*[*r*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si50.png) denote the bias variables. The vector *h*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si51.png) defined as the hidden state vector and it can be written at time step *t* as follows:

*h*[*t*] = *u*[*t*] ⊙ *h̃*[*t*] + (1−*u*[*t*]) ⊙ *c*[*t* − 1],<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>u</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>u</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math> (2.9)

![Equation](images/F000018si52.png)(2.9) 

where *h̃*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si53.png) is given as

*h̃*[*t*] = tanh (*W*[*h*]\[*r*[*t*]⊙*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*h*]).<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>tanh</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>\[</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>\]</mml:mo><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (2.10)

![Equation](images/F000018si54.png)(2.10) 

Here, *W*[*h*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si55.png) and *b*[*h*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si56.png) are the weight parameter and bias variable, respectively.

 

## 2.3 Experiment

 

### 2.3.1 Study area and data

Burdur is a city located in southwestern Türkiye, as shown in [Fig. 2–4](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0025), with a population of approximately 280,000. The city is positioned within the transition zone of the Mediterranean and Central Anatolian regions. Covering an area of around 7175 square kilometers, Burdur is characterized by diverse topography, including plains, lakes, and mountainous areas. Burdur has a warm and temperate climate with an average annual temperature of 12.2°C ([Climate Data, 2023](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib8)). The city has a continental climate with cold, wet, and snowy winters and hot, long, and dry summers. The average temperature in Burdur during January, the coldest month, ranges between 6.9°C and −1.1°C. August is a tropical summer month, with an average high of 32.2°C and an average low of 16.8°C ([Weather Atlas, 2023](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib32)). Burdur receives an average of 651 mm of precipitation annually. The wettest month is January, with an average of 88 mm of precipitation, while the driest month is August, with an average of 9 mm of precipitation ([Weather & Climate, 2023](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib31)). The distribution of rainfall is somewhat uneven, with a notable portion occurring during the winter months.

![Figure 2–4 Geographic location of the study area.](images/F000018f02-04-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 2–4](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#Bf0025) Geographic location of the study area.

The dataset utilized in this chapter was sourced from the Climatic Research Unit ([https://crudata.uea.ac.uk/cru/data/drought/](https://crudata.uea.ac.uk/cru/data/drought/)). This dataset encompasses precipitation data for Burdur Province, spanning the period from January 1, 2001, to December 31, 2018. An overview of the dataset, focusing on the average monthly rainfall (in millimeters) from 2001 to 2018, is presented in [Table 2–1](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#t0010). On the other hand, [Table 2–2](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#t0015) provides an in-depth analysis of precipitation statistics for each individual year within the aforementioned timeframe. The tables include key metrics such as minimum, maximum, mean, median, standard deviation, skewness, and kurtosis values, offering a comprehensive insight into the distribution and characteristics of the precipitation data.

[Fig. 2–5](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0030) depicts the graphical distributions of rainfall amounts in Burdur for each month spanning the years 2001–18. These visualizations offer insights into the dynamic nature of rainfall patterns over time, shedding light on months that have experienced notable increases or decreases in precipitation. A noteworthy trend highlighted in the figures is the diminishing rainfall in June across the years, indicative of a consistent decline in precipitation during this particular month. Additionally, the graphical representation underscores a gradual reduction in rainfall during July, pointing toward a trend of progressively drier summer months in the region. The depicted monthly variations in rainfall amounts underscore the significance of conducting thorough monthly rainfall predictions for the Burdur Province.

![Figure 2–5 Average rainfall (mm) amount for months from 2001 to 2018.](images/F000018f02-05-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 2–5](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#Bf0030) Average rainfall (mm) amount for months from 2001 to 2018.

Based on the data presented in [Fig. 2–6](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0035), it is evident that the monthly average rainfall in Burdur province exhibits a notable pattern. Specifically, the months of July and August consistently record the least amount of rainfall, implying a potential drought during the summer period. This observation suggests that the region faces a dry spell during these months over the years spanning from 2001 to 2018. Contrastingly, the figures indicate a higher precipitation rate during the spring months compared to the autumn months throughout the same time frame.

![Figure 2–6 Monthly average rainfall (mm) from 2001 to 2018.](images/F000018f02-06-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 2–6](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#Bf0035) Monthly average rainfall (mm) from 2001 to 2018.

Examining [Fig. 2–7](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0040), which illustrates the ratio of rainy days to nonrainy days from 2001 to 2018, it is notable that 2018 stands out as the year with the highest number of rainy days. In contrast, [Fig. 2–8](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0045), which provides insight into the average rainfall amount from 2001 to 2018, indicates that there is no significant trend of decrease or increase in the average rainfall over the mentioned years. This observation suggests a relative stability in the overall amount of precipitation, implying that, on average, the region has not experienced a substantial shift in rainfall patterns during the specified time frame.

![Figure 2–7 Ratio of rainy days to nonrainy days from 2001 to 2018.](images/F000018f02-07-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 2–7](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#Bf0040) Ratio of rainy days to nonrainy days from 2001 to 2018.

![Figure 2–8 Average rainfall from 2001 to 2018.](images/F000018f02-08-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 2–8](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#Bf0045) Average rainfall from 2001 to 2018.

In [Fig. 2–9A](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0050), the monthly average rainfall data for Burdur Province spanning the years 2001–18 is visually presented. However, it appears challenging to discern any clear trend or seasonal effects directly from the raw monthly average rainfall data. To address this limitation and gain a better understanding of the potential seasonal patterns, a seasonal decomposition analysis has been conducted and is showcased in [Fig. 2–9B](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0050). The results of the seasonal decomposition, as depicted in [Fig. 2–9B](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0050), offer a clearer perspective on the data.

![Figure 2–9 (A) Monthly average rainfall amount. (B) Seasonal decomposition of monthly average rainfall amount.](images/F000018f02-09-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 2–9 (A) Monthly average rainfall amount. (B) Seasonal decomposition of monthly average rainfall amount.

 

### 2.3.2 Performance metrics

To assess the efficacy of simple RNN, LSTM, and GRU models, various performance metrics, including mean absolute error (MAE), mean square error (MSE), mean squared log error (MSLE), and median absolute error (MEAE), are employed. Let **y**<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si57.png) be a vector representing the future values of the sequence **x**<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si58.png), defined as $\\left. \\mathbf{y} = \\widetilde{\\mathbf{x}} = \\left\\lbrack {x\_{T}\\quad x\_{T + 1}\\ldots x\_{T + k}} \\right. \\right\\rbrack$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>T</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mspace width="1em"/><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8230;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>\]</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si59.png). Here, *y*[*i*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si60.png) and *ỹ*[*i*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000018si61.png) denote actual and predicted values, respectively. The specified performance metrics are defined as follows:

${MAE} = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 0}^{n - 1}\\left| {y\_{i} - \\widetilde{y}}\_{i} \\right|$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">MAE</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="|" open="|"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math> (2.11)

![Equation](images/F000018si62.png)(2.11) 

${MSE} = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 0}^{n - 1}\\left( {y\_{i} - \\widetilde{y}}\_{i} \\right)^{2}$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">MSE</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:math> (2.12)

![Equation](images/F000018si63.png)(2.12) 

${MSLE} = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 0}^{n - 1}\\left( \\left. {\\log\\left( {1 + {\\widetilde{y}}\_{i}} \\right) - \\log\\left( 1 + y \\right.}\_{i} \\right) \\right)^{2}$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">MSLE</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>log</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>log</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:math> (2.13)

![Equation](images/F000018si64.png)(2.13) 

*M**E**A**E* = *m**e**d**i**a**n*(|*y*[*i*] − *ỹ*[*i*]|).<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">MEAE</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">median</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="|" open="|"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (2.14)

![Equation](images/F000018si65.png)(2.14) 

Here, log(.) and median(.) represent the natural logarithm and median operator, respectively.

 

## 2.4 Results

The simulations were conducted on a PC featuring an i7-9750H CPU, 16 GB of RAM, and a processor speed of 2.6 GHz. Additionally, for accelerated model training, we leveraged the capabilities of an NVIDIA GTX 1660 Ti GPU equipped with 6 GB of dedicated memory.

In forecasting the monthly average rainfall spanning from 2001 to 2018, the dataset was partitioned as follows: 80% of the complete time series data was allocated for training, 10% for validation, and the remaining 20% for testing. The test segment of the time series data was never exposed to the model during its training phase. Following the partitioning, the data underwent normalization, scaling it to a range between 0 and 1, to facilitate effective model training and evaluation.

In our experiments, model hyperparameters were established through a combination of simple search techniques. The RNN and GRU networks were configured with two layers, each encompassing 128 hidden units, supplemented by a dense layer. The LSTM model, on the other hand, featured two layers with 256 and 128 hidden units, respectively. The optimization process to minimize the loss function employed the Adam optimizer ([Kingma & Ba, 2014](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#bib16)), with a selected learning rate of *l*[*r*]\=0.001. Despite setting the epoch number to 100, we implemented an early stopping method to mitigate the risk of model overfitting.

Examining the daily rainfall data, it becomes apparent that the signal is characterized by a high level of noise. Therefore we determined that for more effective and suitable predictions, utilizing moving averages (MA) of the time series data over longer intervals would be beneficial. To this end, we conducted experiments and predictions employing three distinct approaches: the 3-month MA, the 6-month MA, and the 12-month MA.

[Figs. 2–10](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0055) to [2–12](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#f0065) depict the simulation results of rainfall amounts obtained through the utilization of 3-month MA, 6-month MA, and 12-month MA, respectively. The training, validation, and test splits can be seen in the figures. Notably, the most favorable outcomes are observed when employing the 12-month MA, attributed to a significant reduction in noise compared to the 3-month MA and 6-month MA.

![Figure 2–10 Monthly average rainfall forecasting using 3-month MA.](images/F000018f02-10-9780443219054.jpg)

![Figure 2–11 Monthly average rainfall forecasting using 6-month MA.](images/F000018f02-11-9780443219054.jpg)

![Figure 2–12 Monthly average rainfall forecasting using 12-month MA.](images/F000018f02-12-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 2–12 Monthly average rainfall forecasting using 12-month MA.

[Table 2–3](#Ch002_15-30_B9780443219054000018.xhtml#t0020) provides a comprehensive overview of model performances, measured in terms of MAE, MSE, MSLE, and MEAE metrics. The metrics showing the most successful model performance values for the 3-, 6- and 12-month MA are marked in bold. While the LSTM model exhibits commendable performance with the 6-month MA, the GRU model surpasses other models in the 3- and 12-month MA scenarios. Conversely, the performance of the simple RNN lags behind when compared to the other models.

## 2.5 Conclusion

This chapter focused on predicting precipitation levels in Burdur Province. The daily and monthly precipitation data exhibit significant noise, prompting our approach to utilize 3-, 6-, and 12-month MA for predictions. GRU, LSTM, and traditional RNN models were deployed for this purpose. The predictive performances of these models were assessed using metrics such as MAE, MSE, MSLE, and MEAE. Upon comparing the models, the GRU model emerges as the superior performer, particularly for the 3- and 12-month MA of precipitation, outpacing other models across these metrics. On the other hand, for 6-month MA, GRU excels in terms of MSLE, while the LSTM model demonstrates superior performance in terms of MAE, MSE, and MEAE metrics when compared to other models.

Due to the high frequency of nonrainy days throughout the year, the dataset contains a significant number of zero values. Moreover, the precipitation amount does not demonstrate a consistent upward or downward trend over the year; instead, it adheres to a distinct seasonal pattern. Therefore predicting on a daily basis proved to be less successful, and forecasting on a seasonal basis yielded more accurate results.

# Sustainable development goal-13: a case of zero carbon footprint

Prisilla Jayanthi Gandam,    CMR Institute of Technology, Hyderabad, Telangana, India

 

## Abstract

Sustainable development goals are initiated in all countries to promote a healthy atmosphere and reduce carbon dioxide emissions worldwide. The aim is not just for any individual state or person; rather, it is a national concern. The zero carbon footprint involves cooperation from various sectors that include industries, hospitals, residential buildings, and transportation. In this study, the emphasis is on the carbon emissions impacting climate change, which in turn distracts the whole ecosystem. However, the overall SDG index score of India is 54, and CO in Anand Vihar reached 120 ppm in February–March 2020. The geospatial techniques of were implemented for climate change adaptation and mitigation. The key is to save energy and reduce carbon emissions by using clean and green electricity.

 

### Keywords

Carbon; climate change; ecosystem; electromagnetic field; radiation; renewable energy; solar power

 

## 3.1 Introduction

The Sustainable Development Goal framework is to provide social, environmental, and economic progress. In the SDG, Goal-13 deals with climate action that relates to carbon emissions. It requires waste elimination in production and an increase in yield savings.

This pandemic year affected many lives and livelihoods, leading to the climate crisis and impacting global economic loss and social life. It was an unknown and unexpected pandemic that affected human lives. Though the decline of CO[2] emissions significantly fell by 6.4% in the transportation sector, a huge increase was found in the healthcare industry with all the waste disposals. The emergency decision to reduce carbon emissions by half by 2030 and reach net-zero carbon by the year 2050 with 1.5°C was stated globally. A remarkable statement at the Paris Agreement and the adoption of the SDG go hand-in-hand.

Science clearly states that if one sector fails to meet these goals, society’s economy is disrupted. SDGs and targets are integrated and applied universally, considering different national realities, capacities, and development levels and saluting national policies and priorities. The global level of ambition is that the energy sector achieves the goal. The production and use of energy are the major sources of global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. In the atmosphere, CO[2] levels and other GHG made new records in 2019; the year happens to be the second warmest year. Coal is the leading emitter of GHG, and coal is the enemy of the human race, polluting the environment globally.

In the year 2020, with the COVID-19 pandemic, a major drop of 6% in GHG emissions was found due to travel bans and economic stoppages. Transport emissions account for around 60% of oil demand, and the drop in oil demand contributed the largest share to the decline in 2020 emissions ([IEA, 2021](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib24) [https://www.iea.org/data-and-statistics/charts/change-in-co2-emissions-by-fuel-1990-2020](https://www.iea.org/data-and-statistics/charts/change-in-co2-emissions-by-fuel-1990-2020)). A large portion of the energy use in the transport zone is fueled by oil, which releases toxic air pollutants and GHGs ([Guttikunda & Jawahar, 2012](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib6)). One of the largest polluting industries is healthcare, responsible for nearly 5% of the total global GHG ([Sherman, 2021](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib15); [Jayanthi & Muralikrishna, 2023](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib8)). The use of electric vehicles will eliminate CO[2] emissions by 2030 and establish automated smoke detectors/sensors for vehicles on smart roads connecting major cities. The COVID-19 did not pause climate change (CC), but when the pandemic disappears, emissions are expected to return to extreme levels ([https://www.un.org/sustainabledevelopment/climate-change/](https://www.un.org/sustainabledevelopment/climate-change/)).

Yet another reason for India’s pollution problem is unhealthy levels of ambient PM[2.5], and setting a goal to achieve a new zero-carbon target is a challenge. The assurance of ensuring minimal emissions will require transforming the significant sectors of the economy—energy, transport, agriculture, industry, and buildings. This transformation aims to produce less waste, use resources more efficiently, and promote sustainable production and consumption. This revolution is a challenge for the pollution problems of persistent air and water. In the last year, emissions grew throughout the entire world.

 

## 3.2 Discussion and results

The state-of-the-art climate model signifies a gradual increase in torrential rain events with an upcoming warmer world. This event clearly proves how societies are not resilient to current weather extremes. One needs to reduce GHG emissions as early as possible, thereby creating and refining emergency alerts among the people and management systems and making the infrastructure “climate resilient”—to lessen the casualties and costs and make them withstand these disasters of extreme sudden flooding ([Nijhuis, 2021](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib11)). Global warming is the major cause of indefinite rainfalls.

 

### 3.2.1 Sea level increase

In a discussion, a scientist from the IPCC stated that the global sea level will rise from 0.3 to 1 m by the year 2100. Simultaneously, the sea level is rising around 2.3 m with every degree the planet is warmed. The question of how long it takes for the sea level to rise with the increase in temperature still remains unknown to researchers ([Willis et al., 2018](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib18)). According to the survey, the largest temperature rise was in the years 1910–45 and later in 1975. However, if no preventive measures were considered to reduce CO[2] emissions and other GHGs. By 2100, temperatures would rise by 5°C and predicted sea levels would rise by 48 cm because of globalized human wealth ([Enzler, 2021](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib3)).

 

### 3.2.2 Radiation from cell phones or towers

Researchers from Kolkata’s Jadavpur University have experimented by exposing nearly 50 mirch plants to mobile radiation from the seed stage. Professor Bhaskar stated that in the comparison with nonexposure to radiation (normal plants) with these plants, the exposure leaves were found to be wrinkled and looked dull. It proved that plants are affected by mobile radiation ([Cell phone radiation affects plants, vegetables: Research, 2016](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib19)). In another study, researchers from the Salim Ali Centre for Ornithology and Natural History, Coimbatore, and studies in Spain and Belgium claim that bird mortality is caused by electromagnetic radiation (EMR). According to Punjab University, EMR damages bird eggs and embryos ([Ram, 2008](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib12)). The study reveals that the emission of radiation from cell phone towers and mobile phone handsets is causing side effects on honey bees ([Santhosh, 2018](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib14)). High-level damage to trees is observed within the neighborhood of mobile towers. The demonstration using the statistical analysis of EMR from mobile phone towers was harmful to trees. The results were reliable with the trees damaged by mobile phone towers, which started on one side gradually and extended to the whole tree over time ([Waldmann-Selsam, 2016](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib17)). The researcher suggests that there is an increase in indoor concentrations of air pollutants due to the use of certain types of chemicals in home products, inadequate ventilation, and electronic smog radiation. This concludes that radiation gradually decreases the number of trees and birds. As the trees are affected, the surrounding area is more susceptible to carbon dioxide pollutants and other pollutants released the transport. In this way, indirectly, EMF radiation causes global warming in society.

 

### 3.2.3 Emission from information communication and technology industries

ICT industries revolutionized the economic growth of the state/country with the various industries they developed. The researchers and analysts estimated that at most 50 billion devices will be added to ICT by 2020; statistical outcomes prove more than 100 billion upcoming five years down the line. The data centers, the largest energy consumers on the planet, have a large share of GHG emissions, beating the energy consumption levels of many countries ([Reports on the Increasing Energy Consumption of Wireless Systems and Digital Ecosystem, 2021](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib29)). A tremendous amount of energy is consumed in developing any AI system. Moreover, ChatGPT emits CO[2] (8.4 tons) per year; by promoting sustainable practices in AI R&D ([Sophie, 2023](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib16)), one can reduce the emissions.

From McMaster University (Canada), the researchers stated that GHG from ICT, which includes computers, phones, and data centers, would increase global emissions from 1% (2007) to over 14% (2040). The existing carbon footprint of the global transportation sector was reduced by half ([Garg et al., 2011](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib5)). The overall contribution of CO[2] emissions from the ICT industries has a huge impact on CC. In an EU report, researchers estimated that to keep the global temperature rise below 2°C, a decrease in carbon emissions of around 15%–30% is essential by the year 2020 ([Patel, 2018](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib9)). However, due to the pandemic, the carbon emissions from PCs did not reduce but increased. Most of the work and shopping (essentials and groceries) were carried out online during the lockdown. *1 kWh of electricity is required to download 300HD movies in 4G; however, with 5G, one kWh can download 5K UHD movies (*[*Cho, 2020*](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib2)*)*.

 

### 3.2.4 Artificial neural network—calculation for emission rate

The ANN model ([Fig. 3–1](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#f0010)) is implemented for the computation of [Greenhouse gas emissions calculations (n.d.)](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib22) ([https://www.pca.state.mn.us/business-with-us/](https://www.pca.state.mn.us/business-with-us/)), to produce more accurate results for analyzing carbon net zero. This model trains the different pollutants that are taken as inputs to predict the carbon concentration. All these parameters are multiplied by the weights and passed into the various hidden layers to produce the results. This prediction will help to minimize the number of pollutants in any particular location or region. The calculation of the emission rate is shown in [Fig. 3–1](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#f0010). This study involves air quality index data from the city of Anand Vihar, Delhi, from February 26 to March 27, 2020. [Fig. 3–2](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#f0015) depicts the amount of CO and other pollutants, mainly PM[2.5], PM[10], and NO[2] emissions in Delhi. [Fig. 3–3](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#f0020) shows the levels of the Air Quality Index from February 26 to March 27, 2020.

However, after lots of research on the emission of CO[2] from the ICT industries was carried out, the proposal of a “Carbon Efficient Green Policy” was suggested for cloud data centers with more energy efficiency and a low carbon footprint ([Garg et al., 2011](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib5)). The hydrogen produced from the electrolysis of water with zero-carbon electricity is referred to as *green hydrogen*. It has significant potential to assist countries in transiting their economies to meet climate goals ([Fan et al., 2021](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib4)). It was observed that before IR, CO[2] concentration remained around 280 ppm (volume). However, in March 2020, the global monthly average concentration rose to 413.67 ppm, that is, 3 ppm higher than the year 2019 ([Greenhouse Gases, 2020](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib23)). Investing in technology named “carbon capture and storage,” captures emissions from electricity generation and industries that produce fossil fuel gases, thereby processing and preventing CO[2] from entering the atmosphere. Global CO[2] emissions are projected to decline to 2010 (45%) by 2030 and reach net zero by around 2050, thus avoiding a temperature rise beyond 1.5°C ([Fan et al., 2021](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib4)). Overall, the SDG offers good insights into climate-resilient development pathways toward a 1.5°C warmer world.

 

#### 3.2.6.1 Hydropower

Global warming is rising and impacting CC, with sudden heavy rains triggering floods, glaciers melting, and the occurrence of wildfires. It is recommended to use zero-emission technology for generating electric energy. Hydropower is a clean, affordable, and most common renewable source. It is low-cost electricity and durable. It reduces the emissions of GHG and energy bills across society. Hydropower relies on a constant recharging system in the water cycle to produce electricity, using water as fuel that is not reduced during the process.

The water flows through pipes over the blades of turbines, moves the blades, and then turns the generator to produce electricity called hydroelectricity. The principle behind the hydropower plant is that it converts the potential energy (due to water at an elevated outlet) and kinetic energy (due to fast-flowing water) into mechanical energy with the turbine blades' movement. The stored water falls through the height with high pressure and strikes the turbine blades, and the turbine runner starts rotating. The runner is connected to the central shaft, which in turn is connected to the generator, which generates electricity, i.e., the turbine’s mechanical energy is converted into electric energy. This electrical energy can be supplied for domestic or industrial use through the transmission lines with a voltage regulator by the transformer.

Hydropower is safer for the environment than other major sources of electrical power that use fossil fuels. Moreover, hydropower plants do not emit the waste gases or heat that are common with fossil-fuel facilities, which are major contributors to air pollution and global warming. Normally, generators emit smoke during their runs and create horrible noise pollution.

 

### 3.2.7 Climate change with extreme rising temperature globally

39 researchers’ team study, including the scientists, states that a large amount of rainfall pattern varies from year to year, influencing the CC. Extreme rainfall occurred across a larger area of Western Europe and affected the global temperature ([Climate change made floods in Western Europe more likely, 2021](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib20)). CC caused heavy rainfall events that triggered the floods, as shown in [Fig. 3–10](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#f0055). The increased global warming caused by the emission of GHGs and temperature increases is a key factor in the variation of the rainfall pattern.

Map lines delineate study areas and do not necessarily depict accepted national boundaries.

In another case study, Northwestern USA and Western Canada experienced an exceptional and dangerous heatwave ([Fig. 3–11](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#f0060)). This unpredictable event experienced landslides caused by glacier melting in mountains, the frequent occurrence of forest fires, and bad air quality with pollutants ([June ends with exceptional heat, 2021](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib25)).

The indisputable evidence from scientists suggests that the planet’s health is declining as Earth has been getting warmer. Glaciers are melting, driving a 21% increase in sea level rise ([Melting glaciers drove ‘21% of sea level rise’ over past two decades, 2021](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib26)). The study led by NASA states that if the global temperature rises by 2°C above preindustrial levels, people would face multiple impacts of CC. In [Fig. 3–12](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#f0065), the fire weather index predicts extreme weather conditions, including low rainfall and high winds that increase region temperature ([NASA Study Reveals Compounding Climate Risks at Two Degrees of Warming, 2023](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib27)). NASA claims July 2023 was the hottest month in New York.

Map lines delineate study areas and do not necessarily depict accepted national boundaries.

According to a climate science report, flooding is caused when heavy precipitation falls on land or rivers are unable to absorb and drain too much water. However, the IPCC noted several variables relating to sudden floods ([Flooding and Climate Change: Everything You Need to Know, 2019](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib21)). The scientists from New York, NASA’s Goddard Institute for Space Studies, reported that the warming climate trend is alarming with intensified forest fires, stronger hurricanes, wreaking droughts, and increasing sea levels ([NASA Says 2022 Fifth Warmest Year on Record, Warming Trend Continues, 22 Fifth Warmest Year on Record, Warming Trend Continues, 2023](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib28)).

 

## 3.3 Geospatial approach for climate change

In a case study of Bilaspur city ([Fig. 3–13](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#f0070)), Chhattisgarh (India), GIS techniques were implemented to identify the land dynamics over an area. The study included mapping, monitoring, and analysis of land use and land cover change. Interestingly, the AI algorithm was implemented for urban planning and management. The CC is affected globally in urban, coastal, and glacier areas. Temperature, air pollution, and precipitation were direct CC impacts. Glacier melting due to rising temperatures influences the rise in sea levels and land transformation globally. On the whole, the average global temperature increased by 2°C due to urban expansion. The result of [Fig. 3–13](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#f0070) shows the variation in the earth's surface temperature and highlights the spatial distribution of this study area between the years 1981 and 2020 ([Halder & Bandyopadhyay, 2021](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib7)). Geospatial techniques proved indispensable for making more accurate estimates and predictions, and devising more optimized CC adaptation and mitigation plans ([Boulos & Wilson, 2023](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib1)).

![Figure 3–13 Climate change scenarios of Bilaspur (A) Urban Heat Island (B) Urban thermal field variation index (Halder & Bandyopadhyay, 2021). Map lines delineate study areas and do not necessarily depict accepted national boundaries.](images/F000079f03-13-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 3–13 Climate change scenarios of Bilaspur (A) Urban Heat Island (B) Urban thermal field variation index ([Halder & Bandyopadhyay, 2021](#Ch003_33-47_B9780443219054000079.xhtml#bib7)). Map lines delineate study areas and do not necessarily depict accepted national boundaries.

With the above factors, one can see that the extreme global warming rise is causing sudden heavy rainfalls, and the melting of glaciers is increasing the sea level rise. A precautionary scientific approach should be taken to control GHG emissions. However, a solution named Carbon Lighthouse provides easy decarbonization.

 

## 3.4 Conclusion

The IPCC has a role in assessing the mechanisms of scientific, technical, and socioeconomic production worldwide to understand the risk of human-induced CC and its observation and projected impacts on humans. The economic and social standards of poorer people’s livelihoods need to be improved to reduce their vulnerability. Geospatial techniques were also seen being implemented for CC adaptation and mitigation. The use of hydropower and solar energy will reduce CO[2], and thereby, the SDG aims to reduce carbon emissions by 2030.
