# Evaluating computational time series methods for monthly rainfall forecasting in the wettest region of Türkiye: a case study of Rize province

İsmail Kırbaş^([1](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#aff1)), Osman Tayfun Bişkin^([2](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#aff2)) and Ahmet Çifci^([2](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#aff2)),    ¹Department of Computer Engineering, Faculty of Engineering and Architecture, Burdur Mehmet Akif Ersoy University, Burdur, Turkey,    ²Department of Electrical-Electronics Engineering, Faculty of Engineering and Architecture, Burdur Mehmet Akif Ersoy University, Burdur, Turkey

 

## Abstract

Accurate rainfall forecasting is vital for effective water resource management and mitigating climate change impacts. This chapter evaluated the predictive capabilities of three computational time series approaches—Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average (ARIMA), Prophet, and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks—for monthly precipitation forecasting in Rize province, Türkiye. The Rize province was chosen as the study region for analysis due to its topographical variability and propensity for flash flooding, as it is regarded as the wettest part of Türkiye. Meteorological data from 2004 to 2023 was utilized to develop the models and compare their forecasting performances. To address the nonstationary nature of rainfall time series, moving average filters with windows of 3 and 5 months were applied for data preprocessing prior to modeling.

The ARIMA, Prophet, and LSTM models were trained on filtered historical data from 2004 to 2021 and their short-term forecasts were evaluated against actual observations from 2022 to 2023. Progressive improvement in prediction accuracy was observed when smoothed input datasets with larger filtering windows were employed. The ARIMA and LSTM models exhibited strong capability in capturing temporal trends and periodic variations to generate robust monthly forecasts. Introducing longer-term moving averages into the modeling process enhanced stationarity and reduced noise, allowing the algorithms to more precisely deduce rainfall patterns and relationships with past occurrences. The study demonstrated the viability of computational time series models for precipitation forecasting in Rize and validated the value of simple preprocessing techniques in optimizing nonstationary climate data for modeling applications.

 

### Keywords

Computational analysis; rainfall forecasting; Rize; ARIMA; Prophet; LSTM

 

## 4.1 Introduction

Rainfall is an essential element of the Earth’s hydrological cycle and has a substantial impact on the environment and the sustenance of life ([Hou et al., 2014](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib17); [Ravi et al., 2022](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib27); [Rodrigo-Comino, 2021](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib29)). Temperature, humidity, air pressure, and wind patterns are just a few of the variables that affect this intricate meteorological occurrence. Accurate assessment and examination of rainfall are crucial for comprehending local climatic trends, effectively managing water resources, optimizing agricultural practices, and maintaining ecological systems ([Barua et al., 2013](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib7); [Berhane et al., 2020](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib8); [Sidle & Bogaard, 2016](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib32)). Rainfall is commonly quantified in millimeters (mm) or inches (inch) within a defined timeframe, frequently on a daily, monthly, or annual basis ([Miftahuddin et al., 2020](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib23)). Significant variability in the spatial distribution of rainfall is a result of topographical factors like mountainous terrain, proximity to seas, and prevailing atmospheric circulation patterns. This variety results in a wide range of climatic zones, spanning from dry deserts to abundant rainforests. Investigating rainfall patterns entails the utilization of statistical analysis, modeling, and forecasting methodologies. Scientists employ a range of devices, including rain gauges, weather radar, and satellite images, to observe and quantify rainfall across distinct geographical and temporal dimensions ([de Oliveira Simoyama et al., 2023](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib26); [Guzzetti et al., 2020](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib14); [Han et al., 2023](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib15)). The aforementioned data are of utmost importance for comprehending extended-term patterns, severe weather occurrences, and the consequences of climate change on rainfall patterns. Rainfall not only has a significant impact on the environment but also has severe socio-economic consequences. Sufficient rainfall is crucial for agriculture since it supplies the required moisture for the development of crops and the production of food. On the other hand, an excessive or inadequate amount of rainfall can result in the occurrence of floods, droughts, and water scarcity, which have an impact on people, economies, and ecosystems.

Türkiye’s geographical location and varied topography contribute to the country’s rich climatic diversity, which is characterized by seven distinct climatic zones. Each zone exhibits unique rainfall patterns, creating a mosaic of climatic conditions. This diversity is a result of factors such as elevation, proximity to water bodies, and regional topography. Topography and maritime influences emerge as pivotal factors shaping rainfall in Türkiye. The interaction between mountainous terrain, coastal proximity, and prevailing wind patterns contributes to the intricate precipitation patterns observed in different regions. The coastal areas may experience higher humidity and increased chances of rainfall due to maritime influences, while the inland areas may be more susceptible to arid conditions. The Eastern Black Sea Basin stands out as the wettest region in Türkiye, receiving the highest amount of rainfall ([Akçay et al., 2022](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib2); [Şimşek et al., 2023](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib38)). The proximity to the Black Sea and the orographic effect—the lifting of moist air over mountainous terrain—contribute to the abundant precipitation in this area. On the other hand, the Central and Southern parts of Türkiye often experience rainfall shortages, especially during the summer months ([Albayrak et al., 2022](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib3); [Sen et al., 2012](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib30)). These regional disparities have implications for agriculture, water resources, and overall environmental sustainability. The seasonal distribution of rainfall is another key aspect of Türkiye’s climate. While the winter months typically bring increased precipitation, especially in coastal areas influenced by Mediterranean and Black Sea climates, the summer months are often marked by drier conditions. This seasonality has direct consequences for agricultural practices, as the availability of water significantly impacts crop growth and yields. In recent years, Türkiye has observed changes in extreme rainfall events, with an increase in magnitudes from shorter to longer durations. This trend is associated with the broader impact of climate change ([Danandeh Mehr & Kahya, 2017](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib11); [Nigussie & Altunkaynak, 2019](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib25); [Yilmaz, 2015](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib36)). The warming of the atmosphere contributes to more intense convective rainfall events, potentially leading to issues such as flash floods and soil erosion. Understanding these changes in Türkiye is essential for effective water resource management and mitigating the consequences of climate change.

 

## 4.2 Literature review

Accurate rainfall measurement and forecasting are indispensable for a wide range of sectors and for addressing the challenges posed by climate variability and change. Rainfall forecasting is a challenging task due to the irregular patterns and climate variations worldwide. Different models and algorithms are employed to forecast rainfall by analyzing historical weather data, aiming to predict both the occurrence and amount of rainfall. [Shi et al. (2015)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib31) presented an innovative method for precipitation nowcasting by introducing a Convolutional Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) (ConvLSTM) network. This approach addressed the challenges of predicting future precipitation based on past radar observations. The ConvLSTM network leveraged the strengths of both convolutional neural networks for spatial feature extraction and LSTMs for capturing temporal dependencies. Through experiments on real radar data, the authors demonstrated that their proposed approach outperforms traditional methods for precipitation nowcasting. The ConvLSTM network exhibited improved accuracy and the ability to capture complex spatiotemporal patterns in the data. [Kim et al. (2017)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib19) developed the DeepRain model, employing deep learning techniques for precipitation prediction using multichannel radar data. The model, based on a ConvLSTM network, efficiently processed radar data by capturing both spatial and temporal dependencies. Through experiments on real-world radar data, the authors demonstrated that DeepRain outperforms traditional methods in precipitation prediction. [Hu et al. (2018)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib18) explored the use of artificial neural networks (ANNs) and LSTM networks for simulating the rainfall-runoff process based on flood events from 1971 to 2013 in the Fen River basin. The results of the study demonstrated that LSTM models outperform conceptual and physical-based models in rainfall-runoff simulation. [Akbari Asanjan et al. (2018)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib1) focused on utilizing Precipitation Estimation from Remotely Sensed Information using ANNs (PERSIANN) in combination with LSTM recurrent neural networks. By integrating these two approaches, the study aimed to enhance the accuracy and reliability of short-term precipitation forecasts. The study concluded that the proposed framework made accurate predictions of precipitation, particularly for convective systems characterized by complex and evolving dynamics. [Gao et al. (2020)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib12) compared LSTM, gated recurrent unit (GRU), and conventional ANN models, demonstrating that LSTM and GRU models outperform ANN when the time step is optimized. The results indicated that both LSTM and GRU models can handle larger time steps without sacrificing prediction accuracy, with GRU models standing out for their less complicated structures and efficiency in short-term runoff predictions. [Ni et al. (2020)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib24) developed two hybrid models, wavelet-LSTM (WLSTM) and convolutional LSTM (CLSTM), based on LSTM, for monthly streamflow and rainfall forecasting. The models were compared to multilayer perceptron and LSTM. The outcomes showed that WLSTM and CLSTM were better options for predicting time series. [Sulasikin et al. (2021)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib33) examined the use of three different time series models: Seasonal Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average, Facebook Prophet, and LSTM to predict monthly rainfall for flood mitigation in Central Jakarta. The findings indicated that Facebook Prophet had the lowest mean squared error (MSE) and root mean squared error (RMSE), making it the most suitable model for forecasting monthly rainfall in Central Jakarta. [Rhaiem et al. (2021)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib28) presented the development of an innovative Extract, Transform, and Load tool for handling rainfall data from the National Observatory of Tunisian Agriculture and its integration into a big data platform. They also employed Voronoi spatial analysis and Volunteered Geographic Information data from OpenStreetMap to estimate rainfall measures for new spatial units. Moreover, the study evaluated the effectiveness of Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average (ARIMA), ANN, and LSTM in time series forecasting. The results revealed that LSTM exhibited superior prediction accuracy. [Anul Haq (2022)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib6) developed and optimized a novel deep learning model called Climate Deep LSTM (CDLSTM) for forecasting temperature and rainfall values in the Himalayan states of India. Additionally, the performance of the CDLSTM model was evaluated by employing the Facebook Prophet model, thereby enhancing its forecasting capabilities. The study identified a robust correlation between temperature and rainfall changes in the Himalayan region. As a result, CDLSTM emerged as a promising approach for climate change forecasting, particularly in regions such as the Himalayas, which have significant importance and vulnerability to climate impacts. [Chen et al. (2022)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib39) introduced a novel deep learning-based LSTM model for forecasting monthly rainfall data. The model’s efficacy was assessed through a comparative analysis with a Random Forest (RF) data-driven model, utilizing 41 years of monthly rainfall data from meteorological stations in Türkiye, specifically Rize and Konya. Optimal window sizes were employed to identify the most suitable lag times for the rainfall time series. The LSTM model demonstrated superior performance in predicting rainfall compared to the RF model at both meteorological stations. [Allawi et al. (2023)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib4) introduced a methodology for detecting rainfall pattern nonlinearity by integrating optimizer algorithms with an ANN, specifically employing the artificial bee colony, particle swarm optimization, and imperialism competitive algorithm. The study conducted a real-world case study in Malaysia using 54 years of monthly rainfall data, resulting in an ANN model that is utilized for real-time rainfall forecasting. The statistical analysis showed that the imperial competitive algorithm with ANN model outperformed other models, proving its potential as a highly accurate tool for predicting monthly rainfall.

Compared to the previous studies, the primary contributions of this chapter are as follows:

*   • The study applies and compares three time series forecasting models—ARIMA, Prophet, and LSTM—to predict monthly rainfall in Rize province, Türkiye.
*   • The effect of preprocessing the input data using different-sized moving average filters is systematically analyzed. The results demonstrate how filtering can improve model performance by reducing noise and enhancing stationarity.
*   • Visualization of trends, seasonality, and residual components is facilitated through detailed decompositions of both the raw and filtered time series. This helps diagnose strengths and weaknesses in capturing different signals.
*   • Transparent validation of model fitting and forecast skill is achieved through the thorough presentation of evaluation metrics, including prediction plots and loss curves.

 

## 4.3 Overview of study area and data used

Rize is situated between approximately 40°20′ to 41°20′ North latitudes and 40°22′ to 41°28′ East longitudes. It is bordered to the north by the Black Sea, to the south by the province of Erzurum, to the west by Trabzon Province, and to the east by the province of Artvin (as shown in [Fig. 4–1](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0010)). The total geographical area of Rize is approximately 3920 km². Topographically, Rize province in northeastern Türkiye presents a contrasting landscape. In the western regions, there are lush green hills covered with tea plantations. The corrosive currents of the Black Sea have eroded the rugged topography of the vast plateaus in the eastern regions, creating canyons. As of 2023, the estimated population of Rize is approximately 344,000. There are several significant rivers and streams within the boundaries of Rize province. The Fırtına River is well recognized as the most significant river, earning the nickname “storm water” because of its turbulent rapids and strong currents. The river begins in the Kaçkar Mountains and spans a distance of 68 km before reaching the Black Sea. Running parallel to the Fırtına, the Hemşin River is shorter. It flows through the Hemşin and Pazar districts and flows into the Black Sea.

![Figure 4–1 Geographic location of the Rize province. Map lines delineate study areas and do not necessarily depict accepted national boundaries.](images/F000031f04-01-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–1](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0010) Geographic location of the Rize province. Map lines delineate study areas and do not necessarily depict accepted national boundaries.

Rize receives more rainfall than any other city in Türkiye, recording an annual rainfall exceeding 2300 mm and an average of almost 173 rainy days annually. Rize’s climate is mild and humid due to its location near the Black Sea. Year-round temperatures average around 14.5°C along the coast ([MGM, 2023](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib22)). However, temperatures get cooler in the mountainous areas further inland from Rize. The effects of the Black Sea give Rize a wet climate with frequent rainfall throughout the year. On August 26, 2010, the region experienced a significant downpour, with a total rainfall of 166.5 mm within a 24-hour period. This intense rainfall led to substantial damage, including the collapse of numerous retaining walls. The flood wreaked havoc, causing severe damage to cars and apartments in its path ([Şen & Kahya, 2017](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib37)).

The dataset used in this chapter was obtained from the Center for Hydrometeorology and Remote Sensing (CHRS) through their data portal at [https://chrsdata.eng.uci.edu/](https://chrsdata.eng.uci.edu/). This dataset specifically pertains to rainfall data for the Rize province and covers the time period from January 2004 to October 2023. [Table 4–1](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#t0010) provides an overview of the dataset, focusing on the average monthly rainfall values (in millimeters) spanning from 2004 to 2023. The table includes various key metrics such as minimum, maximum, mean, standard deviation, skewness, and kurtosis, which collectively offer a comprehensive understanding of the distribution and characteristics of the rainfall data.

Table 4–1

Monthly average rainfall spanning the years 2004–23.

 
|   || Rainfall || Moving average 3 || Moving average 5 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Count || 234 || 234 || 234 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Mean || 32.652 || 32.544 || 32.596 |
| Std Dev || 28.006 || 17.246 || 13.638 |
| Min || 0 || 1.860 || 7.666 |
| 25% || 13.430 || 20.142 || 23.222 |
| 50% || 25.935 || 30.377 || 31.141 |
| 75% || 45.463 || 42.728 || 39.425 |
| Max || 178.620 || 91.037 || 78.466 |
| Kurtosis || 3.730 || 0.686 || 0.619 |
| Skewness || 1.582 || 0.833 || 0.762 |

[Fig. 4–2](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0015) shows the monthly rainfall raw data and data filtered with rolling windows of size 3 and 5 months. The blue line represents the raw data. It can be seen that the raw data exhibits high volatility, with sharp fluctuations between low and high rainfall values month-to-month. This is indicative of the noisy nature of the raw data, which contains sudden anomalies and outliers. The orange line depicts the data after applying a 3-month moving average filter. Smoothing the data in this way reduces some of the variability by averaging neighboring observations. This filtered curve appears less jagged compared to the raw data plot. Finally, the gray line shows the monthly rainfall series processed with a 5-month moving average window. Visually, this curve exhibits an even higher degree of smoothing relative to the 3-month filtered data. The fluctuations are dampened further. Notably, all three plots maintain the overall increasing rainfall trend over the 20-year period from 2004 to 2023. This suggests the trend is robust to different degrees of smoothing.

![Figure 4–2 Monthly rainfall raw and rolling window filtered for Rize.](images/F000031f04-02-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–2](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0015) Monthly rainfall raw and rolling window filtered for Rize.

[Fig. 4–3](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0020) presents the decomposed components of the monthly rainfall raw data, including the observed values, trend, seasonal, and residual components. The trend line shows an overall increasing pattern in rainfall over time. Clear seasonal variations are evident in the seasonal component, with higher rainfall observed during certain months of the year. The residual graph indicates remaining unexplained variations after removing the trend and seasonal effects.

![Figure 4–3 Decomposed graphics for monthly rainfall data.](images/F000031f04-03-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–3](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0020) Decomposed graphics for monthly rainfall data.

[Fig. 4–4](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0025) depicts the decomposed components of the monthly rainfall data filtered with a 3-month moving average window. Similar to the previous figure, the trend indicates an increase in rainfall, while seasonal fluctuations are observed in the seasonal graph. However, the residual variations are smaller compared to the raw data decomposition, suggesting the filter helped reduce noise.

![Figure 4–4 Decomposed plots for rainfall data filtered with a moving average (window size 3).](images/F000031f04-04-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–4](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0025) Decomposed plots for rainfall data filtered with a moving average (window size 3).

[Fig. 4–5](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0030) displays the signal decomposition for the monthly rainfall preprocessed with a 5-month moving average filter. The trend line is smoother compared to the previous decompositions. The seasonal component exhibits clearer periodic patterns. Furthermore, the residual graph has the fewest fluctuations, highlighting the effectiveness of the longer 5-month window in noise reduction.

![Figure 4–5 Decomposed plots for rainfall data filtered with a moving average (window size 5).](images/F000031f04-05-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–5](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0030) Decomposed plots for rainfall data filtered with a moving average (window size 5).

[Fig. 4–6](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0035) displays the histogram plots of the raw monthly rainfall data as well as the data filtered using 3- and 5-month moving average windows from 2004 to 2023 in Rize. The histogram of the raw data (first panel) exhibits a right-skewed distribution, with a long tail toward higher rainfall values. This positive skew indicates that extreme high rainfall amounts occur more frequently than extreme low values. Additionally, the raw data histogram has a wider spread, reflecting the larger variations present in the noisy input series. After applying a 3-month moving average filter, the midpoint histogram (second panel) shows a distribution that is still right-skewed yet narrowed considerably compared to the raw plot. This suggests that 3-month averaging cuts down on some outliers and anomalies present in the original series. The third panel depicts the histogram of data smoothed with a 5-month window. It has an even tighter concentration around the mean relative to the other two distributions. The longer filter window of 5 months produces a rainfall series close to a normal distribution with minimal skew.

![Figure 4–6 Histogram plots for raw and filtered monthly rainfall data.](images/F000031f04-06-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–6](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0035) Histogram plots for raw and filtered monthly rainfall data.

 

## 4.4 Methodology

 

### 4.4.1 Autoregressive integrated moving average

Box and Jenkins proposed the use of ARIMA as a model to represent stochastic signals ([Box & Jenkins, 1970](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib10)). It is a generalized version of Autoregressive Moving Average (ARMA) models. ARMA models are generally applied to wide-sense stationary stochastic signals. However, ARIMA models are applied if the signals or data are nonstationary in the sense of mean. Therefore it is widely used in forecasting problems in cases where data or signals have nonstationary properties. When denoting an ARMA model, it is represented as ARMA(*p*,*q*), where *p* is the order of the autoregressive (AR) part and *q* is the order of the moving average (MA) part. Similarly, an ARIMA model is denoted as ARIMA(*p*,*d*,*q*), where *p* and *q* have the same meaning as in an ARMA model. However, in an ARIMA model, there is an additional parameter *d*, which refers to the number of times the raw data have been differenced to make it stationary.

Let *x*[*t*] represents the value, or observation, of time series data at time *t*. The representation of this signal using an ARMA(*p*,*q*) model can be expressed as follows:

*x*[*t*] = *θ*[1]*x*[*t* − 1] + *θ*[2]*x*[*t* − 2] + … + *θ*[*p*]*x*[*t* − *p*] + *Φ*[0]*e*[*t* − 0] + *Φ*[1]*e*[*t* − 1] + … + *Φ*[*q*]*e*[*t* − *q*],<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mo>&#8230;</mml:mo><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#934;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>e</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#934;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>e</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mo>&#8230;</mml:mo><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#934;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>q</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>e</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>q</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math> (4.1)

![Equation](images/F000031si1.png)(4.1) 

where *θ* and *Φ* are AR and MA coefficients, respectively. The term *e*[*t*] denotes the error term in a stochastic signal. ARIMA models are generalized version of the ARMA models and representation of signal using an ARIMA(*p*,*d*,*q*) model is given by

*y*[*t*] = *θ*[1]*y*[*t* − 1] + *θ*[2]*y*[*t* − 2] + … + *θ*[*p*]*y*[*t* − *p*] + *Φ*[0]*e*[*t* − 0] + *Φ*[1]*e*[*t* − 1] + … + *Φ*[*q*]*e*[*t* − *q*],<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mo>&#8230;</mml:mo><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#934;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>e</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#934;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>e</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mo>&#8230;</mml:mo><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#934;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>q</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>e</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>q</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math> (4.2)

![Equation](images/F000031si2.png)(4.2) 

where *y*[*t*] is the differenced time series signal. In addition, *θ* and *Φ* represent AR and MA coefficients, respectively. The term *d* in the ARIMA model is the degree of differencing, and differenced time series are obtained according to the value of this parameter. For instance, if *d* is selected as 1, then *y*[*t*] becomes *y*[*t*] = *x*[*t*] − *x*[*t* − 1]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000031si3.png).

 

### 4.4.2 Prophet model

[Taylor and Letham (2018)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib34) proposed the Prophet model as a modular regression model with interpretable parameters. It allows the researchers to apply their knowledge about the data. Therefore the Prophet model has the advantage of using both statistical forecasting and judgement forecasting. A time series signal can be written using the Prophet model as follows:

*x*[*t*] = *g*[*t*] + *s*[*t*] + *h*[*t*] + *e*[*t*],<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>s</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>e</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math> (4.3)

![Equation](images/F000031si4.png)(4.3) 

where *g*[*t*] represents the trend term of the signal and *s*[*t*] denotes the periodic change in data. The term *h*[*t*] indicates the holiday term and represents the effects of holidays on data. This property of the Prophet model is quite useful for financial data. Finally, *e*[*t*] is the error term, which represents random features of data. In order to forecast the seasonality effects in data, Prophet utilizes the Fourier series expansion ([Wang et al., 2022](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib35)).

 

### 4.4.3 Long short-term memory

LSTM was developed with the primary goal of addressing the vanishing gradient problem, as first proposed by [Hochreiter and Schmidhuber (1997)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib16). The LSTM architecture is an advanced variant of the GRU model for processing sequential data ([Kirbas, 2019](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib20)). As described in detail by [Gers et al. (2000)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib13), an LSTM layer consists of input, output, and forget gates. The input and output gates regulate the flow of input and output data within the layer, while the forget gate is responsible for erasing cell memory ([Gers et al., 2000](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib13)). Denoting *i*[*t*], *o*[*t*], and *f*[*t*] as the input, output, and forget gates, respectively, an LSTM layer is mathematically expressed as follows:

*i*[*t*] = *σ*(*W*[*i*]\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*i*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="\]" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math>

![Equation](images/F000031si5.png)

*o*[*t*] = *σ*(*W*[*o*]\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*o*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="\]" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math> (4.4)

![Equation](images/F000031si6.png)(4.4) 

*f*[*t*] = *σ*(*W*[*f*]\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*f*]),<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="\]" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math>

![Equation](images/F000031si7.png)

where the variables *b*[*i*], *b*[*o*], and *b*[*f*] are bias parameters, while *W*[*i*], *W*[*o*], and *W*[*f*] represent the input, output, and forget weight parameters, respectively. *σ*(.) in [Eq. (4.4)](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#eqn4) is the sigmoid activation function. The vector *h*[*t*] is the hidden state at time *t* and given by

*h*[*t*] = *o*[*t*] ⊙ tan *h*(*c*[*t*]),<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>o</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:mi>tan</mml:mi><mml:mi>h</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math> (4.5)

![Equation](images/F000031si8.png)(4.5) 

where *c*[*t*] = *i*[*t*] ⊙ *c̃*[*t*] + *f*[*t*] ⊙ *c*[*t* − 1]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000031si9.png) and ![ent](images/odot_8857.png)represent the Hadamard product. The function tan *h*(.)<mml:math><mml:mi>tan</mml:mi><mml:mi>h</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000031si10.png) corresponds to the hyperbolic-tangent function. The expression *c̃*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000031si11.png) can be given as

*c̃*[*t*] = tan *h*(*W*[*c*]\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*c*]).<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>tan</mml:mi><mml:mi>h</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="\]" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (4.6)

![Equation](images/F000031si12.png)(4.6) 

Here, *W*[*c*] denotes the weight parameter, and *b*[*c*] is the bias variable.

 

### 4.4.4 Performance evaluation criteria

To evaluate the effectiveness of ARIMA, Prophet, and LSTM models for forecasting, several error metrics were calculated, including mean absolute error (MAE), MSE, RMSE, and mean absolute percentage error (MAPE). These common performance metrics were employed to compare the ARIMA, Prophet, and LSTM models. Let *y*<mml:math><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000031si13.png) be a vector representing the future values of the sequence *x*<mml:math><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000031si14.png), defined as *y* = *x̃* = \[*x*[*T*] *x*[*T* + 1]…*x*[*T* + *k*]\]<mml:math><mml:mi>y</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mo>\[</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>T</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mspace width="1em"/><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8230;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>\]</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000031si15.png). Here *y*[*i*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000031si16.png) and *ỹ*[*i*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000031si17.png) denote actual and predicted values, respectively. The error metrics are defined as follows ([Bişkin & Çifci, 2021](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib9); [Kırbaş, 2019](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#bib21)):

${MAE} = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 0}^{n - 1}\\left| {y\_{i} - \\widetilde{y}}\_{i} \\right|$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">MAE</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="|" open="|"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math> (4.7)

![Equation](images/F000031si18.png)(4.7) 

${MSE} = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 0}^{n - 1}\\left( {y\_{i} - \\widetilde{y}}\_{i} \\right)^{2}$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">MSE</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:math> (4.8)

![Equation](images/F000031si19.png)(4.8) 

${RMSE} = \\sqrt{\\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 0}^{n - 1}\\left( {y\_{i} - \\widetilde{y}}\_{i} \\right)^{2}}$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">RMSE</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msqrt><mml:mrow><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msqrt></mml:math> (4.9)

![Equation](images/F000031si20.png)(4.9) 

${MAPE} = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum\_{i = 0}^{n - 1}{\\left| \\frac{{y\_{i} - \\widetilde{y}}\_{i}}{y\_{i}} \\right| \\times 100}.$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">MAPE</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:msubsup><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="|" open="|"><mml:mrow><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mover accent="true"><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#732;</mml:mo></mml:mover></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#215;</mml:mo><mml:mn>100</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (4.10)

![Equation](images/F000031si21.png)(4.10)  

## 4.5 Results and discussion

The predictive performance of the ARIMA, Prophet, and LSTM models was evaluated on the monthly rainfall time series data for Rize province, Türkiye spanning 2004–23. The data were divided into training and testing sets, with data from January 2004 to December 2021 used for model training and the remaining data from January 2022 to October 2023 used for evaluation. Three experiments were conducted based on the original raw monthly rainfall data as well as data preprocessed using moving average filters with window sizes of 3 and 5 months.

[Fig. 4–7](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0040) depicts a plot of loss values versus the number of training epochs for the LSTM model applied to Rize’s monthly rainfall data. The loss value measures the difference between the predicted and true target outputs of the model during training. A lower loss indicates better performance, with the goal being to minimize this value as much as possible. In this figure, the blue line tracks the evolution of the loss as the LSTM model parameters are iteratively updated based on the training data at each epoch. It can be seen that initially, the loss is high, but it steadily decreases over successive training cycles, reaching a minimum value before leveling off. This behavior shows that the LSTM network is effectively learning patterns in the input time series using backpropagation and gradient descent optimization. With more training, the model improves its ability to capture the temporal relationships between past and future rainfall values. Notably, the loss reduces sharply within the first 10 epochs, after which the rate of decrease starts leveling off. This suggests that most of the learning occurs early on, with marginal benefits afterward. It provides insight into determining an ideal number of training epochs to avoid overfitting.

![Figure 4–7 Loss value plot during long short-term memory model training.](images/F000031f04-07-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–7](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0040) Loss value plot during long short-term memory model training.

[Fig. 4–8](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0045) presents the results of applying the LSTM model to forecast monthly rainfall for the period of January 2022 to October 2023 in Rize. Specifically, it shows a comparison of the predicted values against the actual observed rainfall measurements over this test period. The blue line depicts the true rainfall totals for each month, as recorded historically. Meanwhile, the green dots represent the amounts forecast by the LSTM network following its training on prior data from 2004 to 2021. Visually inspecting the plot, it can be seen that the predicted values closely trace the ups and downs of the true monthly rainfall curve throughout most of the evaluation phase. This close tracking performance indicates the model’s ability to accurately deduce the rainfall patterns over the test months based on its learnings from past data. However, some deviations also occur occasionally, such as in March and September 2022 as well as July 2023, where the real observations diverged noticeably from the model forecasts. This suggests that while the LSTM network captured the general tendencies reasonably well, it remained imperfect at anticipating certain outliers. Notably, the model appears to capture the seasonal fluctuations reliably, with the peaks and troughs in the predicted curve generally concurring with reality. This validates that the LSTM remembers the periodic monthly variations exhibited in the training rainfall series.

![Figure 4–8 Raw monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for LSTM model.](images/F000031f04-08-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 4–8 Raw monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for LSTM model.

[Figs. 4–9](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0050) and [4–10](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0055) show the results of using the LSTM model to forecast monthly rainfall in Rize, as compared to actual observations, but with rainfall data preprocessed with 3- and 5-month moving average filters, respectively. In [Fig. 4–9](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0050), the input to the LSTM was filtered using a 3-month window. When evaluating predictions over 2022–23, it can be seen that the green forecasted data trace the ups and downs of observed rainfall (red line) more precisely than with raw data ([Fig. 4–8](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0045)). This closer fit implies that 3-month averaging removed noise, allowing the LSTM to learn patterns more effectively. Similarly, [Fig. 4–10](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0055) displays evaluation results using a 5-month filtered input to the LSTM model. Here, predicted values (green) correspond near-perfectly with actual measurements (red), exhibiting almost no deviations at all time points. This superior match suggests that longer averaging reduced distortions to the point where the LSTM completely captured Rize’s underlying precipitation tendencies. Notably, while both [Figs. 4–9](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0050) and [4–10](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0055) show improved forecasts over [Fig. 4–8](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0045), predictions became progressively more accurate moving from the 3- to 5-month filtered datasets. The implementation of progressive enhancement confirms that the inclusion of additional smoothing techniques successfully reduced nonstationarities, leading to input data that aligns more closely with the LSTM’s assumption of stationarity. As a result, the predictive skill of the model improved.

![Figure 4–9 Moving average filtered (window size 3) monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for long short-term memory model.](images/F000031f04-09-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–9](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0050) Moving average filtered (window size 3) monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for long short-term memory model.

![Figure 4–10 Moving average filtered (window size 5) monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for long short-term memory model.](images/F000031f04-10-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–10](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0055) Moving average filtered (window size 5) monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for long short-term memory model.

The results of implementing the ARIMA model to predict monthly rainfall in Rize from January 2022 to October 2023 are shown in [Fig. 4–11](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0060). The predicted values are compared to the actual observations during this evaluation period. The blue line tracks the true measured rainfall totals for each month during the test timeframe. Superimposed in green are the amounts forecast by the ARIMA model after training on historical data from 2004 to 2021. Upon visual inspection, it is evident that the ARIMA predictions generally follow the ups and downs reflected in the real monthly rainfall curve. This suggests that the model was able to accurately predict changes in precipitation due to its understanding of the underlying time series pattern. Some divergences do exist, such as in February, August, and September 2022, where actual rainfall exceeded expectations. However, such discrepancies are minor relative to the overall close tracking otherwise displayed.

![Figure 4–11 Raw monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for ARIMA model.](images/F000031f04-11-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 4–11 Raw monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for ARIMA model.

The evaluation of the ARIMA model’s forecasting performance is shown in [Figs. 4–12](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0065) and [4–13](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0070). The model was evaluated using 3- and 5-month moving averaged rainfall series as inputs, respectively. The evaluation period was from 2022 to 2023. In [Fig. 4–12](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0065), the ARIMA model was trained on data preprocessed with a 3-month moving average filter. When comparing predictions to actual measurements, it is clear that the green forecasted curves more accurately reflect variations in observed rainfall (represented by the red line) than raw data (as shown in [Fig. 4–11](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0060)). This enhancement implies that the filter helped the model capture trends. [Fig. 4–13](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0070) displays evaluation outcomes employing a 5-month averaged input to ARIMA. Here, predicted values correspond nearly perfectly to real observations, with barely perceptible deviations between the green and red lines at any point. This strong match demonstrates that longer averaging induces even higher stationarity, allowing the model to learn precipitation tendencies with the utmost accuracy. Notably, as with the LSTM model, adopting smoothed inputs moving from 3- to 5-month windows produced progressively better accord between ARIMA forecasts and reality. This reinforces that additional filtering assists in further removing noise, yielding inputs optimally suited to ARIMA’s assumptions.

![Figure 4–12 Moving average filtered (window size 3) monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for autoregressive integrated moving average model.](images/F000031f04-12-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–12](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0065) Moving average filtered (window size 3) monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for autoregressive integrated moving average model.

![Figure 4–13 Moving average filtered (window size 5) monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for autoregressive integrated moving average model.](images/F000031f04-13-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–13](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0070) Moving average filtered (window size 5) monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for autoregressive integrated moving average model.

[Fig. 4–14](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0075) depicts the results of applying the Prophet model to forecast monthly rainfall in Rize from January 2022 to October 2023 and comparing the predicted values to actual observed rainfall over this evaluation period. The blue line traces the true measured monthly rainfall amounts, while the black dots represent the forecasts generated by the Prophet model after training on historical data from 2004 to 2021. On visual inspection, it can be seen that the Prophet predictions closely track the fluctuations in actual rainfall throughout most of the test timeframe. This suggests the model effectively captured the underlying trends and patterns in the time series based on its training. Some minor divergences are observed on occasion, such as in March and September 2022, but these deviations are relatively small compared to the overall accuracy of the alignment between predicted and observed values.

![Figure 4–14 Raw monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for Prophet model.](images/F000031f04-14-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 4–14 Raw monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for Prophet model.

[Fig. 4–15](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0080) presents the Prophet model’s forecasting performance using preprocessed monthly rainfall data filtered with a 3-month moving average window as input and comparing predictions over 2022–23 to actual measurements. It can be seen that the black forecasted data corresponds even more closely to fluctuations in observed rainfall (blue line) relative to when raw data was used as input to Prophet ([Fig. 4–14](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0075)). This tighter fitting implies that smoothing removed noise and variations, allowing the model to deduce trends and periodicities more precisely. The closer adherence between predicted and realized values validates that the 3-month averaging induced slightly higher stationarity, which empowered more accurate modeling of the rainfall generating process.

![Figure 4–15 Moving average filtered (window size 3) monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for Prophet model.](images/F000031f04-15-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–15](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0080) Moving average filtered (window size 3) monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for Prophet model.

Similarly, [Fig. 4–16](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0085) displays evaluation outcomes for Prophet when provided with a 5-month moving averaged rainfall series as input. Here, predicted amounts (black dots) are virtually indistinguishable from factual observations (blue line), showing near-perfect congruence at every time point. This superior match suggests the longer filtering resulted in an input series exceedingly close to stationarity, letting Prophet comprehend Rize’s rainfall tendencies. Comparing [Figs. 4–15](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0080) and [4–16](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#f0085), it is affirmed that supplementary smoothing continually benefited Prophet’s predictive skill by successively reducing distortions in the series.

![Figure 4–16 Moving average filtered (window size 5) monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for Prophet model.](images/F000031f04-16-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 4–16](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#Bf0085) Moving average filtered (window size 5) monthly rainfall training data, predictions, and actual values for Prophet model.

[Table 4–2](#Ch004_49-70_B9780443219054000031.xhtml#t0015) summarizes the MAE, MSE, RMSE, and MAPE values obtained from the models on the test sets for each experiment. In the raw data experiment, the Prophet model outperformed the ARIMA and LSTM models, with the lowest MAE of 19.225, MSE of 564.023, and RMSE of 23.749. In terms of MAPE, the ARIMA model performed best with a value of 3.635, followed closely by LSTM at 3.700, and then Prophet at 4.206. When a moving average filter with a 3-month window size was applied, the performance of all models improved substantially according to all metrics. The ARIMA model achieved the best results, with MAE, MSE, and RMSE values of 8.349, 86.139, and 9.281, respectively. The MAPE was also the lowest, at 0.353 for ARIMA. For the experiments using 5-month moving average filtered data, ARIMA again performed best with MAE, MSE, and RMSE values of 4.850, 28.473, and 5.336. The MAPE of 0.199% was also the lowest among the models.

 Numerical results of the experiment. 

 
|    || Model || MAE || MSE || RMSE || MAPE |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| LSTM || 23.778 || 653.265 || 25.559 || 3.700 |
| Prophet || 19.225 || 564.023 || 23.749 || 4.206 |

| LSTM || 13.724 || 258.638 || 16.082 || 0.732 |
| Prophet || 13.866 || 280.013 || 16.733 || 0.981 |

*ARIMA*, Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average; *LSTM*, Long Short-Term Memory; *MAE*, mean absolute error; *MAPE*, mean absolute percentage error; *MSE*, mean square error; *RMSE*, root mean squared error.

 

## 4.6 Conclusion

Rainfall forecasting is important for agriculture, disaster management, and ensuring food and water security. Farmers rely on short- and long-term precipitation projections to decide planting and harvesting times to maximize crop yields. Water management agencies depend on rainfall forecasts to regulate reservoir levels and generate hydropower. Accurate rainfall predictions are essential for issuing timely flood and drought warnings to protect lives and minimize losses. However, forecasting poses challenges due to atmospheric and terrain factors interacting nonlinearly in complex, variable spatial-temporal patterns that are difficult to model and increase uncertainty beyond 10 days.

This study explored the effectiveness of ARIMA, Prophet, and LSTM models for forecasting monthly rainfall in Rize province, Türkiye, based on 20 years of historical meteorological data. A key focus was to assess and compare the predictive performance of these three models, which have gained popularity for time series forecasting tasks. The monthly rainfall series exhibited high volatility and nonstationary properties that required preprocessing prior to modeling. Moving average filters with windows of 3 and 5 months were applied to smooth out fluctuations and induce stationarity. Signal decomposition revealed clear, increasing trends and seasonal variations that the models aimed to capture. When directly applying the ARIMA, Prophet, and LSTM techniques to the raw data, all three produced forecasts that reasonably tracked actual monthly rainfall fluctuations over the evaluation period of 2022–23. However, adopting smoothed inputs enhanced their performance notably. Training the models on 3-month filtered data yielded closer-fitting predictions. Using a 5-month averaged input resulted in forecasts that closely matched observed values, demonstrating near-perfect replication of Rize’s precipitation tendencies. This substantiates that additional filtering assisted the models by further mitigating noise and nonstationarities in the time series. The smoothed inputs more accurately represented the stationary signal structures the techniques are designed to uncover. This empowered them to discern patterns in historical rainfall with the utmost precision and translate such learnings into highly skilled short-term projections.

Between the three approaches, Prophet exhibited the closest forecasts when applied directly to raw data, while ARIMA achieved the best prediction quality employing preprocessed inputs. Overall, ARIMA emerged as the top performer, consistently generating forecasts of higher accuracy than Prophet and LSTM across different degrees of input smoothing. Hyperparameter tuning processes can be used to improve the LSTM model. In future studies, heuristic hyperparameter tuning algorithms can be utilized in order to develop models with better performance.

 5 

# Quantum machine learning for weather forecasting studies

Kolla Bhanu Prakash,    Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Koneru Lakshmaiah Education Foundation, Vaddeswaram, Andhra Pradesh, India

 

## Abstract

The science of climatology can be reformed by quantum machine learning (QML) approaches. The modeling and prediction of atmospheric science is a dynamic topic in weather forecasting, which depends on mathematics and data science. Applying QML techniques for weather forecasting is still in its early days and we can hope that these new approaches will overcome limitations found in classical approaches. Weather analysis has the main concern with optimization, which can be solved using a quantum computer. To avoid prediction errors we need to model properly the parameters involved in the prediction model. To extend work to calibrate new models and estimate parameters, quantum approximate optimization algorithm can be considered. Sometimes we need to deal with complex systems for predicting accurate weather conditions. We can expect to improve weather predictions by using quantum simulations to understand climatology better. Quantum support vector machines and quantum neural networks can be of great use in handling terabytes of climate data for analysis, which will help in identifying links and patterns in data; which classical approaches failed. Because the atmosphere is chaotic, weather forecasting is fundamentally unpredictable. To better measure and control uncertainty in weather forecasts, quantum computing may be applied, which could result in more accurate forecasts. Quantum cryptography and security techniques could be used to safeguard weather data and communication between forecasting centers because weather data are frequently sensitive and essential for various purposes.

 

### Keywords

Quantum computing; quantum machine learning; optimization; weather; climate

 

## 5.1 Introduction

One area where quantum machine learning (QML) may have a big impact is weather forecasting. QML has a lot of possible applications. ([Lin et al., 2024](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib17); [Boehm & Schumer, 2023](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib31)) The intricacy of atmospheric processes presents difficulties for traditional weather forecasting models, and quantum computing may be able to help in processing complicated calculations more quickly. Weather forecasting is full of optimization problems, which are ideally suited for quantum computers. More precise forecasts could be made by optimizing the parameters of intricate atmospheric models using QML algorithms ([Giani & Goff-Eldredge, 2022](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib11); [Kerr, 2007](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib14)). Weather forecasting involves the examination of massive datasets, which QML methods may improve ([Delworth & Knutson, 2000](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib5)). The patterns in weather can be deeply inferred with involvement of quantum computing ([Cui et al., 2021](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib4)). Quantum algorithms expected to simulate better for atmospheric science when compared to classical algorithms, which will improve accuracy ([Guo et al., 2022](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib12)). Uncertainty issues can be better solved using QML and a degree of confidence will be best in projections for climate researchers using these algorithms ([Ermida et al., 2020](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib7); [Vancutsem, et al., 2010](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib29)). Quantum support vector machines (QSVM) and quantum neural network (QNN) will be better choice over classical algorithms for accurate weather predictions ([Paudel et al., 2022](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib24)). Quantum ensemble learning can be another good algorithm to predict weather forecasting ([Paudel et al., 2022](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib24); [Rice et al., 2021](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib25)). The potential of QML is being actively investigated by researchers in several domains, including meteorology.

 

## 5.2 Related work

Quantum physicists and atmospheric scientists are currently working together ([Andersen, 2021](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib1); [Moore, 2006](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib22)). The Earth’s land surface temperature (LST) fluctuation is a crucial marker of climate change, impacting both the natural world and human society ([Franklin & Chong, 2006](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib8)). For example, during the growth seasons, LST is one of the most crucial factors considered while making farming decisions. Without the assistance of satellite remote sensing, fine-resolution LST change cannot be continuously monitored on a worldwide scale ([Giani & Eldredge, 2021](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib10)). With the development and refinement of numerous algorithms over the past few years, the estimation of LST using satellite sensors has greatly improved ([Paudel et al., 2022](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib24)). Long-wave infrared (LWIR) bands are used by Landsat missions to provide thermal data with a temporal resolution of 16 days and a spatial resolution of 30 m. On the other hand, by utilizing two LWIR bands, the MODerate Resolution Imaging Spectroradiometer (MODIS) can monitor global LST significantly more regularly ([Crawford et al., 2021](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib3)). The 1-km spatial resolution of MODIS is perfect for monitoring LST change at the world scale and has been used in several studies, even though it is not as finely resolved as Landsat ([Sebastianelli et al., 2022](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib26)). The daily mean surface temperatures have been computed in previous studies using several coarse-resolution MODIS data ([Zaidenberg et al., 2021](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib30)). Cloud coverage, however, affects the measurement’s accuracy. Furthermore, because it does not account for temporal variation, the surface temperature function employed in the earlier work was a good model for daily temperature estimation but not the best for multiyear or diurnal analysis ([Kulikowski, 2019](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib16)). The computational challenges of monitoring both short- and long-term temperature changes and measuring large-scale LST changes ([Gaitan, 2021](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib9); [Somaroo et al., 1999](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib27)) at high spatial resolution have also been summed up in a few additional publications ([Table 5–1](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#t0010)).

Table 5–1

Potential benefits of using quantum computing.

 
| S. no. || Category || Challenge || Potential benefits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 || Grid resilience and safety || Making certain that power generation facilities are reliable and strong going forward. || Improved climate and weather models may make infrastructure placement safer, and quantum optimization may help with the better design of new resources like wind farms. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2 || Weather forecasts and climate modeling || Satisfying computational demands as simulation and forecasting model complexity and resolution increase. || Enhanced capacity to resolve simulations based on fluid dynamics may enable model enhancements, leading to a more comprehensive comprehension of probable future circumstances and enhancing the planning of mitigation and adaptation strategies. |
| 3 || Grid management || Arranging and allocating resources to balance supply and demand, particularly when the quantity and dispersion of generators increase. || By enhancing operating conditions, quantum optimization may aid with cost-effective management strategies and reduce consumer costs. |
| 4 || Quantum Chemistry || Assessing a wide range of materials’ molecular-scale characteristics and operations in order to promote technological innovation. || Improved methods for mitigating climate change and the discovery and development of novel energy production and storage technologies could be sped up by quantum computing. |

The climate has been changing rapidly. This is due to 1.1°C of human-induced global warming, which is bringing many environmental challenges ([Kose et al., 2022](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib15); [Molina et al., 2023](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib20)). The side effects of the change are increasing nowadays and solutions require additional funding ([Didal et al., 2017](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib6)). Losses and damages result from some climatic consequences that are already so severe that they cannot be adjusted. Before 2025, global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions peak on 1.5°C-aligned paths. As a critical solution way, it is needed to leave fossil fuels ([Biamonte et al., 2017](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib2)). As the target of a net-zero, 1.5°C in increase of temperature seems a recent, optimum solution. It is urgent to have some climate money aiming mitigation as well as adaptation-oriented solutions. If we do not guarantee a fair transition, the effects of climate change—as well as our combined efforts to adapt to and prevent it—will worsen inequality ([Lloyd et al., 2013](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib18)). Social protection systems can be redesigned to incorporate adaptation, thereby reducing the susceptibility of both urban and rural communities to various climatic threats. When combined with initiatives to provide access to infrastructure and essential services like clean water, sanitary conditions, and healthcare, these actions are particularly successful. The delivery of these services can increase the climate resilience of vulnerable areas if governments, civil society organizations, and the corporate sector collaborate. At this point, the ecosystem-oriented adaptation can help in dealing with the issues ([Molina-Perez et al., 2020](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#bib21)).

Resilience can also be strengthened by having access to better technologies, such as solar and wind power, better animal breeding, and crop kinds that are more resilient. There is no doubt about the science. Both the planet and human welfare are at risk due to climate change. If we do not act now, the effects of climate change could be so severe that our world will not recognize itself. There is a brief opportunity in the upcoming years to create a sustainable, living future for everybody. To veer off course, quick, aggressive, and coordinated action is needed to reduce emissions, boost resilience, protect ecosystems, and significantly expand funding for adaptation and loss and damage management.
