## 5.3 Mathematical modeling

Mathematical modeling quantum state encoding equation: $\\left| \\psi \\right\\rangle = \\sum\_{i = 0}^{N - 1}{\\sqrt{p\_{i}}\\left| x\_{i} \\right\\rangle}$<mml:math><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">|</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#968;</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10217;</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:msqrt><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msqrt><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">|</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10217;</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000122si1.png). This equation represents the encoding of classical weather data *x*[*i*] into a quantum state |Ψ⟩. *p*[*i*] represents the probability amplitude associated with each classical data point *x*[*i*].

Quantum circuit equation:

*U*(*θ*) = *e* − *i**H**θ*.<mml:math><mml:mi>U</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>e</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>H</mml:mi><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (5–1)

![Equation](images/F000122si2.png)(5–1) 

This equation defines the unitary operator *U*(*θ*) corresponding to a quantum circuit used in QML. *H* represents the Hamiltonian operator describing the dynamics of the quantum system, and *θ* represents the parameter of the circuit to be optimized.

Quantum variational circuit equation:

|*Ψ*(*θ*)⟩ = *U*(*θ*)|*Ψ*[0]⟩.<mml:math><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">|</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#936;</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10217;</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>U</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">|</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#936;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10217;</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (5–2)

![Equation](images/F000122si3.png)(5–2) 

This equation describes the state produced by applying the variational quantum circuit *U*(*θ*) to an initial quantum state |Ψ[0]⟩. *Θ* is a variational parameter.

Cost function equation:

*C*(*θ*) = ⟨*Ψ*(*θ*)|*H*|*Ψ*(*θ*)⟩.<mml:math><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10216;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#936;</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo><mml:mo>|</mml:mo><mml:mi>H</mml:mi><mml:mo>|</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#936;</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10217;</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (5–3)

![Equation](images/F000122si4.png)(5–3) 

Gradient descent equation:

*θ*[*t*] + 1 = *θ*[*t*] − *η*∇*C*(*θ*[*t*]).<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#951;</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8711;</mml:mo><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (5–4)

![Equation](images/F000122si5.png)(5–4) 

The equation is for the gradient descent optimization algorithm, which can be employed for updating the parameters *θ*. *Η* is for the learning rate, and ∇*C*(*θ*[*t*]) is the gradient (for the cost). It considers the iteration *t*.

Quantum kernel equation:

*K*(*x*[*i*], *x*[*j*]) = ⟨*Ψ*(*x*[*i*])|*Ψ*(*x*[*j*])⟩<mml:math><mml:mi>K</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10216;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#936;</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo><mml:mo>|</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#936;</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10217;</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math> (5–5)

![Equation](images/F000122si6.png)(5–5) 

The equation is the quantum kernel *K*(*x*[*i*], *x*[*j*]), which is employed under the QSVM.

Quantum convolution operation equation:

$\\left| \\Psi\_{out} \\right\\rangle = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{M}{\\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{K}\\left( {\\sum\\limits\_{l = 1}^{L}\\begin{matrix} & ! \\\\ {W\_{jkl} \\otimes \\left| {\\Psi\_{in}\\left\\lbrack j + l - 1 \\right\\rbrack\\left\\lbrack k \\right\\rbrack} \\right\\rangle} & \\\\ \\end{matrix}} \\right.}$<mml:math><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">|</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#936;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">out</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10217;</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mi>M</mml:mi></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mi>K</mml:mi></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>l</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mi>L</mml:mi></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:mtable><mml:mtr><mml:mtd/><mml:mtd><mml:mo>!</mml:mo></mml:mtd></mml:mtr><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mi>l</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8855;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">|</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#936;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">in</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>\[</mml:mo><mml:mspace width=".2em"/><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>l</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>\]</mml:mo><mml:mo>\[</mml:mo><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mo>\]</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10217;</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:mtd><mml:mtd/></mml:mtr></mml:mtable></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:math> (5–6)

![Equation](images/F000122si7.png)(5–6) 

In the equation, the |Ψ[in]⟩ corresponds to the input quantum state while the *W*[*jkl*] are filters and the |Ψ[out]⟩ is for defining the output.

Quantum pooling operation equation:

$\\begin{matrix} {1P} \\\\ {\\left| \\Psi\_{pool} \\right\\rangle = \\left. \\sqrt{}\\sum\\left| {\\Psi\_{in}\\left\\lbrack i \\right\\rbrack} \\right\\rangle \\right.} \\\\ {P\_{i} = 1} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$<mml:math><mml:mtable><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mi>P</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mtd></mml:mtr><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">|</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#936;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">pool</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10217;</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mo>&#8730;</mml:mo><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">|</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#936;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">in</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>\[</mml:mo><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>\]</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10217;</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:mtd></mml:mtr><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mtd></mml:mtr></mml:mtable></mml:math> (5–7)

![Equation](images/F000122si8.png)(5–7) 

Quantum activation function equation:

|*Ψ**o**u**t*⟩ = *f*(|*Ψ**i**n*⟩)<mml:math><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">|</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#936;</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">out</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10217;</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>f</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">|</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#936;</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">in</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">&#10217;</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math> (5–8)

![Equation](images/F000122si9.png)(5–8) 

The above equation is for the quantum activation function *f*(.), connected to the quantum state |Ψ[in]⟩. It briefly results to the quantum state |Ψ[out]⟩. Quantum activation functions introduce nonlinearity into the quantum convolutional neural network (QCNN) model, similar to classical CNNs.

Quantum weight updation equation:

$\\begin{matrix} {(t + 1)} & {(t)} & {\\partial L} \\\\ W\_{jkl} & {= W\_{jkl} - \\eta\\partial} & W\_{jkl} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$<mml:math><mml:mtable><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mtd><mml:mtd><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mtd><mml:mtd><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#8706;</mml:mi><mml:mi>L</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mtd></mml:mtr><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mi>l</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mtd><mml:mtd><mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mi>l</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#951;</mml:mi><mml:mi>&#8706;</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mtd><mml:mtd><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mi>k</mml:mi><mml:mi>l</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mtd></mml:mtr></mml:mtable></mml:math> (5–9)

![Equation](images/F000122si10.png)(5–9) 

During training, the equation represents the update rule for the quantum convolutional filters *W*[*jkl*] using gradient descent optimization, where *L* is the loss function, *η* is the learning rate, and *∂*[*W*]^(*∂L*)*jkl* is the gradient of the loss with respect to the filters.

 

## 5.4 Implementation

The implementation part includes using IBM QISKIT environment as well as the application framework developed by using Python. As general the procedure for the estimating accuracy for weather forecasting using IBM QISKIT includes the steps as:

1.  1. Install and setup IBM QISKIT environment.
2.  2. Import all the required simulators, instances, algorithms, feature maps, and datasets.
3.  3. Load the required dataset and perform preprocessing with initial features.
4.  4. Split the data into training and testing and standardize the features.
5.  5. Implement QSVM for feature maps.
6.  6. Build, deploy, and train QSVM and test it for performance.
7.  7. Calculate accuracy and check for results validation compared to other algorithms.

Following the related steps followed over the IBM QISKIT environment, the weather forecasting steps are done through Python by following the pseudo code as follows:

1.  1. Import the necessary libraries: pandas, sklearn, matplotlib, and numpy.
2.  2. Produce first meteorological data.
3.  3. Establish a DataFrame.
4.  4. Display meteorological data.
5.  5. Get the model’s data ready.
6.  6. Divide the data into sets for testing and training.
7.  7. Develop and adjust the linear regression model.
8.  8. Forecast using the test set.
9.  9. Determine the model’s mean square error.
10.  10. Show the forecasts visually ([Table 5–2](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#t0015)).

[Figs. 5–3](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#f0020) and [5–4](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#f0025) represent quantum training and quantum thermal annealing for weather forecasting. Quantum thermal annealing is a technique used in quantum computing, primarily for optimization problems. It involves leveraging quantum properties to find the global minimum of a given function, which can be useful in solving complex optimization tasks. Weather forecasting involves processing large amounts of data and optimizing models to make accurate predictions. While quantum computing holds promise for enhancing certain aspects of weather forecasting, such as improving model optimization or handling large datasets more efficiently, quantum thermal annealing specifically might not be the most suitable approach for this task. Quantum annealing is considered one of the best mathematically modeled approaches for effective weather forecasting which involves combinatorial optimization problems. [Fig. 5–5](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#f0030) gives a Pytorch program for weather forecasting.

## 5.5 Discussion

In climate studies, particularly when dealing with spatial or spatiotemporal data, kernel functions are often used in various statistical and machine learning applications such as spatial interpolation, spatial smoothing, spatial regression, and spatial clustering. The kernel function is a good option. The experimental design is explained in [Fig. 5–6](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#f0035).

![Figure 5–6 Experimental design.](images/F000122f05-06-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 5–6](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#Bf0035) Experimental design.

QSVM will revolutionize climatology soon with its customized framework and mathematical modelling. [Fig. 5–7](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#f0040) is an example of such a simulation.

![Figure 5–7 Quantum support vector machine for weather forecasting.](images/F000122f05-07-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 5–7](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#Bf0040) Quantum support vector machine for weather forecasting.

A notion from quantum computing, specifically about QML, is the Fock space classifier. The Fock space classifier for climate studies is shown in [Fig. 5–8](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#f0045). A mathematical concept known as “fock space” is used to characterize the quantum states of systems with variable quantities of particles, like atoms in quantum chemistry or photons in quantum optics. The Fock space can be used in QML to represent the feature space, where features of the data are encoded by quantum states. The Fock space classifier manipulates quantum states in the feature space in order to carry out classification tasks. The elasticity of substitution and the environment’s sensitivity to GHGs influence the best course of action in response to climate change.

![Figure 5–8 Fock space classifier for climate studies.](images/F000122f05-08-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 5–8](#Ch005_73-82_B9780443219054000122.xhtml#Bf0045) Fock space classifier for climate studies.

While increasing climate sensitivity demands more immediate and comprehensive mitigation and adaptation activities, higher substitution elasticity of replacement points to a larger reliance on technology innovation and market-based solutions. Nevertheless, to effectively address the difficulties posed by climate change, a comprehensive strategy that incorporates mitigation, adaptation, and international collaboration is necessary.

 

## 5.6 Conclusion and future scope

To solve several significant issues regarding the functioning of Earth’s climate as well as how to create and use energy more effectively, incredibly intricate computations are needed. This includes modeling the complex interactions between constantly changing clouds and climate, as well as assessing a wide range of materials to maximize solar energy output or energy storage. With classical computers running binary code programs, these kinds of problems are getting harder and harder to answer, especially as the number of computations required increases. To cut down on processing costs, many problems are therefore solved with models that rely on approximations. These models’ lack of precision is the price we must pay for these capabilities, however. Furthermore, supercomputers are getting close to surpassing the capabilities of traditional computers even with these approximations.

Recently, scientists have discovered possible high-impact uses for quantum computing technologies. Among them are studies on climate change, renewable energy, and the creation of energy-generating and energy-storing systems that are optimal. Soon, perhaps, QML or quantum deep learning techniques will be able to address the challenges associated with climate forecasting.

 6 

# Emerging technologies approach for climate modeling

Prisilla Jayanthi Gandam^([1](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#aff1)), Muralikrishna Iyyanki^([2](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#aff2)), Xi Chen^([3](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#aff3)), Valentina Emilia Balas^([4](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#aff4)) and Utku Kose^([5](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#aff5),) ^([6](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#aff6)),    ¹CMR Institute of Technology, Hyderabad, Telangana, India,    ²Defence Research and Development Organisation, Research Centre Imarat (RCI), Hyderabad, Telangana, India,    ³Machine Learning Faculty, UC Santa Cruz, CA, United States,    ⁴Aurel Vlaicu University of Arad, Arad, Romania,    ⁵Department of Computer Engineering, Faculty of Engineering, Süleyman Demirel University, Isparta, Turkey,    ⁶University of North Dakota, Grand Forks, ND, United States

 

## Abstract

Climate change represents the most significant challenge facing humanity on a global scale. Machine learning combined with innovative research, can be a powerful tool in combating this challenge. Greenhouse gases are the main cause of the climate crisis that is a persistent and inescapable problem, realized by 195 countries within the framework of the Paris Agreement. UN recalls climate crisis—A Race We Can Win. The climate crisis is triggered by humans and the solutions must also come from every citizen. As citizens, one needs to cease exacerbating the climate problem, and instead consider measures like pollution tax, to foster a greener planet. Let us bring the science and latest technology together to stop climate crisis before it wipes away humans. Nature-based solutions, and new and efficient technologies can help reduce the emissions to net-zero. Moreover, adapting to climate change can bolster resilience and mitigate the environmental changes amplifying global warming.

 

### Keywords

Climate change; digital twin; forest fires; nowcasting; physics-guided models

 

## 6.1 Introduction

Climate models are designed to simulate the uncertainties associated with climate change prediction. These models enhance the understanding of the climate behavior predictability. Scientist utilizes these models which are based on global patterns observed in the ocean and atmosphere. Let us understand how climate is different from weather. Weather pertains to the meteorological conditions at a specific time and place, such as wind, snow, sunshine, and temperature. In contrast, climate refers to the long-term characteristics of the weather experienced at a specific place. In essence, climate provides a comprehensive summary of weather conditions considering the variability over extended periods. Scientists contend that greenhouse gases (GHGs) traps incoming solar energy, leading to warmer Earth’s surface. A sharp rise in GHGs, specifically CO[2], intensifies greenhouse effect, thereby exacerbating climate change. Computer simulations play a pivotal role in climate modeling, helping researchers deciphers the impact of GHG emissions on the Earth’s climate.

The study encompasses topics such as the emission of CO pollutant, forest fires, climate change adaptation, food security, forecasting climate, and physics-guided machine learning.

 

## 6.2 Carbon monoxide pollutant—statistical trends in Visakhapatnam

Carbon monoxide (CO) is a known to react with other gases, producing harmful compounds. This interaction influences the volume of GHG when released into the atmosphere. For this study, CO data were collected from Visakhapatnam, a region in the state of Andhra Pradesh, India, for the months of April, July, and December in 2022. The data were charted to showcase the relationship between CO levels and specific dates on both monthly and hourly basis. [Fig. 6–1](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#f0010) displaces the monthly trends, indicating CO level fluctuations throughout the month, rather than consistent level. [Fig. 6–2](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#f0015) represents hourly data trends for the 3 months under consideration. A notable observation from the trends is a dip in the curve at 10:00 a.m. across all the months. The curve spikes during the evening rush hour and diminishes around 9:00 p.m.

![Figure 6–1 The monthly trends of CO in Visakhapatnam.](images/F000092f06-01-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 6–1](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#Bf0010) The monthly trends of CO in Visakhapatnam.

![Figure 6–2 The hourly trends of CO in Visakhapatnam.](images/F000092f06-02-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 6–2](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#Bf0015) The hourly trends of CO in Visakhapatnam.

Few sources of CO are forest fires, volcanoes, emission from vehicles, and biomass burning. Human emissions are liable for two-thirds of CO in the atmosphere. In the next section, forest fires are explored for upsetting the climate change.

 

## 6.3 Forest fires—naturally air pollution—human hazardous

Forests are a reservoir for very rich minerals and wealthy natural resources. These forests are open to natural fire and continue to burn for several days, weeks, and months in few states of India, and few parts of the world. California, Siberia, and North America are well known places where forest fires occur frequently burning up the trees. In India, Uttarakhand is the place which is very frequently prone to fires. Forest fires are a natural part of the ecology, a threat to property and wildlife, and are a risk factor for respiratory and cardiovascular diseases.

A natural forest fire destroys the wildlife incontrollable and causes the rise of temperature in the environment. The fire triangle (heat, oxygen, and fuel) helps to burn the green trees in the forest faster and spreads swiftly. The factors include temperature, moisture of soil, and the existence of trees, shrubs, and other potential fuels ([Pacha, 2020](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib26)). [Goodman and Horton (2021)](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib13) reported that the most fire-prone states in India are few regions in Northern East, Madhya Pradesh, Odisha, Chhattisgarh, Himachal Pradesh, and Uttarakhand. More than 3.5 M acres have been burned in the year 2020 (United States), which ended as the most destructive season on record ([Kinver, 2021](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib21)).

According, to the EU’s Earth monitoring service, the wildfires in Siberia, North America, and in and around the Mediterranean have emitted CO[2] making a record for warming a planet ([France-Presse, 2021](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib35)). Copernicus Atmosphere Monitoring Service (CAMS) in 2021 reported that globally, forest trees go up in flames, which emitted more than 2.5 billion tons of CO[2]—equivalent to India’s annual emissions from all different sources—in July and August. [Cerdà (2019)](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib9) noted that above half of CO[2] emissions from wildfires in July came from North America and Siberia.

The forest fire enhances sediment and water yielding during the period of disturbance ([Katila et al., 2019](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib20)). sustainable development goals (SDG) undermines the forest contributions to climate and development ([Baumgartner, 2019](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib6)). Deforestation and forest fires are the major concerns to achieve the SDGs, that is, an increased vulnerability to extreme weather events (SDG 1) are faced by that region, a rainfall loss and pollinators of crop is observed (SDG 2), and severe respiratory illnesses can be caused by forest fires (SDG 3) ([https://www.weforum.org/agenda/2020/09/climate-feedback-climate-change-forest-fires](https://www.weforum.org/agenda/2020/09/climate-feedback-climate-change-forest-fires)).

In April 2019 the Goseong Fire ([Hirschlag, 2023](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib14)) in South Korea became the country’s most devastating wildfire, raging for 3 days, consuming 529 hectares demolishing over 2000 structures, and necessitating the evacuation of around 4200 individuals. The inferno resulted in estimated damages of US $4.6 million and claimed two lives.

Wildfires release vast quantities of finer particulates (PM[2.5]) and even finer than nanoparticles that are harmful to human health. The tiny particles are 30 times smaller than the human hair that can easily penetrate into the lung membranes and damages the respiratory system, passing into the blood stream ([Aguilera et al., 2021](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib2)). By the end of 2019, during the bushfires in Australia, there was an increase in hospital admissions by 34% for breathing problems developed due to wildfires in the New South Wales.

The forest fires study in the western United States for the year 2004–09, estimated that around 46 M people were exposed to a wave of smoke. The smoke has caused high PM[2.5] levels with the increase of 7.2% increase in hospital admissions due of respiratory illness. It concluded that 339,000 premature deaths were caused in that year. High-efficiency particulate absorbing filter (HEPA) filters in air purifiers can reduce fine particles within indoors.

The researchers at NASA discovered that the Earth is surrounded by a haze of smoke in the troposphere over places like Antarctica. It affects one-fifth of the aerosols from global fires. Breathing in this size particle will cause a variety of health issues such as heart disease, stroke, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, and lower respiratory infections including pneumonia. The study estimated that the wildfires in northern California released as much CO[2] within just a single week, the estimation emitted was higher over PM[2.5], and the other pollutant released from all the cars and trucks in California in the year 2017.

In June 1950 the Chinchaga Fire started in British Columbia, Canada that increased and was out of control and ended 5 months later in Alberta; it was estimated that this fire burned approximately 1.2 M hectares (3 M acres) of boreal forest, whereas the fires that recorded in the year 2020 burned 1.6 Mha and in 2021, in California, 16 kha of forest was burned totally.

During the beginning of the 20th century, the temperatures in California, Washington, and Oregon have increased drastically. Rains replaced snow in the Northwest, warmer temperatures have reduced snowpack, and soils dried up. All of these factors combine to create drier conditions that allow wildfires to ignite and spread. In the year 2020 fire emissions are three times higher than the historical 21st century average Washington and Oregon. In California wildfires generated emissions of 4–8 times higher than the daily emissions from all economic activity across the state. The global increase in temperature with increase in CO[2] concentration has been seen.

In a study of forest fires in the California for the period 1999–2012, the increase in respiratory hospitalizations ranged from 1.3% to 10% with a 10 μg/m³ increase in wildfire-specific PM[2.5] ([Holm et al., 2021](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib15)). The major components of wildfire emissions are organic and elemental carbons, as well as gases, including CO[2], CO, NO[2], and volatile organic compounds (VOCs). The pediatric asthma visits happen to increase and overall children have the potential for long-term health effects from repeated high exposures during wildfires ([Reid & Maestas, 2019](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib27)). The study states the long-term respiratory health impacts of wildfire smoke decreases the functionality of the lungs for 10 years after exposure ([https://bhuvan.nrsc.gov.in/home/index.php](https://bhuvan.nrsc.gov.in/home/index.php)).

 

### 6.3.1 Results of economic loss due to forest fires

The economic loss ([Table 6–1](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#t0010)) was found as represented in state-wise in India due to forest fire from the year 2013 to 2018. The highest was found to be in the years 2016 and 2018 in Himachal Pradesh. [Table 6–2](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#t0015) represents the highest number of forest fires in India for the period 2018–20 detected by Forest Survey of India (FSI) using Modi Sensors. The highest number of fires recorded were in Madhya Pradesh (MP) (4929), Mizoram (2795), and Assam (3000).

Artificial neural networks (ANNs, [Al-Kahlout et al., 2020](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib4); [Nebot & Mugica, 2021](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib24); [Safi & Bouroumi, 2013](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib28)) and support vector machine ([Li et al., 2020](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib22)) were also implemented for predicting forest fires.

[Table 6–3](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#t0020) lists the various forest fire characteristics. In this study, convolution neural network model ([Fig. 6–4](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#f0025)) is introduced to predict the subsequent date on which a forest fire might occur at a given location. By inputting all the parameters specified in [Table 6–3](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#t0020) into the model, it trains on the data and generates the prediction. The model is then expected to forecast the next date, the precise area that might be affected, and the potential economic loss. This information can aid in proactive measures, such as evacuating nearby residential or commercial buildings.

According to [Buonocore et al. (2022)](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib8), digital twin technology enables utilities to simulate the possibility of any disaster under any given weather condition. Forest digital twin framework is proposed and used for forest management. A real-time tracking and predictions with the digital twin technology helps to protect public health from the air quality risk ([Jiao et al., 2023](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib16)). Automatic Flight Plan Creation uses digital twins, to enable unmanned aerial vehicles (UAV) state monitoring and prediction using artificial intelligence for forest fires ([Bae et al., 2022](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib5)). The digital twins ([Sahal et al., 2021](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib29)) provide an auto-detection by utilizing operational data intelligence. [Yun et al. (2022)](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib34) proposed the architecture of digital twin to provide accurate services for predicting disaster.

 

## 6.4 Climate change adaptation

UN Secretary General, Antonio Guterres once remarked “Climate change is the defining issue of our time… every day we fail to act is a day that we step a little closer towards a fate that none of us wants- a fate that will resonate through generations in the damage done to humankind and life on earth” ([https://www.undrr.org/climate-action-and-disaster-risk-reduction](https://www.undrr.org/climate-action-and-disaster-risk-reduction)).

There is compelling evidence of rising in average global air and ocean temperatures, accelerated melting of snow and ice, and the increasing sea levels. Such profound shift contributes to climate change and consequently escalates disaster risks in two significant ways.

1.  1. The amplification of weather and climate hazards, and
2.  2. The exacerbation of the community vulnerability to natural hazards due to ecosystem degradation, and dwindling availability of water and food resources.

Certain regions experience heightened drought, crop damage, and an increased risk of wildfire. Conversely, regions witnessing increased precipitation are susceptible to floods and landslides, which often result in significant loss of life and assets.

The intergovernmental panel on climate change (IPCC) describes mitigation as “an anthropogenic intervention to reduce the anthropogenic forcing of the climate system; it includes strategies to reduce GHG sources and emissions and enhancing GHG sinks” ([Edenhofer, 2014](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib12)).

Various machine learning techniques have been employed in climate study, such as prediction of rainfall, and the use of multilayer perceptron neural network algorithm for predicting drought ([Ali et al., 2017](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib3)). An ANN model was developed to estimate the yearly average of day-wise solar radiation for Turkey, leading to the creation of a satellite-based solar radiation map ([Şahin et al., 2013](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib30)). Geographic information system and Bayesian Belief Network were introduced for assessing floods vulnerability index in Toronto ([Abebe et al., 2018](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib1)). ANN models have been applied to for climate change adaptation (CCA) policy ([Biesbroek et al., 2020](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib7)), while CNNs have been used for wildfire detection ([Oh et al., 2020](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib25)). Furthermore, in the study by [Jain et al. (2023)](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib17), Artificial Intelligence (AI) has been identified as a promising tool for CCA.

 

## 6.5 Food security—impact of climate change

Climate-induced changes in temperature, precipitation, and rain patterns have impacted the yield quality and food safety. The food availability is a fundamental facet of ensuring food security and living standard ([Karanth et al., 2023](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib19)). Machine learning (ML) algorithms and genomic prediction offer innovative techniques to speed up more breeding efforts toward increase in crop yielding to face the drought and increased temperatures ([Cortés & Lopez-Hernandez, 2021](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib11)). Increase in heavy rainfall will cause overflow from human sewage contaminating the other sources of water, leading to different water-borne diseases. The heavy rainfall will increase the breed of mosquito and other insects causing various diseases like malaria, cholera, dengue, diarrhea, typhoid, and leptospirosis. Moreover, a small rise in temperature will affect the climate change by increasing human infection risk. Temperature increase would risk the domestically acquired diseases, leading to the growth of *Campylobacter* sp. on food ([Semenza et al., 2012](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib31)). Temperature rise will increase the risk of food poisoning and spoilage unless necessary steps such as extension of cold-chain are improved ([James & James, 2010](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib18)). Climate change has impact on food security ([Uyttendaele et al., 2015](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib33)). The project named CLimate change and Emerging risks for Food SAfety (CLEFSA) has addressed the uncertainty and with scientific study proved the risk of food-borne diseases with the temperature rise ([Maggiore et al., 2020](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib23)).

 

## 6.6 Forecasting climate and weather

 

### 6.6.1 TrajGRU nowcasting

Nowcasting refers to the development of AI algorithms for very short-term forecasting. Using remote sensing technology, it predicts weather up to about 3 hours in advance. Nowcasting is pertinent to local weather phenomena such as thunderstorms, cyclones, and extreme temperature variations. With very short-range forecasting from 0 to 6 hours and short-range forecasting up to 72 hours. Nowcasting describes the current weather situation and extrapolates this for several hours assuming no significant alterations in the forecasted precipitation pattern.

Fondazione Bruno Kessler and Arpae introduced two different TrajGRU methods for nowcast forecasting the climate in Northern Italy:

1.  1. A convective event on May 28, 2022 over Northern Italy
2.  2. A stratiform event on April 22, 2022.

Convection involves the vertical transportation of heat and moisture in the atmosphere, due to updrafts and downdrafts in an unstable environment. In contrast, stratiform pertains to situation where large air masses rise diagonally because larger-scale winds and atmosphere dynamics compel them to overlay.

The variable reflectivity (Z\_60) and other such latitude, longitude, geographic map limits, and data resolution in degrees.

The data was obtained from ([https://climateindex.eu/](https://climateindex.eu/)) for this study. TrajGRU model 1 utilizes on reflectivity images as training inputs for nowcasting forecast as shown in [Fig. 6–5](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#f0030). This figure showcases reflectivity images used as inputs, with distinct color gradients representing varying intensities of precipitation. Patterns can be observed for the convective event on May 28, 2022, and the stratiform event on April 22, 2022. Annotations indicate key geographical areas and meteorological phenomena. The map boundaries, latitude, and longitude lines provide geographical context to the forecasted regions.

![Figure 6–5 Visualization of the TrajGRU model 1 outputs for nowcasting forecasts in Northern Italy. Map lines delineate study areas and do not necessarily depict accepted national boundaries.](images/F000092f06-05-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 6–5](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#Bf0030) Visualization of the TrajGRU model 1 outputs for nowcasting forecasts in Northern Italy. Map lines delineate study areas and do not necessarily depict accepted national boundaries.

The TrajGRU model 2 is trained using reflectivity images and lightning data for nowcasting forecast in [Fig. 6–6](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#f0035). The lightning data are sourced from LAMPINET network, operated by Air Force Meteorological Service. The color gradients represent varying levels of reflectivity, while lightning occurrences are indicated by distinct markers.

### 6.6.2 Case study of Italy (E³CI)

**European Extreme Events Climate Index (E**^(**3**)**CI)** is an innovative operational service designed for weather-induced hazard assessment and management. It is supported by the Foundation Big Data and Artificial Intelligence for Human Development (IFAB).

For the E³CI of Italy during the period 1981–2010, one can observe average values close to zero on the same period. However, larger values have been noted in recent years, attributed to the effects of climate change. The mean of the components of E³CI is presented in [Table 6–4](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#t0025).

Table 6–4

Mean of components of E³CI for Italy (1981–2010).

 
| Components || Mean |
| --- | --- |
| Cold stress || −0.000525 |
| --- | --- |
| Drought || −0.002776 |
| E³CI || −0.000994 |
| Extreme precipitation || −0.000536 |
| Extreme wind || −0.001316 |
| Heat stress || 0.000184 |

### 6.6.4 Probabilistic ocean regime predictions using bayesian neural network

For probabilistic prediction of ocean circulation regimes, [Clare et al. (2022)](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib10) implemented Bayesian neural network. Initially [Sonnewald et al. (2019)](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#bib32) identified ocean regimes by k-means clustering. The input features considered were wind stress curl, mean sea surface height (SSH) for 20 years, gradients of mean SSH, bathymetry, and gradients of bathymetry and Coriolis in [Fig. 6–15](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#f0080) to predict the ocean regimes and build a deterministic network. In [Fig. 6–15](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#f0080), one can see the dense layers are more appropriate with the data shaped for a gridpoint-by-gridpoint technique. The last layer must have SoftMax activation as the target data are categorical.

![Figure 6–15 Bayesian neural network (BNN) for prediction of ocean regime. Map lines delineate study areas and do not necessarily depict accepted national boundaries.](images/F000092f06-15-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 6–15 Bayesian neural network (BNN) for prediction of ocean regime. Map lines delineate study areas and do not necessarily depict accepted national boundaries.

In BNNs the parameters (weights and biases) are distributions rather than deterministic shown in [Fig. 6–16](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#f0085). From Tensorflow probability, OneHotCategorical layer should be used to achieve the network output as distribution. In a deterministic network, dense layers are replaced with DenseVariational layers in a BNN.

![Figure 6–16 Box-and-whisker plot of BNN predictions of ocean regimes.](images/F000092f06-16-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 6–16](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#Bf0085) Box-and-whisker plot of BNN predictions of ocean regimes.

Data can be procured from “ecmwf-open-data” to plot temperature ([Fig. 6–17](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#f0090)) and mean sea level pressure ([Fig. 6–18](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#f0095)). This data encompasses

1.  1. coordinates, viz. time (datetime), step (timedelta), heightAboveGround, latitude, longitude, valid\_time (time, step), meanSea;
2.  2. date variables, viz. t2m (time, step, heightAboveGround, latitude, longitude) and msl (time, step, meanSea, latitude, longitude);
3.  3. Attributes, viz. GRIB\_edition, GRIB\_centre, GRIB\_centreDescription, and GRIB\_subCentre.

## 6.7 Physics-guided machine learning—physically informed parameterization

Physics-guided ML is a unique approach within knowledge-guided ML, wherein ML outputs are confined to feasible solutions informed by physical understanding. Consider the task of predicting near-surface specific humidity and relative humidity. By definition, negative predictions or values surpassing 1 (indicative of supersaturation) would be improbable. The following figures ([Figs. 6–19](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#f0100) to [6–24](#Ch006_85-103_B9780443219054000092.xhtml#f0125)) extrapolate various climate models, integrating them with near-surface specific humidity and relative humidity, respectively. These models have been created via deep learning methods utilizing Python on the Google Colab platform.

## 6.8 Conclusion

Modeling climate change is an intricate endeavor, necessitating the comprehension of multifarious parameter dynamics. This study has illuminated climate models specific to Italy and Germany. Given the datasets for other countries, analogous climate models could be rendered. Technologies like ML algorithms, digital twins, and deep learning models have proven instrumental in facilitating daily, monthly, or even decadal climate forecasting for any given region. Such predictions play a crucial role in global warming mitigation efforts and the broader goal of preserving our planet.
