 7 

# Computational modeling for agriculture and climate change relation

İlhan Uysal,    Burdur Mehmet Akif Ersoy University, Bucak Zeliha Tolunay School of Applied Technology And Business, Department of Information Systems and Technologies, Burdur, Turkey

 

## Abstract

Agriculture has been a fundamental pillar in the development of human societies, economies, and cultural practices for thousands of years. Yet, the reliability and efficiency of these agricultural systems are currently at risk due to the intricate and extensive effects of climate change. Factors like escalating temperatures, shifting rainfall patterns, and severe weather events are formidable obstacles to worldwide food security and the enduring viability of agriculture. In light of these issues, there is a growing necessity for researchers and policymakers to rely on computational modeling. This sophisticated approach is essential for comprehending, forecasting, and tackling the intricate dynamics between agriculture and climate change.

This research delves into the challenges imposed by climate change on global food security and the sustainability of agriculture. It emphasizes the role of computational modeling in confronting these issues. The discussion encompasses the constraints inherent in agricultural data, the advancement of computational models in the fields of agriculture and climate science, and how these models are crucial in predicting future scenarios based on various climate change conditions. Additionally, the chapter evaluates the financial consequences of climate change on agricultural sectors. It includes case studies and instances demonstrating how computational modeling aids in formulating strategies for climate-resilient agricultural practices and guides policy-making processes.

 

### Keywords

Agriculture; climate change; computational model; climate policy; farming practices

 

## 7.1 Introduction

Agriculture has long been a cornerstone of human progress, influencing the development of societies, economies, and cultural norms throughout history. Yet, this vital sector now faces serious threats from the intricate and widespread effects of climate change. Issues such as escalating temperatures, shifting rainfall patterns, and severe meteorological phenomena present substantial obstacles to the stability of the global food supply and the sustainability of agricultural practices. To combat these challenges, there is an increasing imperative for researchers and policymakers to utilize computational modeling. This advanced tool is crucial for deciphering, forecasting, and addressing the complex interplay between agricultural practices and the changing climate.

For millennia, agriculture has been a foundational element of human society, playing a crucial role in providing sustenance, employment, and shaping cultural identities. The evolution and enhancement of agricultural methods and technologies have been pivotal in supporting population expansion, economic growth, and elevating standards of living globally. However, contemporary agricultural stability and output are now facing an unparalleled challenge due to climate change.

Attributable to human-induced factors like greenhouse gas emissions, widespread deforestation, and rampant industrialization, climate change has emerged as a paramount threat to the Earth’s environmental equilibrium. The steady increase in average global temperatures, alterations in rainfall patterns, and the frequency of extreme climatic phenomena are exerting profound impacts on both natural habitats and human communities.

Agriculture plays a dual role in the context of climate change, acting both as a contributor and a casualty in this intricate dynamic. Agricultural activities are a significant source of greenhouse gas emissions, further intensifying climate change issues. Conversely, agriculture is profoundly impacted by climate shifts, influencing factors such as crop production, water resources, and overall food security. Addressing this intricate interplay is crucial for the maintenance of sustainable agriculture and ensuring global food stability.

In the realm of science, computational modeling emerges as a vital instrument. This involves crafting mathematical models and computer-based simulations to mirror and scrutinize real-world scenarios. Specifically, in the sphere of agriculture and climate change, these computational models provide a structured method to dissect and comprehend the intricate interrelations among climatic factors, agricultural practices, and economic elements.

The use of computational modeling offers distinct benefits compared to classic experimental methods. It allows scientists to investigate numerous climate conditions, evaluate the effects of different farming methods, and forecast future scenarios under varying climate change circumstances. This approach minimizes the reliance on labor-intensive and lengthy field studies, simultaneously enhancing the capability to make well-informed choices and create effective adaptation plans.

Over the years, computational models in agricultural and climate sciences have evolved significantly. From the first simple models to complex, coupled models that integrate biophysical and socioeconomic components, the field has witnessed remarkable advances. Today’s models are more sophisticated, data-driven, and able to capture the subtleties of climate–agriculture interactions at various scales.

A steady increase in global temperatures directly affects crop physiology, phenology, and yields. Heat stress at critical growth stages can lead to reduced yields and even crop failure. Understanding temperature thresholds for various crops and regions is crucial to developing adaptive strategies to cope with rising temperatures.

Climate change is altering rainfall patterns, leading to more frequent and intense droughts or heavy rainfall events. Changes in water availability significantly affect crop water demand, irrigation practices, and water resources management. Computational models help assess water demand–supply dynamics and optimize irrigation strategies in a changing climate.

As climate change progresses, there is an anticipated rise in the frequency and severity of extreme weather phenomena like hurricanes, floods, and storms. These occurrences can lead to significant harm to agricultural crops, infrastructure, and communities reliant on farming. Computational models are instrumental in assessing the risk and susceptibility of agricultural frameworks to these events, aiding in the development of strategies for disaster readiness.

Increased atmospheric carbon dioxide concentrations resulting from human activities affect plant physiology, including photosynthesis, water use efficiency, and nutrient uptake. Understanding the effects of elevated carbon dioxide levels on different crops helps to predict changes in plant growth patterns and adapt agricultural practices accordingly.

[Fig. 7–1](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#f0010) illustrates how climate change influences crop yields through a combination of direct, indirect, and socioeconomic factors ([Raza et al., 2019](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib36)).

![Figure 7–1 Direct, indirect, and socioeconomic impacts of climate change on agricultural production (Raza et al., 2019).](images/F000109f07-01-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 7–1](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#Bf0010) Direct, indirect, and socioeconomic impacts of climate change on agricultural production ([Raza et al., 2019](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib36)).

 

## 7.2 Preliminaries

In their research, Helfer and colleagues introduced a computational framework that leverages pervasive computing to forecast soil fertility and yield in agriculture, based on historical context. By integrating a variety of sensors and machine learning techniques, their theory suggests that farming can be made more efficient and productive, with a reduced ecological footprint. The objective of the study is to describe this proposed model and its architectural deployment, to review comparable endeavors in precision agriculture and data prediction, and to showcase the outcomes of certain experiments and quantitative analyses. The study’s results indicate that this model is effective in estimating soil health and agricultural productivity. The experiments and analyses included in the study demonstrate promising levels of precision and efficacy. This model is anticipated to enhance agricultural efficiency and productivity, while simultaneously reducing environmental impacts ([Helfer et al., 2020](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib16)).

Kompas et al. utilized a temporal variant of the computable general equilibrium (CGE) model, known as GTAP-INT, within the global trade analysis project (GTAP) framework, to assess the effects of climate change on individual countries' GDP and the overall economic advantages of adhering to the Paris Climate Agreement. This model was implemented for all 139 countries in the GTAP database, with outcomes detailed for a range of temperature shifts and representative concentration pathway (RCP) scenarios. The research indicates that climate change’s impact on the global average GDP varies significantly based on the RCP scenario, yet the effects on each country could be substantial across different RCPs. Furthermore, the study highlights the considerable global economic benefits that could result from compliance with the Paris Agreement. Despite its comprehensive analysis, the study acknowledges certain limitations of the CGE model, including a lack of randomness and an inability to consider the jump-diffusion characteristics of stochastic processes. Overall, this research offers an in-depth evaluation of the economic consequences of climate change and the importance of measures to counteract these effects ([Kompas et al., 2018](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib22)).

In their comprehensive review, Jarvis and colleagues discuss various global climate models and their projections for the 21st century’s climate patterns. This review delves into the scientific principles underpinning global climate modeling, highlighting the uncertainties inherent in future climate forecasts. It includes a synopsis of the Special Report Emission Scenarios. Present scientific consensus suggests a rise in global temperatures by 1°C–3°C by 2050, accompanied by intricate and region-specific alterations in precipitation. The review notes a significant unpredictability in forecasting extreme weather events, particularly hurricanes. It also explores the expected consequences of climate change on agricultural yield, the prevalence of pests and diseases, and the effects of CO[2] enrichment on crops. Generally, the impact on crop productivity is anticipated to be adverse, with potential productivity gains at modest temperature increases nullified by more significant warming beyond 1°C. The possible mitigating influence of CO[2] fertilization is a subject of ongoing debate. While there is a consensus on the rise of agricultural pests and diseases due to climate change, only a limited number of studies have quantified these impacts, underscoring the need for further research in this domain ([Jarvis et al., 2010](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib18)).

Kumar et al. presented a study discussing the growing importance of computational modeling using intelligent techniques in agriculture to predict agricultural output with minimum input data. The study highlights the need for locally appropriate integrated farming systems (IFS) to address the specific challenges faced by small-scale farmers. Computational models are developed by including various independent variables that directly or indirectly affect the agricultural output of a system. These models can also be used for educational purposes by teaching the processes and behavior of agricultural systems in response to different input variables. Calibration, verification, and validation procedures are crucial for building accurate simulation models. While computer simulations are effective in depicting and comparing theoretical scenarios, they need to be compatible with real-world observations to accurately model actual case studies. By using computational modeling, researchers can significantly reduce the costs associated with conducting multiple field or laboratory experiments. As a result, computational modeling has been described as an increasingly effective tool for designing and analyzing agricultural systems ([Kumar et al., 2015](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib24)).

Francesco Bosello and Jian Zhang’s research focuses on evaluating the economic effects of climate change on agriculture by 2050 through a CGE model. Their findings indicate that while the overall impact on food supply and welfare is modest, the distributional effects are notably pronounced, particularly in developing nations. The study employs an integrative model that merges global circulation models, crop growth simulations, land use planning, and economic analysis. Key climate factors such as temperature shifts, CO[2] levels, and precipitation changes are considered, alongside other environmental changes brought about by climate change, including alterations in land quality, water resources, and the frequency of extreme weather events. The research also examines the direct effects of climate change on crop physiology, the repercussions for farming adaptation at the micro-level, and economic adaptation strategies at national and global scales. The study finds a detrimental impact of climate change on cereal production, counterbalanced by increased productivity in other crop types. Overall, the study projects a relatively minor decrease in global food supply (1.8%) and global welfare (0.06%). Nonetheless, the adverse effects on agriculture due to climate change are expected to vary greatly among countries, with developing nations facing the brunt. The study emphasizes the need for consistent, comprehensive modeling to accurately assess the impacts of climate change on agriculture and advises policymakers to prioritize adaptation initiatives in developing countries to address the unequal distributional effects of climate change on agriculture ([Bosello & Zhang, 2005](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib7)).

Purcell et al. presented a review of current research on digital twins in agriculture, focusing on their potential to support sustainable agricultural practices. The concept of a digital twin is examined as a virtual laboratory that can be used to assess and predict the impact of changes in agricultural assets (such as crops, soil, and weather) without the costs, risks, or disruptions associated with physical experiments. The challenges and opportunities of using digital twins in agriculture are also discussed, including the need for data integration and computer modeling, as well as potential negative impacts on the environment and society. It provides a roadmap for the development of digital twins in agriculture, indicating the key milestones that need to be achieved to support their open and sustainable development. They also highlight the importance of socio-technological-ecological considerations in the development of digital twins and the need for interdisciplinary collaboration to ensure that digital twins are used in a way that supports environmental sustainability and social equity ([Purcell et al., 2023](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib32)).

 

## 7.3 Climate change, agricultural adaptation strategies, and computational modeling

Agriculture, one of the main sectors of human activity, is directly affected by a changing climate ([Akalin, 2014](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib3)). With climate change projections pointing to more frequent and severe extreme weather events, changing precipitation patterns, and rising temperatures, the need for effective adaptation strategies in agriculture becomes crucial. Adapting agricultural practices to a changing climate is crucial not only for ensuring food security but also for enhancing the resilience of rural communities and sustainable resource management.

Throughout history, farming communities have developed and passed down traditional knowledge and practices that enhance resilience in the face of changing climatic conditions. Indigenous farmers and rural communities often have valuable awareness of locally adaptive practices such as crop varieties, water management techniques, and land use practices ([Quandt et al., 2023](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib33)). These knowledge systems are deeply connected to local ecosystems and have sustained agriculture in different environments for centuries.

*   • *Crop Diversity and Indigenous Seeds:* Traditional farmers have long relied on diverse crop varieties that exhibit different tolerances to climate stressors. By growing indigenous seeds and locally adapted varieties, farmers can increase resilience to changing environmental conditions and reduce vulnerability to crop failures.
*   • *Agroforestry and Integrated Agriculture:* Agroforestry practices that combine tree cultivation with crop and livestock production promote ecological balance, improve soil fertility, and provide natural buffers against extreme weather events. Similarly, IFS capitalize on synergies between different components to create resilient and sustainable agricultural landscapes.
*   • *Water Harvesting and Conservation:* Local water management practices such as rainwater harvesting, construction of check dams, and community-led water-sharing systems optimize water use and ensure water availability during periods of drought or erratic rainfall.

Advances in science and technology have created new possibilities for agricultural adaptation to climate change. From precision agriculture to climate-smart innovations, these technological interventions empower farmers with data-driven decision-making tools and sustainable practices. Leveraging modern technologies such as remote sensing, geographic information systems, and global positioning systems, precision agriculture optimizes resource use by applying inputs such as water, fertilizers, and pesticides exactly where and when they are needed. This approach increases productivity, reduces environmental impact, and increases resilience to climate variability ([Gebbers & Adamchuk, 2010](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib13)). Plant breeders are developing climate-resilient crop varieties through traditional breeding techniques and, increasingly, biotechnology. These new varieties exhibit traits such as drought tolerance, heat tolerance, and pest resistance, contributing to increased agricultural productivity in changing climates ([Varotto et al., 2020](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib40)). Water scarcity is a major concern in many regions affected by climate change. Water-saving irrigation methods such as drip irrigation and micro-sprinklers minimize water wastage and increase water use efficiency, enabling farmers to manage water resources sustainably. Access to accurate and timely weather information is vital for farmers to plan their agricultural activities and mitigate risks associated with extreme weather events. Weather forecasting and early warning systems are seen as important support for decisions on crop planting, harvesting, and disaster preparedness.

The role of governments and international organizations in supporting agricultural adaptation to climate change is instrumental in promoting resilience and sustainability at broader scales. Policy interventions cover a range of measures, from promoting climate-smart practices to improving research and extension services.

*   • *Climate-Resilient Agricultural Policies:* Governments develop and implement policies that promote the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices ([Manevska-Tasevska et al., 2023](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib27)). These policies should include financial support for farmers, insurance programs, and subsidies for climate-smart technologies.
*   • *Research and Extension Services:* Investing in agricultural research and extension services facilitates the dissemination of knowledge, technological advances, and best practices to farmers. These services enable farmers to adapt more effectively to climate change and promote innovation in agricultural practices.
*   • *Climate Change Adaptation Funds*: The creation of funds dedicated to agricultural adaptation supports projects that increase climate resilience in agricultural communities ([Bouwer & Aerts, 2006](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib8)). These funds are better suited for sustainable land management, infrastructure development, and capacity-building initiatives.

 

### 7.3.1 Basic mathematical expressions and models for climate change, agricultural adaptation strategies, and computational modeling

Climate change refers to the change in climate parameters such as temperature, precipitation, and sea levels worldwide over time. The basic formulas used mathematically for climate change are given below.

The rate of climate change (Δ*T*/Δ*t*): Measures the change in average temperature over a given period and is expressed as [Eq. (7.1)](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#eqn1) ([Kelly et al., 2013](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib21)).

*Δ**T*/*Δ**t* = (*T*2−*T*1)/(*t*2−*t*1)<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#916;</mml:mi><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#916;</mml:mi><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math> (7.1)

![Equation](images/F000109si1.png)(7.1) 

Δ*T* is the temperature change (°C), Δ*t* is the time change (s), *T*1 is the initial temperature (°C), *T*2 is the end temperature (°C), *t*1 is the start time (s), and*t*2 is the end time (s).

Agricultural adaptation strategies include various techniques and practices used to ensure the sustainability of agriculture under climate change conditions. Mathematical expressions are preferred to evaluate and optimize the impact of these strategies. For example, a model such as [Eq. (7.2)](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#eqn2) can be used to optimize irrigation strategies ([Gürbüz, 2019](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib15)):

*I**r**r**i**g**a**t**i**o**n* *e**f**f**i**c**i**e**n**c**y*(%) = *t**o**t**a**l* *w**a**t**e**r* *y**i**e**l**d* − *t**o**t**a**l* *w**a**t**e**r* *u**s**e* × 100<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Irrigation efficiency</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mo>%</mml:mo><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">total water yield</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">total water use</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#215;</mml:mo><mml:mn>100</mml:mn></mml:math> (7.2)

![Equation](images/F000109si2.png)(7.2) 

Computational modeling can be defined as the use of mathematical models to analyze climate change and agricultural adaptation strategies. The energy budget equation used for climate modeling describes the flow of energy from the sun, surface heat, temperature changes in the atmosphere, and radiation balance ([Meyers & Hollinger, 2004](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib28)).

*Q* = *H* + *L**E* + *G* + *Δ**S*<mml:math><mml:mi>Q</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>H</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">LE</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>G</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&#916;</mml:mi><mml:mi>S</mml:mi></mml:math> (7.3)

![Equation](images/F000109si3.png)(7.3) 

According to the mathematical expression given in [Eq. (7.3)](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#eqn3),

*   • *Q* is expressed as the energy balance of the surface (W/m²).
*   • *H* represents the sensible heat flow and is commonly known as latent heat flow. Heat is due to temperature differences from surfaces in contact with air (W/m²).
*   • *LE* represents latent heat flux and is the energy change caused by water vapor transport (W/m²).
*   • *G* represents the subsurface heat flow and indicates the heat stored or released underground (W/m²).
*   • Δ*S* is the change of the energy storage on the surface and usually depends on the change of the surface temperature (W/m²).

Many mathematical models have been developed on climate change and agricultural adaptation strategies. These models are used to determine the impacts of climate change on the agricultural sector and to develop adaptation strategies. Some of them are given below:

*   *Agricultural Production Systems Simulator (APSIM):* This model is used to assess the performance of agricultural production systems. APSIM takes into account many factors such as plant growth, soil water, nutrients, diseases, and pests ([Keating et al., 2003](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib20)).
*   *DSSAT (Decision Support System for Agrotechnology Transfer):* This model is used to assess climate change impacts on agricultural production. DSSAT takes into account many factors such as plant growth, soil water, nutrients, diseases, and pests ([Jones et al., 1998](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib19)).
*   *Agricultural Model Intercomparison and Improvement Project (AgMIP):* In this project, a series of mathematical models were developed to assess the impacts of climate change on the agricultural sector. These models take into account many factors such as plant growth, soil water, nutrients, diseases, and pests. AgMIP can be used to predict changes in agricultural production based on climate change scenarios ([Rosenzweig et al., 2013](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib37)).

Regional climate models (RCMs) are pivotal in providing detailed climate data for local agricultural planning. These models, focusing on smaller geographic areas, bridge the gap left by broader general circulation models (GCMs). RCMs function on complex mathematical equations based on atmospheric physics, chemistry, and dynamics, using a three-dimensional grid to simulate interactions in the atmosphere, land, and oceans. Key elements such as radiation, humidity, wind, and temperature are factored into these high-resolution models. By refining GCM data, RCMs offer accurate regional climate predictions, crucial for effective agricultural strategies and climate impact studies. Statistical and dynamic downscaling are pivotal techniques in regionalizing global climate model outputs for agricultural impact assessments ([Pal et al., 2007](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib31)). Statistical downscaling involves mathematical relationships between large-scale atmospheric variables and local climate features. This approach employs statistical methods like regression analysis, weather typing, or machine learning to infer finer-scale climatic details from coarse-resolution model outputs. Dynamic downscaling, on the other hand, uses high-resolution climate models to physically simulate the climate over a small area, taking into account local topography and land use. This method relies on the fundamental principles of fluid dynamics and thermodynamics, applying them in a high-resolution modeling framework to capture local climate variations. Both techniques are instrumental in transforming broad-scale climate projections into actionable, localized data, crucial for agricultural planning and management ([Kotamarthi et al., 2021](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib23)). Crop simulation models like APSIM and DSSAT operate on complex algorithms and mathematical equations to simulate crop growth and yield under various environmental conditions. These models integrate plant physiology, soil science, and meteorology to predict how crops respond to different factors, such as soil type, weather conditions, and agricultural management practices. They employ algorithms for water balance, nutrient cycling, and light interception, among others, to realistically represent crop growth stages and yield outcomes. These models are critical tools for understanding agricultural systems’ dynamics and assisting in decision-making for sustainable crop production ([Tsuji et al., 1998](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib38)). CGE models in agriculture are advanced economic models that analyze the interconnections within an economy, considering various sectors including agriculture. They are underpinned by mathematical equations representing economic behaviors, such as production, consumption, and trade. These models are used to assess the impact of policy changes, market developments, and external factors like climate change on the agricultural sector and the broader economy. By simulating how economies respond to different scenarios, CGE models provide valuable insights for policymakers in formulating strategies to address the challenges posed by climate change in agriculture ([Böhringer & Löschel, 2006](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib5)).

 

### 7.3.2 The role of computational modeling in analyzing the impacts of climate change on agriculture

As the consequences of climate change become increasingly evident in the agricultural sector, there is a growing urgency to understand and predict its multifaceted impacts on food production, rural livelihoods, and global food security. Computational modeling is emerging as a vital tool in this effort, enabling researchers to simulate the complex interactions between climate variables, agricultural processes, and ecological systems.

Computational models play a central role in projecting future climate scenarios based on various emission trajectories and global climate patterns. These climate projections serve as key inputs for understanding how different greenhouse gas emission pathways can influence regional and global climate patterns. By generating possible future climates, researchers can anticipate potential challenges and opportunities that agricultural systems may face.

*   • *Global Climate Models):* These models, developed using complex mathematical equations, simulate the Earth’s climate system on a global scale. Global climate models predict possible future climate scenarios based on different greenhouse gas emission scenarios, taking into account factors such as atmospheric composition, ocean currents, and land surface interactions ([Ramirez-Villegas et al., 2013](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib34)).
*   • *Downsizing Techniques:* Since Global climate models have a coarse resolution, downscaling techniques are used to downscale climate projections to regional or local scales. RCMs and statistical downscaling methods are used to provide more detailed climate information for specific agricultural regions ([Tumbo et al., 2010](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib39)).

Computational modeling facilitates the assessment of vulnerability and risk in agricultural systems, enabling researchers to identify regions and crops most susceptible to the adverse impacts of climate change. By integrating diverse data sources such as climate data, soil properties, and crop management practices, modeling vulnerability also improves understanding of the potential impacts of climate change on food production and food security ([Bilen et al., 2022](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib4)). Researchers are developing vulnerability indices and indicators using computational models to assess the sensitivity, exposure, and adaptive capacity of agricultural systems. These indicators help prioritize regions and communities that require targeted adaptation measures. Integrated assessment models combine climate, crop, and economic models to assess the impact of climate change on agricultural productivity, trade, and food prices. These models enable decision-makers to evaluate different adaptation and mitigation strategies.

Crop growth and yield models are essential tools for understanding how climate change affects agricultural productivity. These models simulate the physiological responses of crops to changing environmental conditions, providing insights into potential shifts in growing seasons, changes in crop suitability, and the overall impact on food production ([Rauff & Bello, 2015](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib35)). Process-based crop models integrate biological processes such as photosynthesis, transpiration, and nutrient uptake with environmental factors such as temperature, rainfall, and soil properties. These models allow researchers to simulate crop growth and yields under different climate scenarios. By using computational models to analyze the relationships between climate variables and crop suitability, researchers can identify regions where certain crops may become more or less viable in the future due to climate change.

Climate change can affect the distribution and dynamics of pests, diseases, and invasive species, posing additional challenges for agricultural systems. Computational models help predict the potential spread and impact of these biological threats under changing climate conditions, enabling better preparedness and response strategies ([FAO, 2021](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib12)). Species distribution models use climate data and species occurrence records to estimate the potential geographic range of pests, diseases, and invasive species. This information supports the identification of areas at risk and the development of early warning systems. Computational models of ecological networks assess how changes in climate and land use can affect species interactions in agroecosystems. Understanding these dynamics enables the prediction of potential disruptions in ecological balance and ecosystem services.

 

### 7.3.3 Advances in computational models for climate change and agriculture

As the challenges posed by climate change intensify, computational modeling plays an increasingly important role in improving understanding of the complex interactions between climate and agriculture. Over the years, significant progress has been made in improving and extending computational models to better simulate the complexity of climate change impacts on agricultural systems.

Crop-specific models have evolved as specialized tools that provide detailed information on the response of each crop to changing climatic conditions. Often based on extensive field data and knowledge of plant physiology, these models contribute to more precise predictions of crop growth and yields under climate change scenarios. Their accuracy and reliability are crucial for informed decision-making in agricultural planning and adaptation. Physiologically based crop models simulate the physiological processes of specific crops, taking into account factors such as photosynthesis, respiration, and water uptake. By incorporating crop-specific characteristics and responses, these models increase accuracy in predicting crop behavior under different climate scenarios ([Boote et al., 2016](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib6)). Advances in crop genetics and breeding have led to the development of models that take genotype–environment interactions into account. By accounting for the diversity of crop varieties, these models capture variability in crop responses to changing climatic conditions. Researchers are using ensemble modeling approaches to address the uncertainties inherent in product-specific models. Ensemble models integrate multiple product models, taking into account different assumptions and parametrizations to provide a more comprehensive range of potential outcomes ([Abramowitz et al., 2019](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib1)).

While global climate models provide valuable information on large-scale climate patterns, regional-scale models and downscaling techniques provide more detailed and localized information. Downscaling is essential to obtain climate data at spatial resolutions relevant to agricultural systems and to better understand regional climate variability and its impacts on crops. RCMs are regional-scale versions of global climate models and provide more focused climate projections for specific geographic areas. RCMs provide higher-resolution data, capturing local climate characteristics and improving the accuracy of climate impact assessments ([Chokkavarapu & Mandla, 2019](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib10)). Statistical downscaling methods use empirical relationships between large-scale climate variables derived from global climate models and local climate observations. These methods provide fine-scale climate projections that are invaluable for agricultural planning and adaptation strategies ([IPCC, 2007](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib17)).

The integration of biophysical and socioeconomic models marks a significant advance in understanding the multifaceted nature of climate change impacts on agriculture. By combining biophysical models (e.g., crop models, hydrological models) with socioeconomic models (e.g., economic models, land use models), researchers can explore the dynamic feedback and interactions between climate, agricultural productivity, and socioeconomic factors. Integrated assessment models (IAMs) are comprehensive frameworks that combine climate, agriculture, and economic models. They analyze the potential consequences of climate change on agricultural productivity, food prices, and global trade. They contribute to the assessment of adaptation and mitigation strategies under various scenarios ([Nikas et al., 2019](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib29)). Socio-ecological systems modeling takes a holistic approach that considers social, economic, and ecological dimensions. Socio-ecological systems modeling helps uncover the complex interdependencies between human societies and agroecosystems, facilitating a deeper understanding of the impacts of climate change on livelihoods and food security ([Groot & Rossing, 2011](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib14)).

Uncertainty is an inherent aspect of climate change projections and computational modeling. Uncertainty and sensitivity analysis techniques help assess the robustness and reliability of model results, increasing the credibility of modeling results for decision-makers and policymakers. Monte Carlo simulations involve creating multiple model runs with varying input parameters to capture the range of possible outcomes and quantify uncertainty. These simulations provide probabilistic projections that reflect the uncertainties associated with climate change impacts. Sensitivity analysis investigates how changes in model inputs affect the output, allowing researchers to identify the most effective parameters and improve model accuracy. This process contributes to understanding the key drivers of uncertainty and improving key model components ([Macdonald, 2002](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib26)).

 

### 7.3.4 Computational modeling case studies and applications in agriculture and climate change research

Real-world applications of computational modeling in agriculture and climate change research have provided valuable insights into the complex dynamics of this critical relationship. Case studies and practical applications of computational models play a crucial role in understanding the impacts of climate change on agricultural systems, assessing economic consequences, quantifying the carbon footprint of agricultural practices, and formulating climate-smart agricultural policies.

Regional-scale case studies provide a detailed examination of how climate change is affecting specific crops and agricultural practices in different geographic regions. By integrating local climate data, soil information, and crop-specific characteristics into computational models, researchers can assess how climate change affects crop yields, phenology, and overall agricultural productivity ([O’Brien et al., 2018](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib30)). Computational models contribute to the identification of climate hotspots—regions that are projected to experience significant changes in temperature and precipitation patterns. These studies are critical for understanding the vulnerability of specific crops and guiding targeted adaptation efforts. Computational models are used to assess changes in crop suitability and climate niches, helping to identify which crops may thrive or face challenges under different climate scenarios. This information guides decisions on crop diversification and potential shifts in agricultural practices. Case studies provide insights into climate change adaptation strategies, such as changing planting dates, crop selection, and water management practices. Understanding regional climate risks supports farmers and policymakers in implementing climate-resilient agricultural practices.

Understanding the economic impacts of climate change on agriculture is essential for sustainable land use planning, trade policies, and risk management. Computational models support the quantification of the economic consequences of climate change, from changes in crop incomes to potential impacts on regional and global food markets. Integrating economic models with climate and crop models enables researchers to analyze the economic consequences of changing agricultural productivity. Trade analysis also assesses how changes in crop production affect international food markets and food security. Computational models help researchers estimate the costs associated with implementing climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies. These assessments are crucial for understanding the economic viability of different measures and guiding policy decisions. They also facilitate the assessment of external costs associated with climate change impacts on agriculture, including health consequences, ecosystem services, and potential social disruptions.

While agriculture plays a major role in the emission of greenhouse gases, it also presents potential for carbon capture and aiding in climate change mitigation ([Lynch et al., 2021](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib25)). Computational models help quantify the carbon footprint of different agricultural practices, enabling the identification of low-carbon and climate-smart agricultural techniques.

*   • *Life Cycle Assessment (LCA)*: LCA models track carbon emissions and sequestration associated with the entire life cycle of agricultural products, from production to consumption. They, therefore, help identify emission hotspots and support efforts to reduce the carbon intensity of agricultural supply chains ([Adler et al., 2007](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib2)).
*   • *Carbon Farming Applications*: Computational models contribute to assessing the potential of carbon farming practices such as agroforestry, cover crops, and soil carbon sequestration to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and increase the resilience of agricultural systems ([Dumbrell et al., 2016](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib11)).
*   • *Policy Support for Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA)*: Measuring the carbon footprint of agricultural practices provides evidence for policymakers to develop incentives and financial mechanisms that encourage the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices ([Chandra et al., 2018](#Ch007_107-122_B9780443219054000109.xhtml#bib9)).

CSA strategies aim to increase agricultural productivity, resilience, and sustainability while reducing greenhouse gas emissions. Computational models play an important role in guiding the development of CSA plans and supporting the formulation of policies that promote climate resilience in the agricultural sector. They also contribute to prioritizing investments in CSA practices by assessing their potential impact on crop yields, farmer incomes, and environmental sustainability. By integrating climate, crop, and economic models, researchers and policymakers can assess synergies and trade-offs between different CSA practices. This approach ensures that policies are comprehensive, sustainable, and aligned with broader development goals. By helping to establish monitoring and evaluation frameworks for CSA initiatives, it enables continuous assessment of progress and adaptive management of policies as new data and insights become available.

 

### 7.3.5 Challenges and limitations of computational modeling for agriculture and climate change

While computational modeling has revolutionized the understanding of the complex relationship between agriculture and climate change, it is not without its challenges and limitations. As researchers seek to develop increasingly sophisticated models to address pressing issues such as food security and climate resilience, they face barriers related to data availability and quality, the complexity of integrating multiple factors, calibration and validation of models, as well as ethical and social implications.

Data is the foundation of any computational model, and its availability and quality are crucial for accurate and reliable predictions. Several challenges arise in the context of agriculture and climate change. For example, climate and agriculture data may not always be available at the desired spatial and temporal resolutions required by computational models. Fine-scale data can be difficult and resource-intensive to obtain, especially for regional and local studies. In some regions, historical climate data may be missing or agricultural data may be limited, hindering the development of comprehensive models. Missing or incomplete data can lead to biased model results and uncertainty in predictions. In addition, climate data such as temperature and precipitation often contain inherent uncertainties. Agricultural data, including yield records and land use information, can vary in quality and accuracy.

Agriculture and climate change encompass a large number of interlinked factors that make the development of comprehensive computational models challenging. Interactions between climate variables, soil properties, crop physiology, and socioeconomic factors occur at various spatial and temporal scales. Capturing these multiscale interactions in models requires sophisticated algorithms and large computational resources. Agriculture is linked to other sectors such as energy, water, and land use. Integrating these cross-sectoral linkages into models requires coordination across different disciplines and datasets. Climate change is inherently uncertain and future scenarios depend on various socioeconomic and policy factors. Integrating these uncertainties into models and accurately predicting future climate conditions pose significant challenges.

Ensuring the accuracy and reliability of computational models requires rigorous calibration and validation processes:

*   • *Parameterization*: Models often rely on empirical data and assumptions for parameterization, which can lead to biases and uncertainties. Calibration of model parameters with observed data is crucial, but the process is complex and time-consuming.
*   • *Model Validation*: Validation of model outputs against real-world observations is essential to assess their accuracy. However, validation is difficult due to limited data availability or discrepancies between model results and actual measurements.
*   • *Model Complexity*: As models become more complex and involve more processes and interactions, calibration and validation become more complex and require specialized expertise and resources.

The use of computational modeling in agriculture and climate change research raises ethical and social considerations. The adoption of CSA practices may have different impacts on different farming communities and social groups. Models should be designed to assess potential equity and distributional consequences. Models can influence policy decisions and resource allocation. Ensuring that models are transparent and accessible and reflect different stakeholder perspectives is critical for inclusive and equitable decision-making processes. The collection and use of data for modeling purposes raise concerns about data privacy and ownership. Protecting sensitive data, ensuring consent and data protection are key ethical considerations.

 

### 7.3.6 Future perspectives and opportunities

The future of computational modeling in agriculture and climate change research holds great promise as researchers continually strive to improve accuracy, enhance collaboration, leverage advanced technologies, and strengthen policy implications. As computational modeling techniques improve, researchers are actively working to increase the accuracy and precision of climate-agriculture models. Advances in data collection and remote sensing technologies will provide finer spatial and temporal resolutions for climate data, allowing models to more accurately capture local climate variability and its impacts on agricultural systems. Ongoing developments in process-based crop models will incorporate the latest research on plant physiology, genetics, and agronomy. These refined models will provide more robust predictions of crop behavior under different climate scenarios. Modeling approaches that combine multiple models and scenarios will help reduce uncertainties and increase the reliability of model predictions. Leveraging different modeling methodologies will lead to more comprehensive and holistic insights.

The success of computational modeling in addressing agriculture and climate change challenges depends on fostering collaboration between researchers, policymakers, farmers, and other stakeholders. Involving stakeholders, including farmers and local communities, in the modeling process will enrich model development and ensure that models reflect real-world complexities and priorities. Joint efforts to codesign climate adaptation strategies will result in more effective and context-specific solutions by aligning policy recommendations with the needs and aspirations of different communities. Establishing interactive platforms for knowledge exchange and dialog will facilitate continuous learning, foster interdisciplinary cooperation, and ensure a common understanding of the impacts of climate change on agriculture.

The integration of big data and artificial intelligence will revolutionize computational modeling and its applications in agriculture and climate research. Big data analytics and artificial intelligence (AI) techniques will enable the development of data-driven models that improve the accuracy and relevance of model outputs by leveraging large datasets from multiple sources. AI-powered machine learning algorithms can analyze complex climate models and historical data to develop more accurate climate predictions and strengthen the basis of computational models. AI-powered precision agriculture tools will optimize resource use and support intelligent decision-making on planting, irrigation, and pest management, leading to more efficient and sustainable agricultural practices.

To bridge the gap between research and policy implementation, computational models can play an important role in developing effective decision-support systems and policy implications. They will be designed to produce outputs that are directly relevant to policy formulation and decision-making processes. These outputs will provide policymakers with actionable insights for CSA planning. Models will be integrated into existing policy frameworks and planning processes, facilitating the translation of research findings into concrete policy measures. Decision-support systems will include real-time monitoring of climate conditions and agricultural performance, enabling dynamic adaptation to changing conditions and climate trends.

 

## 7.4 Conclusion

Computational modeling plays a crucial and transformative role in addressing the complex dynamics between agriculture and climate change. In this study, we describe the importance of computational models in understanding the impact of climate change on agricultural systems, analyzing adaptation strategies, and formulating climate-resilient policies.

Computational modeling has emerged as an indispensable tool in advancing knowledge of the complex interactions between agriculture and climate change. By simulating future climate scenarios, assessing vulnerability, and predicting crop growth and yields under changing conditions, computational models have provided valuable insights into the challenges and opportunities presented by climate change in agriculture. Through regional case studies, economic analyses, and carbon footprint assessments, these models have supported evidence-based decision-making, paving the way for sustainable agricultural practices and climate-resilient policies. Moreover, the integration of big data and artificial intelligence offers new horizons for greater accuracy and precision in modeling, increasing collaboration between researchers and stakeholders and advancing CSA strategies.

Research findings from computational modeling studies have the potential to have a profound impact on global agriculture and climate change mitigation efforts. These findings can inform policymakers, agriculture stakeholders, and international organizations to drive the adoption of climate-smart practices, improve food security, and support rural livelihoods. Future directions of computational modeling point to continuous improvement in model accuracy, deeper collaboration among various stakeholders, and strategic use of advanced technologies for data-driven insights. As models become more sophisticated and cover broader aspects of climate and agriculture, their applicability in shaping sustainable policies and practices will increase.

In the face of climate change, addressing its impacts on agriculture requires urgent and concerted action. Computational modeling provides a valuable toolkit to understand and navigate this critical relationship. Governments, institutions, and organizations should prioritize research funding for computational modeling studies that focus on climate–agriculture interactions and enable the development of more accurate and comprehensive models. Researchers, policymakers, farmers, and communities should collaborate closely to codesign climate adaptation strategies, ensuring that models reflect local realities and prioritize equitable and inclusive approaches. Research insights from computational models should be translated into actionable policies, with decision support systems assisting policymakers in implementing climate-resilient agricultural practices. Harnessing the potential of big data, artificial intelligence, and other emerging technologies will drive innovation in computational modeling and revolutionize its application in agriculture and climate change research. Civil society, nongovernmental organizations, and advocacy groups play a vital role in raising awareness of the impact of climate change on agriculture and promoting sustainable agricultural practices. By embracing these actions together, all sectors of society can build a future where agriculture is resilient to climate change, global food security is protected and rural communities thrive in the face of environmental challenges.

 8 

# The energy from PM2.5 for better climate: a case of sustainable development

Prisilla Jayanthi Gandam^([1](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#aff1)) and Utku Kose^([2](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#aff2),) ^([3](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#aff3)),    ¹CMR Institute of Technology, Hyderabad, Telangana, India,    ²Department of Computer Engineering, Faculty of Engineering, Süleyman Demirel University, Isparta, Turkey,    ³UNIVERSITY OF NORTH DAKOTA, GRAND FORKS, ND, UNITED STATES

 

## Abstract

Climate change significantly contributes to air pollution, affecting human health and livelihood. In this study of air pollution for three states, namely, Andhra Pradesh, Delhi, and Rajasthan, the GLM regression model was implemented. The model seems very promising with the results obtained. The *z*\-score value was found to be PM2.5 (0.20) with 95% Confidential Interval (CI) (−0.4414 to 0.5406) for Visakhapatnam (V), and for Nehru Nagar (NN), PM2.5 (5.31) with CI (0.3279–0.7109), and Shastri Nagar (SN) had PM2.5 (0.10) with CI (−0.5077 to 0.5608). Moreover, the least AIC and BIC were observed from the GLM model with rainy data, where AIC for V (6.5737), NN (6.9374), and SN (6.4447); similarly, BIC for V (−88.9989), NN (−61.1624), and SN (−65.1675) is a better model. The model proved to be a statistically significant fit. PM2.5 is highly detrimental to human health and carcinogenic, causing cancer and other respiratory problems. For reducing pollutant emissions in view of attaining zero waste and zero carbon, waste-to-energy technology has found a solution to capture PM2.5 carbon nanoparticles and use them as electrodes in supercapacitors.

 

### Keywords

Climate change; PM2.5; sustainable development; nanotechnology; zero carbon

 

## 8.1 Introduction

Air pollution is a global concern that affects climate change (CC) and human health ([Manisalidis et al., 2020](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib21)). According to [WHO (2022)](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib41) ([https://trackingsdg7.esmap.org/](https://trackingsdg7.esmap.org/)), 6.7 million premature deaths were reported due to air pollution. Air pollution-related problems cannot be fixed in isolation by a single nation; what was once a national issue is now a global concern. The pollution from the construction site kills plants and destroys the crops and their yield. This pollution is hazardous to human life in various phases. Industrial pollution contaminates the surfaces of water and soil. The improvement in air quality helps in improving soil quality, crop yields, and water quality, which are closely associated with food security. A few sources of pollution to name are household combustion devices, motor cars, and industrial waste. This pollution can be indoor or outdoor environmental pollution caused by any chemical, physical, or biological agent that disturbs the atmosphere. The pollutants increase due to several factors, like the dryness of the zone, wind, and precipitation causing CC ([Iyyanki & Jayanthi, 2020](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib13)). Major air pollutants consist of CO, NH[3], NO, NO[2], O[3], PM, and SO[2]. The concentration of PM2.5 peaked in densely populated areas that are revolutionizing with industrialization and urbanization ([Ji et al., 2018](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib15)). PM2.5 penetrates easily deep into the lungs and damages the respiratory system ([Xing et al., 2016](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib35)). PM causes several diseases affecting children ([Iyyanki et al., 2018](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib12)) and elderly people suffering from breathing problems. PM is riskier than any other pollutants causing allergies.

In this case study of air pollutants, the states, namely Andhra Pradesh (AP), are moderate states, and Rajasthan (RJ) is a dry state. Delhi has peak summers, and cold winters with moderate rains are considered. Delhi is known as a highly polluted city in India; the reason behind this study and statistical comparative study is carried out between three states. During rains, air quality is very good, washing away the pollution/pollutants in the atmosphere. Minimizing the pollutants in these states can improve and benefit the state’s residents. Minimizing pollution is neither one man’s work nor one state’s work; it is a collaboration of many sectors involved in polluting the atmosphere. The contribution from these sectors helps society, small and large, to develop, improve, and attain zero net carbon.

Waste management has taken several measures, and many possibilities have been implemented to improve the waste of energy. Converting waste to useful energy sources helps the nation and indirectly attains zero net carbon goals. Capturing PM2.5 carbon nanoparticles and using them as electrodes in supercapacitors helps eliminate or use PM2.5. The fabric filters are used to collect PM emissions in ANDRITZ and eliminate PMs from vehicle exhaust fumes with a self-powered triboelectric filter.

In this world of polluted atmospheres, one must look for suitable habitats, live and work in them, and be healthy and safe ([Fig. 8–1](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0010)). Airborne contaminants such as PM2.5, PM10, and CO are harmful to human breathing, leading to various diseases like heart and lung diseases, cancer, and respiratory diseases. According to the pollution status, the city of Zurich, Switzerland (0.49 µg/m³), ranks as the least polluted and has the cleanest air in the world. The of city Bhiwadi, RJ, had the highest levels of PM (92.7) and ranks as the third most polluted city in the world.

## 8.2 Contaminants through particulate matter

PM2.5 and PM10 are produced by activities that emit GHG gases responsible for global warming. The PM10 level in ambient air was found to be associated with a lung function deficit ([Siddique et al., 2010](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib28)). These fine particulates result in heart and lung diseases, lung cancer, asthma ([http://www.npi.gov.au/npidata/action/load/summary-result, n.d](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib44).), and breathing congestion problems. The fine dust emitted by various sources falling under PM10 and PM2.5 is listed in [Table 8–1](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#t0010) ([https://www.dcceew.gov.au/environment/protection/npi/substances/fact-sheets/particulate-matter-pm10-and-pm25, n.d](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib43).). PM2.5 is the most concerning and hazardous pollutant in Australia, which is discussed in the next section.

## 8.3 Nitrogen di oxide pollution—childhood asthma

[Takenoue et al. (2012)](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib30) conducted a study on the relationship between NO[2] exposure and childhood asthma. Nitrogen dioxide (NO[2]) is an unescapable air pollutant and the largest emitter of air pollution related to mortality. NO[2] is largely an urban pollutant associated with pediatric health issues, including asthma, particularly in Asia and Africa. During 2011 and 2012, in China, NO[2] emissions reached their peak. In India, parts of the Middle East and Eastern Europe have shown an increase in NO[2] emissions quantities ([Anenberg et al., 2022](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib2)). In another case study of NO[2], it was proven that gas cooking produces NO[2] and other pollutants, such as ultrafine particles. The study also reveals that children living inside a house with cooking gas pollution will have a 42% increased risk of developing asthma. Especially in children, exposure to indoor NO[2] increases the risk of acute and chronic respiratory illnesses, reducing lung function and exacerbating asthma. According to [Lin et al. (2013)](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib20), children spend the majority of their time indoors. Childhood exposures to outdoor NO[2], PM2.5, and black carbon (BC) contribute to childhood asthma development ([Khreis et al., 2019](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib16)). Severe health diseases are caused by exposure to long-term NO[2]. Chronic exposure significantly contributes to the high COVID-19 fatality rates ([Ogen, 2020](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib25)).

 

## 8.4 Carbon monoxide

The development of an arrhythmia ([Rose et al., 2019](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib27)) and continuously elevated mortality risk ([Chen et al., 2021](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib5)) are seen with the increasing CO concentrations. An increase of 1 mg/m³ CO concentrations (0–3 days) was associated with diseases such as cardiovascular, respiratory, and so on ([Wang et al., 2021](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib33)). In UK homes, long-term exposure to CO focuses on the impacts on human health associated with the pollution of gas appliances. Chronic CO exposure produces COHb levels of 10% or above, leading to hemodynamic effects including polycythemia ([Tam et al., 2012](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib31)).

 

## 8.5 Climate change—global warming

CC is a challenging global issue. CC is caused by major emissions of pollutants. CC disturbs the social and environmental factors of health—breathing clean air, drinking safe water, food, and shelter. BC, a component of fine PM, is one of the major contributors to global warming after CO[2]. BC warms the earth’s atmosphere by absorbing sunlight, accelerating the melting of snow and ice. The increase in PM2.5 concentrations increases CC and vice versa ([Park et al., 2020](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib26)) and thereby would be the greatest global health threat ([Huang et al., 2021](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib11)). CC has a greater and more significant impact on PM2.5 air quality. Several research works have examined CC effects on PM2.5 using models of chemical transport driven by general circulation models ([Amos et al., 2010](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib1)). The study proved that the impact of CC on air pollution varies considerably from zone to zone and season to season depending on the PM2.5 component. It includes seven components of PM2.5, namely ammonium, elemental carbon, nitrate, organic carbon, silicon, sodium, and sulfate ([Weeberb et al., 2019](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib34)). CC controls PM2.5 concentration by adjusting the atmospheric circulation, temperature, and hydrological cycle ([Park et al., 2020](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib26)).

 

## 8.6 Generalized linear model—air pollution in India

A case study for the impact of rainfall on air pollutants in three states of India, AP, Delhi, and RJ, is discussed. The air pollutant data was obtained from [https://app.cpcbccr.com/AQI\_India/](https://app.cpcbccr.com/AQI_India/) and a machine learning algorithm and generalized linear model were applied for analysis. GLM is a regression model that has transformed the conditional expectation of ***y*** into a linear combination of regression variables ***X***. A link function *g*(.) is the transformation function of GLM.

$g\\left( \\pi\_{i} \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 0}^{p}{\\beta\_{j}.}x\_{\\mathit{ij}}$<mml:math><mml:mi>g</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#960;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">ij</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math> (8.1)

![Equation](images/F000043si1.png)(8.1) 

*π*[*i*]\=*E*(*y*\=*y*[*i*]|*X*\=*x*[*i*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#960;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>E</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="|" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math> (8.2)

![Equation](images/F000043si2.png)(8.2) 

The data is collected for three different seasons, namely, rainy (July 2022), winter (November and December 2022), and summer (April and May 2022). The results of GLM for four pollutants, namely PM2.5, PM10, CO, and NO[2], for Visakhapatnam (AP), Nehru Nagar (Delhi), and Shastri Nagar (RJ), are shown in [Table 8–3](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#t0020) through [Tables 8–4](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#t0025) and [8–5](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#t0030). The results clearly show that the *z*\-score value for the rainy season dataset is comparatively lower than the other dataset:

1.  1. Visakhapatnam (V) obtained PM2.5 (0.20) with a 95% Confidential Interval (CI) (−0.4414 to 0.5406), PM10 (9.60) CI=0.7728–1.1644, CO (0.05) CI=−0.4869 to 0.5123, and NO[2] (0.11) CI=−0.8367 to 0.9344.
2.  2. Nehru Nagar (NN) had PM2.5 (0.5194) with CI=0.3279–0.7109, PM10 (0.6111) CI=0.2508–0.9714, CO (−0.1605) CI=−0.8940 to 0.5728, and NO[2] (0.1117) CI=−0.2382 to 0.4617.
3.  3. Shastri Nagar (SN) has PM2.5 (0.10) CI=−0.5077 to 0.5608, PM10 (4.09) CI=0.4155–1.1806, CO (0.09) CI=−0.4743 to 0.5214, and NO[2] (1.90) CI=−0.0188 to 1.2593.

[Tables 8–6](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#t0035) through [Table 8–8](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#t0045) represent the readings obtained for the Akaike information criterion (AIC) and Bayesian information criterion (BIC) from GLM models with data for summer (April and May 2022), rainy (July 2022), and winter (November and December 2022). The values of AIC and BIC are considered to compare the regression models. The lower the values of AIC and BIC, the better the fit of the model. Let us understand what the values provide. AIC gives an evaluation of a constant and the relative distance of the likelihood function between the unknown true (data) and the model fit, whereas BIC provides an evaluation of the posterior probability of a model being true. AIC and BIC for the GLM regression model with the rainy data are comparatively lesser than the other data models. The least AIC was found for V (6.5737), NN (6.9374), and SN (6.4447); similarly, the BIC for V (−88.9989), NN (−61.1624), and SN (−65.1675) is determined to be a better model fit over other models. In [Tables 8–6](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#t0035) through [Table 8–8](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#t0045), the variance function (Var.) and link function (Lin.) are represented as *V*(*u*)=*u* and *g*(*u*)=*u*, respectively.

### 8.6.1 Hourly trends of air pollutants in Visakhapatnam

The hourly data for Visakhapatnam and Shastri Nagar was collected for six consecutive days in April, July, December 2022, and January 2023. The trends clearly show low pollution during the rainy season ([Jayanthi & Muralikrishna, 2022](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib14)). The *x*\-axis and *y*\-axis represent the number of hours and the pollutant quantity, respectively.

In the data collected to measure the air quality index (AQI), there are multiple independent variables (monthly graphs) such as PM2.5, PM10, CO, NO[2], and a dependent variable (AQI); hence, it is called a multivariate time series. In multivariate time series, trend graphs have more than one time series variable. Each variable does not depend on its historical data but depends on other variables.

The equation is given by:

 *Z*[1](*t*) = *a*[1] + *w*[1]10 \* *y*[1](*t*−1) + *w*[1]11 \* *y*[2](*t*−1) + *e*[1](*t*−1)<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mspace width="1em"/><mml:mi>Z</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width=".1em"/></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>a</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mspace width=".1em"/><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mn>10</mml:mn><mml:mo>\*</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mn>11</mml:mn><mml:mo>\*</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>e</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math> (8.3)

![Equation](images/F000043si3.png)(8.3) 

 *Z*[2](*t*) = *a*[2] + *w*[1]20 \* *y*[1](*t*−1) + *w*[1]21 \* *y*[2](*t*−1) + *e*[1](*t*−1)<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mspace width="1em"/><mml:mi>Z</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mspace width=".1em"/></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>a</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mspace width=".1em"/><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mn>20</mml:mn><mml:mo>\*</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mn>21</mml:mn><mml:mo>\*</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>e</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math> (8.4)

![Equation](images/F000043si4.png)(8.4) 

here,

*   *a*[1] and *a*[2] are the constant terms,
*   *w*[10], *w*[11], *w*[20], and *w*[21] are the coefficients,
*   *e*[1] and *e*[2] are the error terms

Similarly, for univariate trend graphs (hourly graphs), single dependent and independent variables, the linear regression equation is given as:

*a* = *m**b* + *c*<mml:math><mml:mi>a</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">mb</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:math> (8.5)

![Equation](images/F000043si5.png)(8.5) 

here,

*   *a*\=independent variable
*   *b*\=dependent variable
*   *m*\=slope
*   *c*\=*y*\-intercept

 

1.  A. April month  
    For Visakhapatnam, in April ([Fig. 8–2A](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0015)), the observation was made from April 25 to 30, 2022. On April 27, PM2.5 was the minimum with a range of 39–42; similarly, CO was the minimum with a range of 20–34 on April 29; NO[2] was the minimum with a range of 38–43 on April 30; and PM10 was 95–106 on April 28.
2.  B. July month 2022  
    In July ([Fig. 8–2B](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0015)), the readings were considered from July 20 to 25, 2022. On July 24, both PM2.5 (40–46) and NO[2] (39–40) were found to have the minimum range; similarly, on July 25, both CO (11–23) and PM10 (79–87) were found to be minimum.
3.  C. December month 2022  
    In December 2022 ([Fig. 8–2C](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0015)), the observation was gathered from December 1 to 6, 2022. On December 2, both PM2.5 (114–119) and PM10 (110–113) had minimum values. Similarly, on December 4, the minimum range of CO (16–45) and NO[2] (30–35) was noted.
4.  D. January month 2023  
    The observations considered in this month were from January 1 to 6, 2023 ([Fig. 8–2D](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0015)). On January 3, PM2.5 was minimum within the range of 73–93; similarly, CO was minimum within the range of 19–31 on January 1. On January 2, both NO[2] (28–33) and PM10 (86–110) were at their minimum.

![Figure 8–2 (A) April month air pollutant trends. (B) July month air pollutant trends. (C) December month air pollutant trends. (D) January month air pollutant trends.](images/F000043f08-02a-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 8–2 (A) April month air pollutant trends. (B) July month air pollutant trends. (C) December month air pollutant trends. (D) January month air pollutant trends.

 

### 8.6.2 Air quality index hourly trends: April, July, December 2022, and January 2023—Visakhapatnam

The trends of AQI show the increase and decrease of the quality of air in the atmosphere in Visakhapatnam ([Fig. 8–3](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0020)) for April, July, December, and January. On July 25, the minimum AQI was 79–87 and the highest AQI was 308–314 on December 6, 2022, based on the reading considered for this study.

![Figure 8–3 AQI trends for Visakhapatnam.](images/F000043f08-03a-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 8–3 AQI trends for Visakhapatnam.

 

### 8.6.3 Monthly trends of air pollutants in Visakhapatnam for April, July, and December 2022

The monthly trends ([Fig. 8–4](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0025)) represent minimum CO (July), NO[2] (July), PM2.5 (April), and PM10 (July), and the highest PM2.5 in December. The analysis proves that during the rainy season, that is, July ([Fig. 8–3](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0020)), the pollutants are found to be minimal with better air quality. The rain washes the pollutants from the atmosphere, keeping the atmosphere clean and with fresh air quality. Rain helps to increase the air quality ([Fig. 8–15](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0080)). This process is known as wet deposition.

![Figure 8–4 Monthly trends for Visakhapatnam.](images/F000043f08-04-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 8–4](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#Bf0025) Monthly trends for Visakhapatnam.

![Figure 8–15 Average rainfall for two states: AP and RJ.](images/F000043f08-15-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 8–15 Average rainfall for two states: AP and RJ.

 

### 8.6.4 Monthly trends of air quality index in Visakhapatnam

[Fig. 8–5](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0030) represents the monthly trends with dates on the *x*\-axis and AQI on the *y*\-axis for the three corresponding seasons. It is clearly evident that AQI ranges from 40 to 119 for July, April and May ranges between 34 and 151, and in November and December, AQI ranges from 73 to 294.

![Figure 8–5 Monthly trends of AQI for Visakhapatnam.](images/F000043f08-05-9780443219054.jpg)
