2.  B. July month 2022  
    In July ([Fig. 8–6B](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0035)), the minimum CO (31–40) was observed on July 21. Similarly, on July 22, PM2.5 (36–41) and PM10 (42–49) on July 25 were minimums, and NO[2] (40–41) on July 23 and 24.
3.  C. December month 2022  
    In December ([Fig. 8–6C](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0035)), Shastri Nagar had a minimum PM10 (152–162) on December 4. Similarly, on December 6, both PM2.5 (127–134) and CO (20–53) were found to be at their minimum, and NO[2] (39–48) on December 1.
4.  D. January month 2023  
    In January ([Fig. 8–6D](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0035)), Shastri Nagar had a minimum CO (17–26) on January 1. Similarly, on January 2, both PM2.5 (91–109) and PM10 (111–129) were found to be at their minimum, and NO[2] (42–49) on January 3.

## 8.7 The need for an air quality index

AQI helps people know how the local air quality impacts one’s health. In the USA, every year, it is estimated that bad-quality air is responsible for 100,000 premature deaths. In India, the estimated deaths are calculated at 670,000 deaths annually. Whenever one breathes air pollutants, they enter the bloodstream and contribute to coughing and itchy eyes, worsening many breathing problems that can lead to cancer and premature deaths as well. Air pollution causes diseases related to the respiratory and cardiovascular systems. Hence, AQI helps to assess and compare short- and long-term diseases at any location. Pollutants like smoke or haze can reduce visibility in the atmosphere. AQI values around or below 100 are generally good or satisfactory ([Table 8–9](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#t0050)). [Table 8–9](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#t0050) represents the comparative values of PM2.5 and corresponding AQI on April 15 for the years 2019, 2020, 2022, and 2023 for three different states: AP (Visakhapatnam), RJ (Shastri Nagar), and Delhi (Nehru Nagar). From the readings, one can observe that PM2.5 and AQI are increasing in NN, whereas SN had the highest in 2021, but for Visakhapatnam, there was a drastic rise in AQI and PM 2.5 values. The graph ([Fig. 8–14](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0075)) represents the variation of the values.

![Figure 8–14 PM2.5 versus AQI for NN, SN and Visakhapatnam.](images/F000043f08-14-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 8–14](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#Bf0075) PM2.5 versus AQI for NN, SN and Visakhapatnam.

The winter season increases the pollution in the air with the use of more personal vehicles. Moreover, the use of firewood and fireplaces to keep the rooms’ inside/outside warm leads to an increase in pollution. In winter, the air pollution remains in the atmosphere for much longer and is breathed at a higher rate than during the summer. As in the rainy season ([Fig. 8–15](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0080)), air quality is good. The choropleth state maps in [Fig. 8–15](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0080) are produced by QGIS software. Due to a lack of data from Delhi state rainfall, the authors would not map. Here, one can see at a glance that the rainfall was high in 2009 and decreased in the upcoming years (2019 and 2022). The cause is the increased pollution in the atmosphere, thereby increasing global warming. Also, one can observe good air quality in snowy places. Snow reduces the pollutants in the air by absorbing pollutants into it and improving the quality of the air in the atmosphere.

 

## 8.8 Waste to energy—recycle of PM2.5

The waste industries today are in every corner and in every heart of the circular economy. Industry 4.0 involves different technologies working toward waste management that have different phases: waste generation, storage, collection, waste disposal, and waste treatment ([ISWA \[2021–2030\], n.d](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib39).). However, industry 5.0 involves 6R: recognize, reconsider, realize, reduce, reuse, and recycle, and the principle of logistics efficiency design. The four R’s—repair, reduce, reuse, and recycle—are essential to thinking about zero waste. Recycling is one of the significant outcomes of industrial upcycling, which involves process waste; empty moving transport vehicles on the roads come under process waste ([Demir & Cicibaş, 2019](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib6)).

Industry 5.0 aims to be human-centric with the development of assistive technology and provide a move toward environmental goals ([Turner et al., 2022](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib32)). CC has negative impacts on waste generation. However, waste treatment and disposal may have both positive and negative climate impacts. Hence, increasing activities of waste management reduce GHG emissions ([Waste and Climate Change: Global trends and strategy framework. United Nations Environmental Programme Division of Technology, n.d](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib42).). Waste is not an issue but is a part of the solution and is a significant source of renewable energy; the energy value can be utilized through thermal processes such as incineration and industrial co-combustion. Waste hierarchy ([Waste and Climate Change: Global trends and strategy framework. United Nations Environmental Programme Division of Technology, n.d](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib42).) includes waste prevention, reuse, recycling, waste to energy, and land fill, where energy waste occurs ([Fig. 8–16](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0085)). PM2.5 recycling is discussed in this study.

![Figure 8–16 Waste to energy.](images/F000043f08-16-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 8–16](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#Bf0085) Waste to energy.

Disposal of e-waste and recycling activities emits PM ([Bungadaeng et al., 2019](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib3)) and polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) ([Gu et al., 2010](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib8)), which are exposed to workers and nearby residents, causing various diseases. According to the study by [Zheng et al. (2016)](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib36), PM2.5 consists of carcinogenic and noncarcinogenic elements and PAH that are at higher risk for human health, especially children than adults. Recycling of e-waste, emission controls, and protective measures for workers are not available or informed at an early stage ([Kwarteng et al., 2022](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib19)).

ANDRITZ fabric filters are used to collect PM emissions, ash recirculation, and the absorption of partially gaseous pollutants. A wet electrostatic precipitator is used to accumulate submicron particulate and aerosols (PM10) from gas streams ([Clean air for better tomorrow. Air Pollution Control, n.d](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib38).).

In heavy diesel vehicles, diesel particulate filters are used, but no particulate filter is found in most fuel vehicles or light-duty automobiles due to their expensive cost. [Han et al. (2015)](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib10) have proposed a self-powered triboelectric filter for eliminating PMs from vehicle exhaust fumes with the effect of triboelectrification. The triboelectric filter has a very huge collection capacity with an efficiency of PM2.5 (~95.5%) using the self-vibration of the tailpipe.

 

### 8.8.1 Waste to value energy conversion: PM2.5 for electrodes

From a diesel vehicle, with an air metric MV portable sampler attached to the engine exhaust, PM2.5 samples were produced and gathered. During the observation study, for every hour of collection, PM2.5 (4–5 mg) samples were gathered ([Fig. 8–17](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#f0090)) from filter membranes (PTFE) in an air sampler. Then, later at 900°C, annealing PM2.5 samples for one hour under the flow of an Ar (100*s**c**c**m*)<mml:math><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mn>100</mml:mn><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">sccm</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000043si6.png), and sequentially cleaned with 5 *w**t*.%<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">wt</mml:mi><mml:mo>.</mml:mo><mml:mo>%</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000043si7.png) HF, HCl (1.0*M*),<mml:math><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1.0</mml:mn><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">M</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000043si8.png) deionized water, ethanol, and finally at 110°C, dried throughout the night. After annealing and purification, the left-out elements are mostly carbon and a minor quantity of O[2] and N (doping elements). XPS analyses the chemical states and atomic ratios of N, O, and C elements present in the *P**M* − *C**N**P**s*.<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">CNPs</mml:mi><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000043si9.png) Supercapacitors, with an outstanding storage capacity of charging with a specific capacitance $\\left( 134.4\\frac{F}{g}{in} 6.0M{KOH} \\right)$<mml:math><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mn>134.4</mml:mn><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mi>F</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:mspace width=".20em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">in</mml:mi><mml:mspace width=".20em"/><mml:mn>6.0</mml:mn><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">M</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">KOH</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000043si10.png) at a current density $\\left( 0.1\\ \\frac{A}{g} \\right)$<mml:math><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mi>A</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000043si11.png) and good stability over 5000 cycles found in  *P**M* − *C**N**P*<mml:math><mml:mspace width="1em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">CNP</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000043si12.png). *P**M* − *C**N**P**s*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">CNPs</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000043si13.png) have a unique structure and composition, which are excellent electrode materials for supercapacitors. Finally, the overall outcomes proved the development of an effective strategy for the recycling of high-performance carbon material from PM2.5 aerosols. Hence, the *P**M* − *C**N**P**s*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">CNPs</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000043si14.png) offer an innovative electrode material with greater possibility in energy conversion and storage, combined with higher penetrability, electrochemical activity, and stability ([Zhu et al., 2017](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib37)).

![Figure 8–17 PM2.5 for supercapacitors (Zhu et al., 2017).](images/F000043f08-17-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 8–17](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#Bf0090) PM2.5 for supercapacitors ([Zhu et al., 2017](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib37)).

 

## 8.9 Nanotechnology toward waste to energy

The sustainability of the environment is concerned with global CC and pollution, be it water, soil, or air. In this chapter, the study focuses on the air pollutant, that is, PM2.5, and its impact on human health ([Sun, 2019](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib29)). Let us discuss more in detail how environmental contamination can be resolved using a technology-based solution. One such solution is the innovative nanotechnology that provides the ability to detect, perceive, and treat the contamination concentration in the air. The largest contamination confronted by the world is environmental contamination with organic and inorganic compounds. Air, water, and soil are mainly affected by lots of dangerous waste from the revolution of industrialization. Nanomaterials are outstanding adsorbents, catalysts, and sensors. Also, the nanomaterials have unique properties—physicochemical, superficial, and optical electronic—that resolve difficult issues that cannot be treated with conventional technologies. Environmental pollution is harmful to humans when one inhales, ingests, or absorbs it ([Mohamed, 2017](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib23)).

 

### 8.9.1 Nanotechnology in plastic recycle

The low cost and durability of plastic have occupied the main place in humans’ lives, be it everyday household products, industrial packing material, hospitals, and so on. Plastics do not undergo biodegradation but rather form microplastics that enter oceans, causing water pollution and indirectly air and environmental pollution. The water smells foul and spreads into the air, polluting the nearby residential areas. These microplastics are found in drinking water, cooking salt, and other food products (enter the live fish) that cause harm to human life. Nanoadditive use improves mechanical and optical properties of recycled materials and nanocatalyst development for plastic depolymerization. Nanoparticles act as catalysts for plastic depolymerization.

The nanoadditives are of two types: organic and inorganic materials. Organic material consist of carbon-derived components such as graphene, carbon nanotubes (CNT), and nanocellulose. However, in inorganic nanoadditives, metal oxide materials, that is, ZnO, TiO[2], and SiO[2] nanoparticles, are present in the recycling of poly(ethylene terephthalate) and polypropylene as inorganic nanoreinforcements to improve the mechanical properties ([Distaso, 2020](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib7)). Hence, these nanoparticles help the recycling of plastic to be used in a better way and save environmental pollution by reducing GHG and, overall, saving energy. Here, the waste is reused to save energy.

 

### 8.9.2 Nanotechnology: a unique solution for air pollution

The unique reactive properties, that is, adsorbents, catalysts, and sensors of nanoparticles, play a promising approach to overcoming air pollution abatement ([Kumar et al., 2022](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib18)).

 

#### 8.9.2.1 Catalyst

Nanostructured catalyst material has a special property for air pollution control applications. Nanospheres, nanoplates, nanotubes, and nanoaerogels are present in this catalyst material; it has inherent performance and excels in physiochemical stability ([Nasrollahzadeh et al., 2020](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib24)). Certain catalysts remove volatile organic compounds from industrial smoke outlets, which pollute the environment. The catalyst is known as a nanofiber catalyst, produced from manganese oxide. Researchers from the University of Queensland experimented with a nanomaterial named CNT used for collecting GHG emissions caused by mining coal and power generation. This CNT traps gases 100 times faster than other techniques. Further, a magnetic nanocatalyst ((*C**H*[3])[3]−*N**H**C*−*P**d*@*F**e*[3]*O*[4])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">CH</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>3</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>3</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">NHC</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Pd@</mml:mi><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Fe</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>3</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">O</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>4</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000043si15.png) is an outstanding catalyst for decreasing toxic environmental pollutants. At room temperature, [Manjunatha et al. (2021)](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib22) suggested the magnetic nanocatalyst reduces the toxic 4-nitrophenol, hexavalent chromium, methylene blue, and methyl orange in a water-based solution. The purpose of nanocatalysts with an increased surface area is to speed up chemical reactions that convert harmful waste vapors from cars and industrial plants to useful gas ([Turning to Nanotechnology for Pollution Control: Applications of Nanoparticles, 2009](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib45)) ([https://sites.dartmouth.edu/](https://sites.dartmouth.edu/)). Moreover, by storing the oxygen, nanocatalysts support complete fuel combustion, thereby reducing the emission of GHG ([Chausali et al., 2023](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib4)).

 

#### 8.9.2.2 Nanosensors

The efficiency of nanosensors in detecting toxic gases such as NO[2], sulfur dioxide, and hydrogen sulfide is appreciable ([Manjunatha et al., 2021](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib22)).

 

#### 8.9.2.3 Nanobioremediation

Nanosized particles have a large surface area, thus improving chemical and biological reactivity. Nanobioremediation reduces the environmental contamination concentration to low levels. Nanotech processes and applications contribute to the environment with elimination protection by saving raw materials, energy, and water and reducing GHG and hazardous wastes ([Haleema et al., 2022](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib9)).

 

## 8.10 Photoelectrochemical: carbon dioxide

Photoelectrochemical (PEC) CO[2] conversion is an artificial photosynthesis approach and the best approach to converting CO[2] into selective gaseous and liquid products under solar light irradiation. During PEC CO[2] conversion, the endothermic reactions are determined by electricity and light irradiation. Similarly, the photogenerated electrons’ energy levels and holes have to be higher than the overpotential of *H*[2]*O*/*O*[2]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">H</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">O</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">O</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000043si16.png) and lower than the overpotential of *C**O*[2]/*H**C**O**O**H*<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">CO</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">HCOOH</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000043si17.png) for feasible CO[2] conversion. The outcome of CO[2] conversion is selected by the choices of the materials involved in electrodes and electrolytes ([Kumaravel et al., 2020](#Ch008_125-155_B9780443219054000043.xhtml#bib17)).

 

## 8.11 Conclusion

The air pollution and AQI were studied in three states, AP, Delhi, and RJ. The GLM regression model was applied to the dataset. It was observed that the *z*\-score values of PM2.5 and PM10 were high in the winter over the rainy season due to various factors of emissions. Air quality was much better in these states during the rainy season. PM2.5 emissions are an increased risk for the human respiratory system, causing cancer. However, waste-to-energy technologies proved air pollutants, such as PM2.5, that are highly human-risk can be converted to useful energy. Captured PM2.5 can be used for electrodes in supercapacitors.

 Chapter 9 

# Development of deep learning models for climate change within python framework

Hemaraju Pollayi^([1](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#aff1)), Praveena Rao^([2](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#aff2)), Dathathreya Chakali^([1](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#aff1)) and Pratyusha Bandaru^([1](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#aff1)),    ¹Department of Civil Engineering, GITAM University, Hyderabad, Telangana, India,    ²Department of Civil Engineering, Institute of Aeronautical Engineering, Hyderabad, Telangana, India

 

## Abstract

Climate crisis is the risk posed by global warming to planet and humanity, and therefore it is essential to develop deep learning (DL) models for mitigating climate change. Even the most advanced computers are unable to accurately replicate the planet’s climate due to its complexity. The rise in global temperature, a long-term warming of the planet, is owing to the emissions from flaming fossil fuels like coal, oil, and gas. The global and regional climatic models are developed based on the recurrent neural networks (RNNs) considering the real-life data which includes topography, land cover, and soil types These global models are helpful for comprehending how human behavior affects the entire planet. The geographical models, usually a few tens of kilometers, are zooming in on specific areas with finer resolutions and better suited to study the effects on agriculture, diseases, and specific ecosystems. The operations performed in python framework are loading a raw dirty dataset, cleaning, and preprocessing. The dataset is fed into the models for exploring the visualizations regionally and globally. The global temperature trend is predicted after training the models and evaluating the performance of these models. Popular neural networks such as RNN, long short-term memory, and gated recurrent unit are implemented using various optimizers like Adam, SGD, Adamax, Adagrad, Adadelta, RMSProp, and Nadam for predicting the global temperature trend and assessing the performance of the models. Finally, the DL models illustrated in the present work could be utilized to forecast the planet’s upcoming climatic change.

 

### Keywords

Climate crisis; global warming; recurrent neural networks; visualizations; climate change

 

## 9.1 Introduction

As illustrated in [Fig. 9–1](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#f0010), the world’s present population of 8.1 billion is predicted to grow approximately 9.8 billion by 2050, corresponding to the U.S. Census Bureau—International Database ([http://www.census.gov](http://www.census.gov)). This increase in population will raise demand for resources like food, materials, transportation, and energy, as well as raise the potential for deteriorating the environment.

![Figure 9–1 To calculate the global population the U.S. Census Bureau uses data from the international Database.](images/F000080f09-01-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 9–1](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#Bf0010) To calculate the global population the U.S. Census Bureau uses data from the international Database.

Our planet has experienced drastic climatic changes over the past years. Scientists observed that global warming is now imminent owing to satellites orbiting the Earth and other innovations in technology. The mean ambient temperature of the world has grown by 1.62°F (0.9°C), since the late 19th century, mostly due to increased atmospheric emissions of CO[2] and other substances caused by humans. The majority of the warming has happened in the last 35–40 years, which is entirely the result of human activities.

Arguably the biggest threat to civilization is impact of global warming, and its impacts are becoming ever more obvious: storms, droughts, fires, and flooding are becoming stronger and more common. Global environment is altering, affecting agricultural activities and natural resources that are essential to the survival of humanity. According to the 2018 global assessment of climate disruption, if greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions are not eradicated globally within 30 years, catastrophic repercussions will befall the earth ([David et al., 2022](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib5)).

 

### 9.1.1 Global challenge

In addition to raising global temperatures, environmental change is also to blame for melting ice sheets, retreating glaciers, decreasing snowfall, rising sea levels, declining glaciers in the Arctic, and pH imbalances of oceans. Collectively, these problems create a global predicament.

**\[Imp Question\] Will humanity be able to protect the Earth for coming generations?**

Yes, the ability of mankind to mitigate global warming can be strengthened by computational intelligence.

 

### 9.1.2 Artificial intelligence

Artificial intelligence (AI) is described as the replication of cognitive abilities by machines, particularly automated systems. These procedures involve learning (the gathering of data and regulations to utilize it), conceiving (the application of principles to achieve tentative or precise outcomes), and self-rectification. AI is a wide phrase with two candidatures: machine learning (ML) and deep learning (DL).

 

### 9.1.3 Machine learning

With the use of ML, systems may turn automatically deft by collecting data, utilizing it to learn, and then creating computer programs that may utilize the knowledge gained to come up with conclusions.

 

### 9.1.4 Deep learning

DL arranges algorithms into layers to produce an artificial neural network (ANN) by its own; such a system is capable of acquiring information and rendering prudent decisions. ML has a subfield called DL. In the functioning of human brain, the term “deep” in DL implies to several layers of interactions or neurons.

AI has the potential to transform the entire field when implemented effectively. AI has the ability to bring about innovative advances in technology. The following are some potential applications of DL for Earth:

*   • Climatic modeling and weather forecasting
*   • Smart agriculture
*   • Distributed energy grids.

Public agencies (such as NASA) use this to improve the functionality and effectiveness of both climate and weather models. Sophisticated data, such as algorithms and scientific principles involving fluid dynamics in the oceans and atmosphere, are processed by these mathematical frameworks. The equations’ intricacy necessitates expensive, highly energy-intensive computation. Certain characteristics of these climatic simulations can be roughly matched by DL networks, which enables computing to operate significantly faster and include more variables from the real-world system when performing simulations. Such weather and climatic models can benefit from the use of AI approaches to address biases.

AI has tremendous potential for expediting the pursuit of remedies for climatic change. AI is assisting researchers and decision-makers in understanding and addressing the issue of climatic change: (1) collecting and evaluating information, (2) forecasting, (3) improving systems efficiency and predictive maintenance, and (4) facilitating the invention of next-generation technologies. Despite these promising developments, AI’s application in creating climatic solutions is still beset by three major obstacles.

Firstly, ML is being used on the other side of the balance.

*   • By 2025, the petroleum and natural gas sector is expected to make $400 billion in profits thanks to the deployment of AI to speed up exploration and production of gas and oil.  
    Additionally, there’s the issue of AI’s green footprint, which is growing as its application and the need for processing power both expand.
*   • In 2019 the effect was quite minimal: AI contributed only a minor portion of the 1% to 2% worldwide information and communications technology sector’s GHG emissions. However, as AI is used more often and models get more sophisticated, its impact needs to be closely measured and monitored.  
    The final difficulty is accuracy, especially when it comes to safety for people and infrastructures.
*   • Applying ML to power grid regulation and optimization, which has strict limitations. Repercussions of AI optimization might be a power outage, with associated financial losses and possibly even the forfeiture of life—things we definitely do not want—if the physics are not correct.  
    Mitigation of climatic change is cutting emissions, while adaptation entails getting ready for inevitable outcomes.
*   • Modifications to land use, industry, buildings, transportation, and energy infrastructure are necessary to mitigate GHG emissions.
*   • Considering the climate and catastrophic occurrences, adaptation necessitates preparation for resilience and handling catastrophes.

ML has the potential to be a very effective instrument for lowering GHG emissions and assisting society in adapting to a changing environment. Both intriguing research questions and excellent commercial potential exist in smart grids to disaster management to identify high impact problems (the global effort against climate change) where ML can be used in conjunction with other disciplines to close current gaps.

A systematic review presented for hydrological processes climate change and earth systems using DL and ML ([Ardabili et al., 2020](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib1)). In an effort to improve urban resilience and reduce hazards to cities throughout the globe, a new viewpoint on the prediction of weather-related catastrophes has been offered. It relies on historic catastrophe data, computational models of the climate, and environmental measurements ([Haggag et al., 2021](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib6)). [Chakrabortty et al. (2021)](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib3) estimated flood susceptibility based on ANN, deep learning neural networks (DLNN), and data base (DB) algorithm approach. [Wunsch et al. (2022)](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib15) examined the direct effects that environmental change will have on Germany’s supplies of groundwater in the 21st century. The Earth surface is increasing its temperature according to the researchers at NASA, and the past two decades have seen several of the highest temperatures ever recorded ([NASA, 2024](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib13)). [Lee et al. (2020)](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib10) suggested a long-term, short-term memory framework, a data-based black-box technique, for the rainwater runoff monitoring procedure at the Kratie station on the main channel of the river Mekong. The initial DL-based forecasts of glacier formation were created by simulating the French Alps’ glacier evolution in the 21st century ([Bolibar et al., 2022](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib2)). Geographic engineering and the Earth’s surface temperature are the two fields of study that have made the greatest use of ML and DL algorithms in relation to mitigating and adapting to changing climates ([Ladi et al., 2022](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib9)). The LSTM beats the industry-standard exponential-smoothing and auto-regressive integrated moving average (ARIMA) models by 32%–48% in synthesizing a prediction over five critical climate factors with an estimated overall root mean square error of 0.91 ([Malik et al., 2023](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib12)). Policymakers and other stakeholders should use these results as a reference when creating measures to reduce the impact of climate change on agricultural land and increase resilience. Using forecasting models and instances related to rising temperatures, a deep neural network projects potential environmental, economic, and risky situations related to hydrocarbons that are polycyclic aromatic in 2050 ([Ki et al., 2021](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib8)). According to the evaluation, in western and southern regions, cancer rates could rise by over 50%, surpassing the less-hazardous threshold. [Lydiri et al. (2023)](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib11) utilized a convolutional neural network to analyze Twitter users’ thoughts about the changing climate. The outcomes demonstrated that our method works better than the logistic regression, support vector machine, and naive Bayes ML techniques. In an area characterized by the southern monsoon, the effects of climate change as well as the high rainfall events that go along with it pose a serious danger to land resources ([Paramita et al., 2022](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib14)). To estimate the vulnerability of gully subsidence in the basin of River Dwarakeswar, researchers looked at ANN, SVM, and DLNN.

 

## 9.2 Deep learning models for climate change

In the global fight toward global warming, this chapter strives to give an extensive summary of the areas in which DL could prove very impactful when used through creative research or efficient engineering. One common DL technique for handling consecutive data is recurrent neural networks (RNNs). They have already been effectively used in a number of fields, including the processing of natural languages, language modeling, and voice recognition. Yet, the disappearing and ballooning gradient issues make retraining RNNs difficult. Many RNN architectures, such as gated recurrent unit (GRU) and long short-term memory (LSTM) networks, have been put forward to address these problems. In this chapter, the authors compared these models and highlight their strengths and weaknesses.

 

### 9.2.1 Recurrent neural networks

Neural networks with loops that enable them to process sequential data are called RNNs. Unfortunately, the vanishing gradient problem impedes the conventional RNN’s ability to learn dependencies that persist. Two RNN variations that tackle this problem are GRU and LSTM. Sequential data processing and recurrence are the underlying principles of LSTMs, RNNs, and GRUs.

In an RNN, the result at each time step is dependent upon the preceding outputs as well as its input at that moment. An RNN with just one concealed layer and time-based backpropagation training resembles an Elman network, and it is the most basic type. Nevertheless, the issue of vanishing gradients impedes the typical RNN’s capacity to recognize dependencies that persist over time. [Fig. 9–2](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#f0015) shows the representation of standard RNN and feed-forward network.

![Figure 9–2 Representation of (A) standard recurrent neural network and (B) feed-forward network.](images/F000080f09-02-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 9–2](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#Bf0015) Representation of (A) standard recurrent neural network and (B) feed-forward network.

[Fig. 9–3](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#f0020) displays the RNN unit’s architecture. It receives information from both present and prior steps. Here, tan  *h*<mml:math><mml:mi>tan</mml:mi><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si1.png) serves as the activation function; however, you can also utilize different activation functions in its place. The primary characteristic of RNN is its utilization of layers that are concealed to solve problems. For the purpose of making predictions, RNNs can utilize concealed layers to aid in memory of the word (or data) sequence. The hidden state of RNN is given by

*h*[*t*] = tan  *h*(*W*[*h**h*]*h*[*t* − 1]+*W*[*x**h*]*x*[*t*]).<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>tan</mml:mi><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mi>h</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">hh</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">xh</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (9–1)

![Equation](images/F000080si2.png)(9–1) 

![Figure 9–3 Architecture of recurrent neural network unit.](images/F000080f09-03-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 9–3](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#Bf0020) Architecture of recurrent neural network unit.

 

### 9.2.2 Long short-term memory networks

LSTM was introduced by [Hochreiter and Schmidhuber (1997)](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib7). Periodically, an LSTM stores data within its memory cell. Three gates that are connected to the memory cell: the forget gate, the input gate, and the output gate. The input gate regulates how much fresh data are retained in a memory cell. The output gate regulates how much data are extracted out of the memory cell. The forget gate regulates how much data are erased from the memory cell. Essentially, each LSTM network has a trio of gates to regulate information flow and cells to store data as shown in [Figs. 9–4](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#f0025) and [9–5](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#f0030). Data are carried by the cell states through the first step to all subsequent time steps without disappearing.

![Figure 9–4 Architecture of long short-term memory cell.](images/F000080f09-04-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 9–4 Architecture of long short-term memory cell.

![Figure 9–5 Diagrammatic representation of long short-term memory architecture.](images/F000080f09-05-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 9–5 Diagrammatic representation of long short-term memory architecture.

The following mathematical notations are used for the LSTM architecture:

*f*[*t*] = *σ*[*g*](*W*[*f*]*x*[*t*]+*U*[*f*]*h*[*t* − 1]+*b*[*f*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>U</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math>

![Equation](images/F000080si3.png)

*i*[*t*] = *σ*[*g*](*W*[*i*]*x*[*t*]+*U*[*i*]*h*[*t* − 1]+*b*[*i*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>U</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math>

![Equation](images/F000080si4.png)

*O*[*t*] = *σ*[*O*](*W*[*O*]*x*[*t*]+*U*[*O*]*h*[*t* − 1]+*b*[*O*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>O</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>O</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>O</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>U</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>O</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>O</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math> (9–2)

![Equation](images/F000080si5.png)(9–2) 

*C*[*t*] = *f*[*t*] ⊙ *C*[*t* − 1] + *i*[*t*] ⊙ tan  *h*(*W*[*c*]*x*[*t*]+*U*[*c*]*h*[*t* − 1]+*b*[*c*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>f</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:mi>tan</mml:mi><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mi>h</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>U</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>c</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math>

![Equation](images/F000080si6.png)

*h*[*t*] = *O*[*t*] ⊙ tan  *h*(*C*[*t*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>O</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:mi>tan</mml:mi><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mi>h</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math>

![Equation](images/F000080si7.png) 

### 9.2.3 Gated recurrent unit networks

Cho et al. suggested GRU in 2014 ([Cho et al., 2014](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#bib4)). The flow of data is managed by GRU through the usage of gates as shown in [Figs. 9–6](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#f0035) and [9–7](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#f0040). But there are just two gates on it: the reset gate and the update gate. The quantity of fresh data incorporated into the prior state is managed by the update gate. The quantity of prior data that is erased is managed by the reset gate. The GRU architecture employs the subsequent mathematical notations:

*z*[*t*] = *σ*[*g*](*W*[*z*]*x*[*t*]+*U*[*z*]*h*[*t* − 1]+*b*[*z*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>z</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>z</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>U</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>z</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>z</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math>

![Equation](images/F000080si8.png)

*r*[*t*] = *σ*[*g*](*W*[*r*]*x*[*t*]+*U*[*r*]*h*[*t* − 1]+*b*[*r*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>U</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math> (9–3)

![Equation](images/F000080si9.png)(9–3) 

*h*[*t*] = (1 − *z*[*t*]) ⊙ *h*[*t* − 1] + *z*[*t*] ⊙ *ϕ*[*h*](*W*[*h*]*x*[*t*]+*U*[*h*](*r*[*t*]⊙*h*[*t* − 1])+*b*[*h*])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>z</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>z</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#981;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>W</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>U</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>r</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8857;</mml:mo><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>h</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math>

![Equation](images/F000080si10.png)

![Figure 9–6 Architecture of gated recurrent unit cell.](images/F000080f09-06-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 9–6 Architecture of gated recurrent unit cell.

![Figure 9–7 Diagrammatic representation of gated recurrent unit architecture.](images/F000080f09-07-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 9–7 Diagrammatic representation of gated recurrent unit architecture.

 

#### 9.2.3.1 Long short-term memory versus gated recurrent unit

The employment of gates to regulate information flow is a common feature shared by GRU and LSTM. But GRU has just two gates, whereas LSTM has three. Because of this, LSTM is both more complicated and expressive than GRU. Additionally, LSTM and GRU differ in that the former contains a distinct memory cell while the latter merges them into one vector of memory. The effectiveness of LSTM as well as GRU across a variety of tasks (including sentiment analysis, language modeling, and speech recognition) has been examined in a number of studies. The findings are conflicting; whereas some research indicate that LSTM works better than GRU, others suggest the contrary. The majority of research, however, concur that both LSTM as well GRU are equally useful when analyzing sequential data, although the particular job and dataset will determine how well each model performs.

 

### 9.2.4 Mathematical equations for MSE, MAE, MAPE, and *R*²

To assess the accuracy of the network, mean squared error (MSE), mean absolute error (MAE), mean absolute percentage error (MAPE), and *R*\-squared (*R*^(**2**)) are calculated by [Eqs. (9–4)](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#eqn4), [(9–5)](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#eqn5), [(9–6)](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#eqn6), and [(9–7)](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#eqn7), respectively.

${MSE} = \\frac{1}{N}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N}\\left( {y\_{actual} - y\_{predicted}} \\right)^{2}$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">MSE</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">actual</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">predicted</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:math> (9–4)

![Equation](images/F000080si11.png)(9–4) 

${MAE} = \\frac{1}{N}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N}\\left| {y\_{actual} - y\_{predicted}} \\right|$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">MAE</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="|" open="|"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">actual</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">predicted</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math> (9–5)

![Equation](images/F000080si12.png)(9–5) 

${MAPE} = \\frac{1}{N}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N}\\left| \\frac{y\_{actual} - y\_{predicted}}{y\_{actual}} \\right|$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">MAPE</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="|" open="|"><mml:mrow><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">actual</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">predicted</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">actual</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math> (9–6)

![Equation](images/F000080si13.png)(9–6) 

$R^{2} = 1 - \\frac{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N}\\left( {y\_{actual} - y\_{predicted}} \\right)^{2}}{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N}\\left( {y\_{mean} - y\_{actual}} \\right)^{2}},$<mml:math><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>R</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">actual</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">predicted</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mean</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">actual</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math> (9–7)

![Equation](images/F000080si14.png)(9–7) 

where *y*[*p**r**e**d**i**c**t**e**d*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">predicted</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si15.png) represents the output of the ANN model, *y*[*a**c**t**u**a**l*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">actual</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si16.png) is the results of the numerical simulation, *N*<mml:math><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si17.png) is the total amount of data, and *y*[*m**e**a**n*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mean</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si18.png) is the mean of the numerical simulation results for all data points.

 

### 9.2.5 Mathematical equations for optimizers: Adam, SGD, Adamax, Adagrad, Adadelta, RMSProp, and Nadam

In this chapter, the following equations are used to better understand the properties of optimizers:

 

#### 9.2.5.1 Adagrad

 

$\\theta\_{t + 1} = \\theta\_{t} - \\frac{\\eta}{\\sqrt{G\_{t} + \\epsilon}}g\_{t},$<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#951;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msqrt><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>G</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#1013;</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msqrt></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math> (9–8)

![Equation](images/F000080si19.png)(9–8) 

where *θ*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#952;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si20.png) is the node weight for generation *t*<mml:math><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si21.png), *η*<mml:math><mml:mi>&#951;</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si22.png) is the learning rate, *g*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si23.png) is the vector of gradients for the current generation, *G*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>G</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si24.png) is a diagonal matrix containing the squares of all previous gradients, and *ϵ*<mml:math><mml:mi>&#1013;</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si25.png) is a very small term that provides numerical stability.

 

#### 9.2.5.2 Adamax

 

$\\left. \\begin{matrix} {m\_{t} = \\beta\_{1}m\_{t - 1} + \\left( {1 - \\beta\_{1}} \\right)g\_{t}} \\\\ \\quad \\\\ {v\_{t} = \\beta\_{1}v\_{t - 1} + \\left( {1 - \\beta\_{2}} \\right){g\_{t}}^{2}} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right\\},$<mml:math><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="}" open=""><mml:mrow><mml:mtable><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>m</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>m</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mtd></mml:mtr><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:mspace width="1em"/></mml:mtd></mml:mtr><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mtd></mml:mtr></mml:mtable></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math> (9–9)

![Equation](images/F000080si26.png)(9–9) 

where *m*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>m</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si27.png) is an exponentially decaying average of past gradients, *β*[1](=0.900 *i**n* *K**e**r**a**s*)<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.900</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">in Keras</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si28.png) is the decay rate, *v*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si29.png) is an exponentially decaying average of past squared gradients, and *β*[2](=0.999 *i**n* *K**e**r**a**s*)<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>0.999</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.25em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">in Keras</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si30.png) is the decay rate.

 

#### 9.2.5.3 Adam

 

*v*[*t*] = max (*β*[2]*v*[*t* − 1],|*g*[*t*]|)<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>max</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="|" open="|"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:math> (9–10)

![Equation](images/F000080si31.png)(9–10)  

#### 9.2.5.4 SGD

The following equation is used to generate a new sequence of data with less noise:

*v*[*t*] = *β**v*[*t* − 1] + (1−*β*)*s*[*t*],<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>v</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>s</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math>

![Equation](images/F000080si32.png)

where *s*[*t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>s</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si33.png) is the noisy data generated in time *t*<mml:math><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si34.png).

The following equations are used to update weights (*w*)<mml:math><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi>w</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si35.png) and bias (*b*)<mml:math><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi>b</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si36.png) in SGD:

$\\left. \\begin{matrix} {w\_{t} = w\_{t - 1} - \\eta\\frac{\\partial L}{\\partial w\_{t - 1}}} \\\\ \\quad \\\\ {b\_{t} = b\_{t - 1} - \\eta\\frac{\\partial L}{\\partial b\_{t - 1}}} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right\\},$<mml:math><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="}" open=""><mml:mrow><mml:mtable><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#951;</mml:mi><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8706;</mml:mo><mml:mi>L</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8706;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac></mml:mtd></mml:mtr><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:mspace width="1em"/></mml:mtd></mml:mtr><mml:mtr><mml:mtd><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#951;</mml:mi><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8706;</mml:mo><mml:mi>L</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8706;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac></mml:mtd></mml:mtr></mml:mtable></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math> (9–11)

![Equation](images/F000080si37.png)(9–11) 

where $\\frac{\\partial L}{\\partial w}$<mml:math><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8706;</mml:mo><mml:mi>L</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8706;</mml:mo><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si38.png) is the derivative of loss with respect to weight and $\\frac{\\partial L}{\\partial b}$<mml:math><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8706;</mml:mo><mml:mi>L</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8706;</mml:mo><mml:mi>b</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si39.png) is the derivative of loss with respect to bias.

 

#### 9.2.5.5 Adadelta

 

$w\_{t} = w\_{t - 1} - \\eta\_{t}^{\\prime}\\frac{\\partial L}{\\partial w\_{t - 1}}{with}\\eta\_{t}^{\\prime} = \\frac{\\eta}{\\sqrt{g\_{t} + \\varepsilon}},$<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#951;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8242;</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#8706;</mml:mi><mml:mi>L</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#8706;</mml:mi><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">with</mml:mi><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#951;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8242;</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mi>&#951;</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:msqrt><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#949;</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msqrt></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:mo>,</mml:mo></mml:math> (9–12)

![Equation](images/F000080si40.png)(9–12) 

where

$g\_{t} = \\beta g\_{t - 1} + \\left( {1 - \\beta} \\right)\\left\\lbrack \\frac{\\partial L}{\\partial w\_{t - 1}} \\right\\rbrack^{2}.$<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>g</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close="\]" open="\["><mml:mrow><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8706;</mml:mo><mml:mi>L</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8706;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mo>.</mml:mo></mml:math> (9–13)

## 9.3 Operations in python framework using Jupyter Notebook

In this chapter, the raw dirty global datasets are collected from Berkeley Earth ([http://berkeleyearth.org/data/](http://berkeleyearth.org/data/)). It offers gridded data on temperatures as well as superior resolution time series data for both land and sea from 1750. The following newest generation of products are augmented by ML techniques to improve the spatial resolution:

*   • GlobalLandTemperaturesByCountry.csv (Country-wise Global Average Land Temperature)
*   • GlobalLandTemperaturesByState.csv (State-wise Global Average Land Temperature)
*   • GlobalLandTemperaturesByMajorCity.csv (Global Land Temperatures by major city)
*   • GlobalLandTemperaturesByCity.csv (City-wise Global Land Temperature)
*   • GlobalTemperatures.csv (Global Temperatures of Land and Ocean-and-Land).

The raw dirty dataset which requires lots of data cleaning and preprocessing. After performing necessary feature engineering steps and exploring the data with visualizations, data are prepared to feed into the models. After that, the models are built, trained, and evaluated for tasks: predicting global temperature trend and predicting US and India temperature trend. The following operations are performed for the trends:

1.  1. Loading dataset
2.  2. Performing exploratory data analysis
3.  3. Performing data cleaning
4.  4. Performing data visualization
5.  5. Preparing data to train the global model (global temperature trend)
6.  6. Building and training RNN, LSTM, and GRU model for predicting global temperature trend
7.  7. Evaluating the performance of the model built in Step 6
8.  8. Preparing the data to train the US and India model (US and India temperature trend)
9.  9. Building and training RNN, LSTM, and GRU model for predicting US and India temperature trend
10.  10. Evaluating the performance of the model built in Step 9.

In this chapter, all the operations are implemented using Jupyter Notebook within python framework and its *pseudocode* is as follows:

![](images/F000080u09-01-9780443219054.jpg)![](images/F000080u09-02-9780443219054.jpg)![](images/F000080u09-03-9780443219054.jpg)

![](images/F000080u09-04-9780443219054.jpg)

To produce predictions or judgements, a model is trained using a dataset within both ML and DL systems. The process of optimization, which entails determining the ideal model parameter set to minimize errors or losses on the data being trained, is a crucial step in the training process. The optimization algorithm is essential to the success of any DL program. Algorithms called optimizers are employed in the training phase in order to determine the ideal model’s parameter configuration. The aforementioned techniques iteratively modify the model’s weights and prejudices until they reach an optimal loss value.

Numerous optimizers are available from Keras for neural network model training. The following optimization algorithm are used in the present DL in order to enhance the training of neural networks:

*   • Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam)
*   • Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD)
*   • Adaptive Moment Estimation with Infinity Norm (Adamax)
*   • Adaptive Gradient Algorithm (Adagrad)
*   • Adaptive Delta (Adadelta)
*   • Root Mean Square Propagation (RMSProp)
*   • Nesterov Adaptive Moment Estimation (Nadam).

The advantages and disadvantages of various optimizers are tabulated in [Table 9–1](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#t0010). In the first step, LSTM model is built and trained for predicting global temperature trend with activation=*l**i**n**e**a**r*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">linear</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si48.png), optimizer=*a**d**a**m*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">adam</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si49.png), and loss=*m**s**e*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">mse</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si50.png). In the second step, GRU model is built and trained for predicting global temperature trend with activation=*l**i**n**e**a**r*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">linear</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si51.png), optimizer=*a**d**a**m*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">adam</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si52.png), and loss=*m**s**e*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">mse</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si53.png). In the third step, RNN model is built and trained for predicting global temperature trend with activation=*l**i**n**e**a**r*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">linear</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si54.png), optimizer=*a**d**a**m*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">adam</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si55.png), and loss=*m**s**e*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">mse</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000080si56.png). In [Table 9–2](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#t0015), the trainable parameters are 53,051, 40,251, and 13,301 for LSTM, GRU, and RNN, respectively.

Table 9–1

Advantages and disadvantages of various optimizers.

  
| S. No. || Optimizer || Advantages || Disadvantages |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 || Adam ||  

• Efficient

• Ease of implementation

• Applicable to large datasets and high-dimensional models

 || Hyperparameter tweaking is necessary |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2 || SGD ||  

• Simple to implement

• Efficient in computation

• Works well with big datasets that have an elevated feature space dimensionality

 ||  

• Inert in the nearby minimum

• Extremely sensitive to the starting learning rate

 |
| 3 || Adamax ||  

• Robust to high-dimensional spaces

• Performs well in presence of noisy gradients

 || Computationally expensive |
| 4 || Adagrad ||  

• Adaptive learning rate per parameter

• Efficient with sparse data

 ||  

• Stop learning too early

• Acquisition rates may rapidly decrease as quadratic gradients accumulate

 |
| 5 || Adadelta ||  

• Lack of learning rate exceedingly precise

• Has the ability to adjust the pace of learning even more quickly than Adagrad

 || Rapid convergence is caused by an excessively rapid pace of learning adaptation |
| 6 || RMSProp ||  

• Working well for goals that are not stationary

• A parameter that restricts the buildup of gradients has an adaptive learning rate

 || Slow convergence rate in some situations |
| 7 || Nadam ||  

• Little faster training time than Adam

• Less tuning on hyperparameters

 || Change optimizer if performance of model does not meet expectation |

Table 9–2

Trainable parameters in long short-term memory (LSTM), gated recurrent unit (GRU), and simple recurrent neural network (RNN).

 
| S. No. || Machine learning model || Total parameters || Trainable parameters || Nontrainable parameters |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 || LSTM || 53,051 || 53,051 || 0 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2 || GRU || 40,251 || 40,251 || 0 |
| 3 || Simple RNN || 13,301 || 13,301 || 0 |

For the US data using LSTM algorithm and Adam optimizer, the history, year-wise temperatures, and errors are plotted as shown in [Figs.9–23](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#f0120)–[9–26](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#f0135). Similarly, [Figs.9–27](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#f0140)–[9–30](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#f0155) show the history, year-wise temperatures, and errors for the India data using LSTM algorithm and Adam optimizer.

One popular metric for assessing the model’s forecasting efficiency is the mean squared error, or MSE. [Table 9–3](#Ch009_159-184_B9780443219054000080.xhtml#t0020) shows the MSE values for various optimizers used in LSTM, GRU and RNN algorithms. After learning about these three models, it is observed that LSTM performs well for the sequence global temperature data from 1743 to 2013. With Adam optimizer, faster training and computationally less expensive possible using LSTM, followed by GRU and RNN. Similarly, with SGD optimizer, LSTM prediction is good, followed by GRU and RNN. But using Adamax optimizer, LSTM prediction is good, followed by RNN and GRU. Similarly, with Adagrad and Adadelta optimizers, LSTM prediction is good, followed by GRU and RNN. With RMSProp optimizer, GRU prediction is good, followed by RNN and LSTM. LSTM prediction is good, followed by RNN and GRU using Nadam optimizer.

## 9.5 Conclusions and recommendations

This chapter describes a methodology for the modeling of climate change in terms of global temperature using DL models within python framework. Raw and dirty global datasets are collected from Berkeley Earth ([http://berkeleyearth.org/data/](http://berkeleyearth.org/data/)). It offers data on temperatures in grid format together with superior resolution time series data for both land and sea from 1743 to 2013. Three popular neural networks such as RNN, LSTM, and GRU are built, trained, and evaluated using various optimizers like Adam, SGD, Adamax, Adagrad, Adadelta, RMSprop, and Nadam for predicting global temperature trend as well as predicting US and India temperature trend. The main contributions of the present chapter are as follows:

1.  1. With Adam optimizer, faster training and computationally less expensive possible using LSTM, followed by GRU and RNN. The mean square errors are: MSE=0.09250031065887551 (LSTM), MSE=0.08040434597627735 (GRU), MSE=0.06096303859943463 (RNN)
2.  2. Using SGD optimizer, LSTM prediction is good, followed by GRU and RNN. The mean square errors are: MSE=1.8776324756386953 (LSTM), MSE=0.9815534033191056 (GRU), MSE=0.11220742113813277 (RNN)
3.  3. With Adamax optimizer, LSTM prediction is good, followed by RNN and GRU. The mean square errors are: MSE=0.0998461682132788 (LSTM), MSE=0.09855693036455689 (RNN), MSE=0.09817570471281879 (GRU)
4.  4. Using Adagrad and Adadelta optimizers, LSTM prediction is good, followed by GRU and RNN. The mean square errors are: MSE=22.645661292549917 (LSTM with Adagrad), MSE=2.210322750347732 (GRU with Adagrad), MSE=0.22145172804570754 (RNN with Adagrad), MSE=49.68461904083209 (LSTM with Adadelta), MSE=41.480035564039305 (GRU with Adadelta), MSE=13.816304188418261 (RNN with Adadelta)
5.  5. With RMSProp optimizer, GRU prediction is good, followed by RNN and LSTM. The mean square errors are: MSE=0.10032250216117587 (GRU), MSE=0.08745906210421796 (RNN), MSE=0.07977269791361769 (LSTM)
6.  6. LSTM prediction is good, followed by RNN and GRU using Nadam optimizer. The mean square errors are: MSE=0.09401263234931696 (LSTM), MSE=0.0772197251373337 (RNN), MSE=0.07443513244167563 (GRU).

The DL models illustrated in the present chapter could be utilized to forecast the planet’s upcoming climatic change.
