 10 

# Computational modeling approaches on tourism and climate change relations

Gamze Kose,    Department of Health Tourism, Aydin Adnan Menderes University, Aydin, Turkey

 

## Abstract

Human activities all over the world have a great impact on socioeconomic flow and technological changes. As an endless loop, human activities and outcomes determine the characteristics of societies and the exact situation of the future world. However, it should be indicated that not every human activity or outcome has positive effects on the world or the ecosystem we are living in. Although it seems to have a long history of occurrence, climate change has shown more negative effects, especially in the 21st century. Because of energy consumption, intense use of advanced technology, and wide human activities triggering unbalanced emissions, studies to reduce the effects of climate change have been a high priority, especially in last decade. At this point, there are many subareas to consider for specific relations between climate change and the associated activity scopes. Among these, tourism activities can be a niche area to discuss the possible effects of climate change. Also, the effects of climate change on tourism activities can be evaluated. So, the relationship can be evaluated from a two-sided perspective. The objective of this chapter is to provide a general overview of tourism and climate change and expand the focus to computational modeling approaches. By focusing on computational modeling, it is aimed at understanding how modeling can be effectively used and directed to optimize the tourism-climate change relationship and ensure a source of ideas for further research. Moreover, the chapter also gives a general approach to computational modeling of tourism-climate change interactions. This approach seems to be a timely contribution to the literature in the context of tourism-oriented studies and climate change actions.

 

### Keywords

Computational modeling; tourism; climate change; technology; future

 

## 10.1 Introduction

Tourism can be defined as a concept that explains the movement of people out of their known places (countries or cities) for personal or business-oriented purposes connected with cultural, social, and economic interactions with the target place ([Camilleri, 2018](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib14); [Netto, 2009](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib45)). In this context, tourism is not a common human activity. Instead, it has many reasons and results, which should be examined from the perspectives of both the social and technical sciences. Actually, connections with social and economic advantages have been shaping the general focus on the exact factors associated with tourism potential. Such potentials have been connected with people’s interests, sociocultural characteristics, direct or indirect reasons to perform tourism activities, and economic states to determine the plans for tourism actions ([Camilleri, 2018](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib14); [Sharma, 2004](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib64)). In addition to this, the general approach has been supported with the outcomes of creating an in-system feedback mechanism for ensuring sustainable tourism. All of these efforts have required close interaction with people’s feedback and hidden patterns inside the data, which is gathered widely thanks to the advancing technological components. It is also remarkable to indicate that tourism activities are highly sensitive to global changes in terms of technological and societal conditions.

When today’s conditions are examined in the context of a technological perspective, it can be said that advancements in terms of information and communication technologies (ICT) have made it more practical to perform travel for tourism purposes. ICT allows people to reach out for the desired information about specific points around the world. Furthermore, it is even more practical to get information about travel ways, places of cultural attractions, and potential activities to perform in target places ([Bethapudi, 2013](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib10); [Van Wee et al., 2013](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib72)). As ICT allows people to ensure global communication as well, information gathering and communication advantages have made it just a matter of time to plan and act in the context of tourism. Of course, the digital transformation happening in all areas of modern life has affected the way of doing business, so more practical ways of traveling worldwide have increased the intensity of tourism purposes for not only personal intentions but also business aims ([Abeba, 2024](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib1); [Imtiaz & Kim, 2019](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib30); [Şahin, 2024](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib55)). Therefore, moving from all the explanations so far, it can be clearly said that technology has been a critical catalyst for expanding worldwide tourism activities.

As is happening in tourism, technological advancements have been bringing advantages in many fields of life. However, the intense use of technology and the contributive role of human activities increase the level of climate change. Climate change is known for the remarkable changes and variations in the average state of the weather, considering temperature levels and weather events ([Romm, 2022](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib52)). Former research studies showed early warnings in terms of climate change, but the exact effects of the changing conditions of climate have been experiencing since the 2000s. Considering especially the last years, there have been many unexpected events such as storms, extreme (hot or cold) temperatures, forest fires, melting of the glaciers, and shifts in ecosystems ([Levine & Steele, 2021](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib39); [Walsh et al., 2020](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib73)). Even there have been some debates connecting COVID-19 and any potential pandemics to climate change ([Gupta et al., 2021](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib25); [Lounis et al., 2023](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib42)). In time, the exact term of climate change has been transformed into the climate crisis. Since it seems to be an existential risk for humanity and living organisms, there are currently intense efforts to deal with the negative effects of climate change and take precautions to turn the ongoing state into a balanced one. From nationwide regulations to reduce carbon emission to green solutions for low energy consumption or transitions to electrical vehicles to eliminate fossil fuel vehicles, many different ways are followed within the climate crisis struggle. Except for these, it is also important to analyze the ongoing human actions that can directly or indirectly contribute to the unbalanced use of resources. As associated with this, human activities are generally associated with using fossil fuel vehicles and interacting with advanced technology ([Dunlap & Brulle, 2015](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib20); [Karyono, 2015](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib34); [Trenberth, 2018](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib70)). Interactions with advanced technology include, for example, using cloud services, running artificial intelligence, investing in cryptocurrencies, and even using smart devices to take photos and recordings, or perform instant communication. Similarly, increasing rates of tourism activities can be connected with climate change relations, as there is a remarkable background of technology use for information gathering, communication, travel, and further actions within cultural, social, and business-related interactions. Since there is a wide data flow and potential connections with complex relations, these activities can be observed with the efforts of computational modeling. In addition to the negative effects of human activities, the examination can also focus on the negative effects of climate change on tourism activities. Actually, the literature is more interested in the effects of climate change on tourism, but the exact balance can be found better by building a two-sided discussion accordingly.

As a remarkable and timely perspective, this chapter follows the relationship between tourism and climate change. In this sense, it aims to provide a general overview of the relationship and expand the focus to approaches within the scope of computational modeling approaches. By focusing on computational modeling, it is aimed at understanding how modeling can be effectively used and directed to optimize the tourism-climate change relationship and ensure a source of ideas for further research. Moreover, the chapter also gives a general approach to computational modeling of tourism-climate change interactions. Since the literature on the intersection of tourism and climate change needs input, the efforts and outcomes within this chapter ensure the potential to contribute and quicken the work and further approaches.

As associated with the chapter scope, the next sections are organized as follows: The next section is devoted to explanations regarding climate change and tourism relations by building a discussion perspective with the literature. Following this section, the third section focuses on the computational modeling perspective. In detail, the section provides explanations regarding how computational modeling works and defines sample modeling approaches accordingly. Finally, the chapter ends with discussions on further research and points to conclusions.

 

## 10.2 Connections between tourism and climate change

Today’s tourism scope is highly based on technology use since any phase of the activities can be improved by using technological components. Also, changing climate conditions force the tourism area to change the directions or focus areas to different locations or activity phases. So, it is important to understand how different actors and activities shape the connections between tourism and climate change. This section provides general explanations just before forwarding the focus to the computational modeling view.

 

### 10.2.1 Technology factor

Tourism has multiple phases when it is evaluated from the perspective of different actors such as agencies, managers, tourists, and third-party service providers. In event planning, advertising, and promoting activities, the agencies and managers use data-oriented analyses and decision support systems. When it is considered from the perspective of tourists, similar technology interaction finds its way through the use of social media, specially developed applications, and communication cycles empowered by advanced technology background. So, even before the exact touristic activities, there is a remarkable intensity of technology use, which can be connected with climate change dynamics. At the time of travel, some resources are used and spent for the movement. Considering travel with flights and further transportation, there are multiple ways of using technology, which triggers the spending of resources such as fuel and electricity. When tourism activities are happening, it is almost an endless flow because of unpredictable human activities inside the tourism events. Of course, the speciality of tourism causes increases in human activities, leading to further technology and resource use. [Fig. 10–1](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#f0010) presents an overview of different phases of tourism and potential technology use cases.

![Figure 10–1 Phases of tourism activities and the potential technology use cases.](images/F00002Xf10-01-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 10–1](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#Bf0010) Phases of tourism activities and the potential technology use cases.

Although the exact tourism activities are not at the heart of climate change, they can be accepted as indirect effects, which bring exponential negative effects, when a number of people, resources, technological components, and the speed of technology use are considered at the same time. Actually, the effects of climate change should be analyzed in terms of exponential reasons, which are appearing rapidly in the technological era. The tourism activities happening, especially internationally, are examples of this, but national actions should be included in the same scope as the technology use already takes place in human actions toward tourism intentions. In addition to the perspective of human activities, the analysis can also be done for the effects of climate change on tourism activities. At this point, it can be said that climate change can affect tourism activities by causing them to spend more money on adaptation strategies or newly designed plans according to new destinations as a result of changed ecosystem dynamics around the world. However, it is important to point out that adaptation strategies can lead to more technology or resource use, so an endless loop can be created for mutual negative effects between tourism and climate change. This situation points to the need for optimum solutions, which can be achieved through carefully designed policies and global awareness. As a part of these efforts, computational solutions can greatly help since the current data era tends to be beyond human control capabilities.

 

### 10.2.2 Research on tourism and climate change relations

When the literature is examined, it can be seen that there are some research interests in understanding the relationship between tourism and climate change. Actually, the research studies generally focus on the negative effects of climate change on changing conditions within the scope of tourism. However, the other side of the medallion is always connected with the effects of human activities. Because of this, the exact relationship should be built from a two-sided perspective, as is often mentioned in this chapter.

 

#### 10.2.2.1 Effects of climate change on tourism

The literature connection between tourism and climate change seems to have been established some years ago. In her literature study, [Becken (2013)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib6) indicated a growing knowledge domain between tourism and climate change. She briefly detected the exact relation in the context of research efforts for multiple dimensions like policies, climate impacts, mitigation, and adaptation. Before this study, [Becken and Hay (2007)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib8) provided a book study in which risks and opportunities were widely discussed. In detail, the book accepted both tourism and climate as interacting systems. It considered the negative effects of climate change in terms of winter and coastal tourism cases. In terms of the greenhouse gas issue, the book provided a discussion from the perspective of the tourism industry and human behaviors. Additionally, the climate change mitigation solutions were connected with business, transportation, and carbon compensation projects targeting tourism ([Becken & Hay, 2007](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib8)). [Scott, Hall, et al. (2012)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib60) pointed to winter sports tourism, nature tourism, and coastal tourism as market segments under the effects of climate change and pointed to the complex relationship between tourism and climate change. They also expressed that the notable research method are associated with environmental/ecosystem change, temporal and geographic changes in tourism demand, and destination competitiveness affected by climate conditions. Remarkably, the book study by [Scott, Gössling, et al. (2012)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib57) provided another collection of discussions on the two-sided effects of tourism and climate change relations. Except for the needs for adaptation and mitigation in tourism, the book also focused on the carbon management perspective and considered the emissions of greenhouse gases as a result of growing tourism and mobility. In terms of adaptation and mitigation perspectives, [Weaver (2011)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib74) discussed the potential of sustainable tourism activities and considered sustainability opportunities in local factors like biodiversity and air quality. On the other hand, [Scott and Gössling (2022)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib59) pointed out the negative effects of climate change on cultural as well as natural heritage and tourism demands and argued that the tourism sector cannot sustain itself if the climate change issue cannot be managed effectively. Covering the related efforts, the literature has often received alternative perspectives for policies and societal efforts to find the balance between tourism and climate conditions ([Becken et al., 2020](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib9); [Becken & Clapcott, 2011](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib7); [Dubois & Ceron, 2006a](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib18); [Kajan & Saarinen, 2013](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib35); [Scott & Becken, 2010](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib56); [Scott & Gössling, 2021](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib58)).

 

#### 10.2.2.2 Effects of tourism regarding climate change

The literature focusing on the effects of tourism on the climate has remarkable outcomes when greenhouse gas emissions are considered in detail. [Lenzen et al. (2018)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib38) performed a research study in which they quantified that tourism caused global carbon flows among a total of 160 countries. The study revealed that there is a critical increase in terms of carbon footprints because of tourism activities. The research found that the reasons for tourism activities are related to transportation, food, and shopping. The authors argued that tourism will play a remarkable role in the global growth of greenhouse gas emissions. In detail, [Filimonau et al. (2013)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib23) found out that tourism has a critical indirect effect on greenhouse gas emissions, according to their findings from the research on carbon impacts in the context of holiday package tours. As another detected issue, [Eijgelaar et al. (2010)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib21) showed that Antarctic cruise tourism events have an impact on emissions and the climate crisis. Specifically, tourism demand as well as transportation, including aircraft and cruise ships, seem to be essential factors with negative effects. The related Antarctic tourism problem has been often considered in alternative studies ([Amelung & Lamers, 2007](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib3); [Jabour, 2014](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib32); [Kariminia et al., 2013](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib33); [Nielsen, 2022](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib46); [Shijin et al., 2020](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib65); [Tejedo et al., 2022](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib69)). There are former studies that showed the effects of tourism activities on climate by considering specific tourism locations around the world. [Gühnemann et al. (2021)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib26) showed that tourist travel in Austria contributes to gas emissions because of intense car travel and long-distance flights. Considering the case of Switzerland, [Perch-Nielsen et al. (2010)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib48) detected that especially air transportation has a high impact on gas emissions, leading to a climate crisis. The studies also perform predictions to see if the gas emissions from tourism could be reduced. However, the studies have not provided positive news so far ([Balli et al., 2019](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib5); [Dubois & Ceron, 2006b](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib19); [Isik et al., 2017](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib31); [Liu et al., 2021](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib41); [Scott et al., 2010](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib63)).

The research studies reveal that the tourism activities of high-income countries on the American continent have a leading role in negative effects on climate change ([Gozbasi et al., 2021](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib24)). That can also be extended to other high-income countries on a global scale ([Lenzen et al., 2018](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib38)). On the other hand, Asian countries with high-level tourism sectors were found to have disadvantages in environmental impact ([Nosheen et al., 2021](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib47)), but a very recent study by [Voumik et al. (2023)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib71) showed that tourism does not have a direct impact on carbon emissions in Asia. However, it seems that the main reasons reported in the study can still be indirectly linked to tourism activities. Eventually, it seems that the region-based research studies have not been enough to describe the global climate crisis clearly in the context of the tourism perspective of specific countries. So, the connection between tourism and climate change employs a cumulative state, while solutions can be thought of locally. When the details regarding tourism activities are evaluated, it can be seen that the literature often refers to transportation, technology use, and human mobility in the context of tourism and climate crisis relations ([Dube & Nhamo, 2021](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib17)). When the findings are considered, the following factors can be indicated as the essential components of tourism that contribute to the climate change in a negative way:

*   • *Transportation and Travels*: Especially air transportation, cruise trips, and long-distance car travel (specifically with fossil fuels and/or old cars) cause an increase in greenhouse gas emissions.
*   • *Technology*: Although this factor should be examined further to figure out major technology tools and/or usage perspectives, it seems that the intense use of technology during tourism activities has a role in negative effects. This connection is the actual issue connected with the positive correlation between increases in technology use and climate change.
*   • *Mobility*: Human mobility has many complex dynamics when it is considered in even tourism activities. It includes many human activities, including travel, shopping, technology use, and many more actions that can be connected to climate change scenarios. So, a cumulative view of human mobility seems to be another factor in the negative effects on the climate.

[Fig. 10–2](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#f0015) represents a general overview of the outcomes of the two-sided relationship between tourism and climate change. It can be seen that effects from both sides have their own characteristics and employ complex relations with many parameters. Additionally, the efforts to solve the problem of climate change require more predictive and descriptive analyses in the context of the related relationship. One vital approach in this manner is to use computational modeling to understand and predict dynamics. In this way, further findings can help design balanced relations between tourism and climate change.

![Figure 10–2 Two-sided relation between tourism and climate change.](images/F00002Xf10-02-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 10–2](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#Bf0015) Two-sided relation between tourism and climate change.

 

## 10.3 Computational modeling for tourism and climate change

Computational modeling approaches for tourism and climate change relationships have often been for predictive purposes. This is because further actions on struggling with climate effects or optimizing tourism activities (e.g., lowering gas emissions) require some valuable preknowledge structured with forecasts. Except for these, there is also a need for some descriptive modeling perspectives to understand current situations. Of course, computational modeling is an area with many open opportunities as a result of new scientific outcomes, parameters, and knowledge, feeding the understanding capabilities of dynamic characteristics at the intersection of tourism and climate.

 

### 10.3.1 Computational models for effects of climate change on tourism

The literature provided research studies for computational models from the perspective of climate change effects on tourism. For example, [Mieczkowski (1985)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib44) provided one of the first modeling perspectives by recalling the model as the tourism climate index (*TCIndex*). Considering the daytime thermal comfort index (*Cd\_coi*), the daily thermal comfort index (*Cda\_coi*), the index for the amount of precipitation (*Pa\_rec*), the index of the appreciation of wind (*W*), and the index for the value of sunshine (*S*), the TCIndex score was classified according to values ranging from 20 to 100:

*T**C**I**n**d**e**x* = 2(4*C**d**\_**c**o**i*+*C**d**a**\_**c**o**i*+2*S*+2*P**a**\_**r**e**c*+*W*)<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">TCIndex</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mn>4</mml:mn><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Cd\_coi</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Cda\_coi</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mi>S</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Pa\_rec</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>W</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math> (10–1)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi1.png)(10–1) 

The *TCIndex* was not aimed at focusing on direct climate change effects on tourism, but it has been enough to track the effects of changing climates on tourism flows. Except from this perspective, the literature has been interested in measuring tourism demand to understand the effects of climate change on tourism. In terms of computational modeling, three approaches have been generally considered: discrete choice models, time series analysis, and aggregate tourism demand models ([Rossello-Nadal, 2014](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib54)). Discrete choice models are based on the question of why people choose a specific tourism destination. According to this, [Morley (1992)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib76) came up with a model for utility calculation according to visit destination *i* (rather than *j* considering *i ≠ j*) with the choosing probability calculations as follows:

*U**t**i**l*[*n*\_*i*] = *β*[*n*]^′*a*[*n*\_*i*] + *τ*[*n*\_*i*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Util</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8242;</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>a</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#964;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math> (10–2)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi2.png)(10–2) 

*π*[*n*\_*i*] = Pr (*β*[*n*]^′*a*[*n*[*i*]]+*τ*[*n*[*i*]]\>*β*[*n*]^′*a*[*n*[*j*]]+*τ*[*n*[*j*]])<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#960;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>Pr</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8242;</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>a</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#964;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#62;</mml:mo><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#946;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8242;</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>a</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#964;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math> (10–3)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi3.png)(10–3) 

The related model sparked interest in research studies to evaluate microeconomic perspectives and further marketing analyses. However, it has been often considered under the influence of many parameters as the decision-making processes are somehow complex dynamics ([Marcussen, 2011](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib43); [Rossello-Nadal, 2014](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib54)). On the other hand, discrete choice models were used to understand how climate change affects decisions as well as the general state of tourism ([Bujosa & Rossello, 2013](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib13); [Eugenio-Martin & Campos-Soria, 2010](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib22)).

In the context of time series analysis, it has been better to integrate weather conditions into tourism dynamics. For example, considering the number of tourism flows (*F*[*i*]) in the month *i*, along with the innovation term (*x*[*i*]) and weather variables (*w*[*i*]), a predictive model was proposed in the following equation form ([Bujosa & Rossello, 2013](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib13)):

∃[*p*](*A*)*F**i* = ∇[*p*](*A*)*x*[*i*] + *δ*[*y*](*A*)*w*[*i*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8707;</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mi>A</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mi>F</mml:mi><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8711;</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>p</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mi>A</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#948;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi>A</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>w</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math> (10–4)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi4.png)(10–4) 

It is remarkable that such a model helped to reveal the significant relationship between British tourists’ tourism actions and different variables regarding British weather ([Rossello, 2009](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib53)). It is important to indicate that time series analysis has been valuable, like discrete choice models, since these modeling approaches showed the relationship between tourism and climate change. However, it was pointed out that both modeling methods allow for the gathering of information for short-term predictions, so long-term effects require additional modeling solutions ([Li et al., 2005](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib40); [Rossello-Nadal, 2014](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib54); [Song & Li, 2008](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib67)). To find an answer to this state, aggregate tourism demand models were introduced. As a remarkable model, the Hamburg Tourism Model (HTM) employs two equations for evaluating international tourist departures and arrivals in a target year. Specifically, the HTM tried to understand how climate change affects the tourist flow by considering parameters such as total number of arrivals (*Arr*), total number of departures (*Dep*), average temperature of the target country (*Tar*), population (*Pop*), and further parameters such as per-capita income of the country (*IC*) ([Hamilton et al., 2005](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib28)):

ln *A**r**r*[*d*] = *γ*[0] + *γ*[1]*X*[*d*] + *γ*[2]*T**a**r*[*d*] + *γ*[3]*T**a**r*[*d*]² + *γ*[4]*Y*[*d*] + *γ*[5]ln *I**C*[*d*]<mml:math><mml:mi>ln</mml:mi><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mspace width=".30em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Arr</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#947;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#947;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#947;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Tar</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#947;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>3</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Tar</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#947;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>4</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>Y</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#947;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>5</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mspace width=".20em"/><mml:mi>ln</mml:mi><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mspace width=".20em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">IC</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math> (10–5)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi5.png)(10–5) 

$\\ln\\frac{\\mathit{Dep}\_{\\mathit{OR}}}{\\mathit{Pop}\_{\\mathit{OR}}} = \\delta\_{0} + \\delta\_{1}t\_{\\mathit{OR}} + \\delta\_{2}t\_{\\mathit{OR}}^{2} + \\delta\_{3}Z\_{\\mathit{OR}} + \\delta\_{4}\\mathit{IC}\_{\\mathit{OR}} + \\delta\_{5}\\ln{ X}\_{\\mathit{OR}}$<mml:math><mml:mi>ln</mml:mi><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Dep</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">OR</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Pop</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">OR</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#948;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>0</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#948;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">OR</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#948;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">OR</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#948;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>3</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>Z</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">OR</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#948;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>4</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">IC</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">OR</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#948;</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>5</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mspace width=".20em"/><mml:mi>ln</mml:mi><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mspace width=".25em"/><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">OR</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math> (10–6)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi6.png)(10–6) 

The effects of climate change on tourism were also studied from the perspective of tourism venues. For example, [Pons-Pons et al. (2012)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib50) modeled the effects of winter ski tourism in Andorra. [Hein et al. (2009)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib29) performed another location-specific research by evaluating five different tourism regions in Spain. [Bigano, Hamilton, et al. (2008)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib12) provided a multicountry model for understanding the effects of sea level rise on tourism. [Carrillo et al. (2022)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib15) used a weather prediction model to study on the effects of the Canary Islands. There are also alternative studies to run computational models for understanding the effects of climate change on wide and smaller region choices like North America, the Mediterranean, Portugal, Tibet, the German-Swiss and Austrian Alps, and the Canadian Rocky Mountains ([Bigano, Bosello, et al., 2008](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib11); [Pintassilgo et al., 2016](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib49); [Scott et al., 2004](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib62), [2007](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib61); [Soboll & Dingeldey, 2012](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib66); [Zhong et al., 2019](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib75)). Such choices point to the importance of ensuring sustainability for popular tourism destinations as well as country-wide visiting regions against the negative effects of climate change.

 

### 10.3.2 Computational models for effects of tourism on climate

When the scenario is turned to the case of tourism triggering climate change/crisis, there are remarkable studies based on computational modeling. Such studies consider greenhouse gas emissions caused by tourism activities widely. For example, [Kelly and Williams (2007)](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib36) introduced a model for destination planning strategies with energy use and gas emission states. Based on Whistler, British Columbia (a mountain resort location in North America), they measured the energy and gas emissions by considering aspects such as travel flows (PerKmTr: person kilometers traveled) and energy consumption in buildings:

${\\mathit{Per}\\mathit{Km}\\mathit{Tr}}\_{x} = \\sum\\limits\_{y = 1}^{m}\\left( \\mathit{empl}\_{y} \\times \\mathit{work}\\\_\\mathit{days} \\times {\\mathit{return}\\\_\\mathit{dist}}\_{y} \\times {{mod}\\\_\\mathit{spl}}\_{\\mathit{xy}} \\right)$<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Per</mml:mi><mml:mspace width=".20em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Km</mml:mi><mml:mspace width=".20em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Tr</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>m</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">empl</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#215;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">work</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">days</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#215;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">return</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">dist</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#215;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>mod</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">spl</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">xy</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math> (10–7)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi7.png)(10–7) 

${\\mathit{Per}\\mathit{Km}\\mathit{Tr}}\_{x} = \\sum\\limits\_{y = 1}^{m}\\left( \\mathit{vis}\_{y} \\times {\\mathit{return}\\\_\\mathit{dist}}\_{y} \\times {{mod}\\\_\\mathit{spl}}\_{\\mathit{xy}} \\right)$<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Per</mml:mi><mml:mspace width=".20em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Km</mml:mi><mml:mspace width=".20em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Tr</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>m</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">vis</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#215;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">return</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">dist</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>y</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#215;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>mod</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">spl</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">xy</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math> (10–8)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi8.png)(10–8) 

*E**n**g*\_*c**o**n**s**u**m**p*[*z**t*] = *E**x**i**s*\_*f**l**o**o**r*\_*a**r**e**a*[*z**t*] × *E**x**i**s*\_*E*\_*U*\_*I*[*z**t*]*E**x**i**s**t*\_*f**l**o**o**r*\_*a**r**e**a*[*z**t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Eng</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">consump</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">zt</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Exis</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">floor</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">area</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">zt</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#215;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Exis</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi>E</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi>U</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi>I</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">zt</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Exist</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">floor</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">area</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">zt</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math> (10–9)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi9.png)(10–9) 

Another research study focuses on the dynamics of carbon emissions along with tourism economic growth as well as technological efficiency ([Sun, 2016](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib68)). In this way, the research built a general model, including computations for a visitor’s economic activities and energy usage. In detail, the model uses an approach to tourism direct and domestic indirect greenhouse gas emissions (GHG) as follows:

For calculating the difference in carbon emissions between time periods:

*E**f**f**e**c**t* = *C**D**Y*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Effect</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">CDY</mml:mi></mml:math> (10–10)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi10.png)(10–10) 

*E**f**f**e**c**t*[*t**i**m**e* + 1] − *E**f**f**e**c**t*[*t**i**m**e*] = *E*[*i**n**t*] + *E*[*s**t**r*] + *E*[*f**d*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Effect</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">time</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Effect</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">time</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>int</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">str</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">fd</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math> (10–11)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi11.png)(10–11) 

*E*[*i**n**t*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>int</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F00002Xsi12.png) stands for calculating the intensity effect value:

*E*[*i**n**t*] = *Δ**C**D**Y* + 1/2*Δ**C**Δ**D**Y* + 1/2*Δ**C**D**Δ**Y* + 1/3*Δ**C**Δ**D**Δ**Y*<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>int</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">CDY</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">DY</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">CD</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>Y</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>3</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>D</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>Y</mml:mi></mml:math> (10–12)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi13.png)(10–12) 

*E*[*s**t**r*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">str</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F00002Xsi14.png) is for calculating the structure effect value:

*E*[*s**t**r*] = *C**Δ**D**Y* + 1/2*Δ**C**Δ**D**Y* + 1/2*C**Δ**D**Δ**Y* + 1/3*Δ**C**Δ**D**Δ**Y*<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">str</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">DY</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">DY</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>D</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>Y</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>3</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>D</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>Y</mml:mi></mml:math> (10–13)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi15.png)(10–13) 

*E*[*f**d*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">fd</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math>![Equation](images/F00002Xsi16.png) stands for calculating the final demand effect value:

*E*[*f**d*] = *C**D**Δ**Y* + 1/2*Δ**C**D**Δ**Y* + 1/2*C**Δ**D**Δ**Y* + 1/3*Δ**C**Δ**D**Δ**Y*<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">fd</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">CD</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>Y</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">CD</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>Y</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>D</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>Y</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>3</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>D</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#916;</mml:mo><mml:mi>Y</mml:mi></mml:math> (10–14)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi17.png)(10–14) 

In terms of the past time periods, the model applies calculations done through matrix differences ([Sun, 2016](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib68)), and the tourism direct and domestic indirect GHG emissions are calculated as follows:

*E**f**f**e**c**t*\_*T**o**t**a**l* = *C*(1−*M*[*d*])^( − 1)*D**Y* = *C**B**D**Y*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Effect</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Total</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>M</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>d</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">DY</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">CBDY</mml:mi></mml:math> (10–15)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi18.png)(10–15) 

Considering the past time periods:

*E**f**f**e**c**t*\_*T**o**t**a**l*[*t**i**m**e* + 1] − *E**f**f**e**c**t*\_*T**o**t**a**l*[*t**i**m**e*] = *E*[*i**n**t*] + *E*[*l**t**f*] + *E*[*s**t**r*] + *E*[*f**d*]<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Effect</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Total</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">time</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Effect</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">\_</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">Total</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">time</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>int</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">ltf</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">str</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>E</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">fd</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math> (10–16)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi19.png)(10–16)  

### 10.3.3 Computational model suggestions

Considering the general approaches in the context of the literature, some computational models can be thought of in terms of a balanced modeling structure. In this context, the first model suggestion can be in the form of agents associated with tourism activities. Such an agent can be defined as:

*A*[*i**j*] = *G*(*a*) + *T*(*a*) + *C*<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mi>A</mml:mi><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">ij</mml:mi></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>G</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi>a</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi>a</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>C</mml:mi></mml:math> (10–17)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi20.png)(10–17) 

where multiple agents (*A*) for the actor *i* can be associated with an activity *j*, and the corresponding greenhouse gas emission can be calculated through the model of *G* while the technological emission is calculated with *T*, and constants such as weather events, temperatures, demand, or economic states can be included or excluded depending on the target activity as well as location. Such constants are with another model defined under the *C*. The model can be integrated with some other models defined as financial factors (*F*) and travel factors (*T*):

*F*(*x*[1],*x*[2],*x*[3],…,*x*[*n*]) = (*x*[1],*x*[2],*x*[3…]+*C*[1])/(*x*[10],*x*[11],*x*[*n* − *m*…]+*C*[2])<mml:math><mml:mi>F</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>3</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>&#8230;</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>3</mml:mn><mml:mi>&#8230;</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>10</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>11</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>n</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>m</mml:mi><mml:mi>&#8230;</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math> (10–18)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi21.png)(10–18) 

*T*(*P**K**T*,*V**K**T*) = *E*(*P**K**T*,*V**K**T*) + *C*[3]<mml:math><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>P</mml:mi><mml:mi>K</mml:mi><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>V</mml:mi><mml:mi>K</mml:mi><mml:mi>T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>E</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>P</mml:mi><mml:mi>K</mml:mi><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>V</mml:mi><mml:mi>K</mml:mi><mml:mi>T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>3</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:math> (10–19)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi22.png)(10–19) 

*A*[*i**j*] = *G*(*a*) + *T*(*a*) + (*C*+*M*(*F*,*T*))<mml:math><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>A</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mi>j</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi>G</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">(</mml:mo><mml:mi>a</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="true">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>T</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">(</mml:mo><mml:mi>a</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="true">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>C</mml:mi><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi>M</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">(</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>F</mml:mi><mml:mo>,</mml:mo><mml:mi>T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mo stretchy="true">)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math> (10–20)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi23.png)(10–20) 

As the *F* and *T* are additional models included as effective factors over known constants of agent *A*, they also employ some known models that are associated with direct greenhouse gas emissions. Each agent should be considered an actor in the tourism scenario. These actors can be tourists, agencies, technological tools (software/hardware consuming energy), ICT components, or any other actors interacting with climate effects as well as the activity elements inside the tourism flow. In addition to these agents under the control of tourism activities, some other agents can be connected to climate parameters such as specific weather events (e.g., rainfall, snow, and extreme temperatures) or known rates affecting tourism activities. So, in this way, a balanced interaction among agents can be conducted, like different agents related to tourism and climate are building a top-level system.

Another model view can be combinations of simpler equations that are connected to exact activities, such as:

*T**r**a**v**e**l* = *C**a**r**b**o**n* \* *d**i**s**t**a**n**c**e* \* *t**i**m**e* \* *a**d**d**i**t**i**o**n**a**l*\_*e**m**i**s**s**i**o**n*\_*p**a**r**a**m**e**t**e**r**s*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Travel</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Carbon</mml:mi><mml:mo>\*</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">distance</mml:mi><mml:mo>\*</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">time</mml:mi><mml:mo>\*</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">additional\_emission\_parameters</mml:mi></mml:math> (10–21)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi24.png)(10–21) 

*S**o**f**t**w**a**r**e*\_*U**s**e* = *T**e**c**h**n**o**l**o**g**y*\_*C**a**r**b**o**n*(*T**i**m**e*) \* *t**i**m**e* \* \[*C**a**r**b**o**n*(*i**n**c**o**m**i**n**g*)+*C**a**r**b**o**n*(*o**u**t**g**o**i**n**g*)\]<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Software\_Use</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Technology\_Carbon</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Time</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>\*</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">time</mml:mi><mml:mo>\*</mml:mo><mml:mo>\[</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Carbon</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">incoming</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Carbon</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">outgoing</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>\]</mml:mo></mml:math> (10–22)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi25.png)(10–22) 

*R**o**o**m*\_*U**s**e* = *L**i**g**h**t*(*t**i**m**e*) + *T**e**c**h**n**o**l**o**g**y*\_*C**a**r**b**o**n*(*T**i**m**e*) + *a**d**d**i**t**i**o**n**a**l*\_*e**m**i**s**s**i**o**n*\_*p**a**r**a**m**e**t**e**r**s*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Room\_Use</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Light</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">time</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Technology\_Carbon</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Time</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">additional\_emission\_parameters</mml:mi></mml:math> (10–23)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi26.png)(10–23) 

*L**o**c**a**t**i**o**n*\_*T**r**a**v**e**l* = *C**a**r**b**o**n* \* *d**i**s**t**a**n**c**e* \* *t**i**m**e* \* *l**o**c**a**t**i**o**n* − *b**a**s**e**d*\_*e**m**i**s**s**i**o**n*\_*p**a**r**a**m**e**t**e**r**s*<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Location\_Travel</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">Carbon</mml:mi><mml:mo>\*</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">distance</mml:mi><mml:mo>\*</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">time</mml:mi><mml:mo>\*</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">location</mml:mi><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">based\_emission\_parameters</mml:mi></mml:math> (10–24)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi27.png)(10–24) 

Country\_Emission = Models*o**n**e**m**i**s**s**i**o**n*measurement + Location\_Emission(*X*)<mml:math><mml:mtext>Country</mml:mtext><mml:mo>\_</mml:mo><mml:mtext>Emission</mml:mtext><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mtext>Models</mml:mtext><mml:mspace width=".1em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">on</mml:mi><mml:mspace width=".1em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">emission</mml:mi><mml:mspace width=".1em"/><mml:mtext>measurement</mml:mtext><mml:mo>+</mml:mo><mml:mtext>Location</mml:mtext><mml:mo>\_</mml:mo><mml:mtext>Emission</mml:mtext><mml:mo stretchy="false">(</mml:mo><mml:mi>X</mml:mi><mml:mo stretchy="false">)</mml:mo></mml:math> (10–25)

![Equation](images/F00002Xsi28.png)(10–25) 

Such a model needs many equations, which sometimes interact with each other. However, it can be a simple approach to have an instant descriptive analysis. For the forecasting aspects, the descriptive outcomes can be used inside predictive models with past data among the function parameters. In addition to the current draft model for the effects of tourism on the climate, the climate equations can be defined to ensure the desired balance. Such a model can be expanded to include hardware-based solutions. Based on today’s conditions, the model can also be connected to wearables or Internet of Things-based applications for establishing tourism applications. Actually, these can impact tourism as well as trendy areas like health tourism ([Balandina et al., 2015](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib4); [Kose et al., 2024](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib37)). Internet of Things, wearables, and advanced computational methodologies such as artificial intelligence and data mining ensure catalysis roles in improving the use of models for tourism, healthcare, or entertainment purposes ([Aliyah et al., 2023](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib2); [Deperlioglu et al., 2020](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib16); [Hallur et al., 2021](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib27); [Rath et al., 2024](#Ch010_187-198_B978044321905400002X.xhtml#bib51)). So, further research moving from computational models has potential in advanced methodologies and the systems developed around data and smart tools. However, the use of data and smart tools reveals many open problems for the future.

 

## 10.4 Conclusion and future work

This chapter provided a general view regarding how computational modeling can be used within the relationship between tourism and climate change research. As almost all areas of life are associated with climate change and the growing crisis state, it is important to conduct societal and technical research to introduce solutions. At this point, societal and technical research can be done through the computational modeling perspective, which still has the role of human activities, societal, economic, or technological parameters, and eventually some ways to get predictive or descriptive outcomes. Such outcomes will be critical to building solutions and ways to create balanced relations between tourism and climate. The chapter briefly provided some essential background about the importance of tourism as well as its relations with climate change and ensured a literature view regarding two-sided computational modeling efforts. Finally, the chapter suggested approaches to contribute to the literature from the standpoint of computational modeling.
