 12 

# Modeling the effect of meteorological drought on lake level changes with machine learning techniques

Özlem Terzi^([1](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#aff1)), Emine Dilek Taylan^([2](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#aff2)) and Tahsin Baykal^([3](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#aff3)),    ¹Department of Civil Engineering, Technology Faculty, Isparta Applied Sciences University, Isparta, Turkey,    ²Department of Civil Engineering, Engineering and Natural Sciences Faculty, Süleyman Demirel University, Isparta, Turkey,    ³Department of Civil Engineering, Engineering and Natural Sciences Faculty, Kırıkkale University, Kırıkkale, Turkey

 

## Abstract

This study investigates the impact of meteorological droughts on the water level of Eğirdir Lake, indicating hydrological drought, utilizing machine learning (ML) models, including k-nearest neighbors, support vector regression, AdaBoost, Random Forest, extra trees, gradient boosting regressor, XGBoost, and CatBoost algorithms. Initially, standardized precipitation index (SPI) series were calculated for 12-month period, analyzing precipitation data from 1976 to 2019 obtained from meteorological stations in Eğirdir, Senirkent, Uluborlu, Yalvaç, and Isparta. ML models were developed to estimate the water level of Eğirdir Lake exclusively based on SPI values during only dry periods. The CatBoost algorithm gave the best performance among ML models with an *R*² value of 0.71 for the testing set. Hyperparameter tuning for CatBoost model was enhanced with Optuna the success of the model and mitigate overfitting. Therefore the *R*² value of the CatBoost model increased to 0.75. Subsequently, long short-term memory was employed to forecast the next 10-year SPI values for each station. Additionally, the best ML model, utilizing possible SPI values for the next 10 years, forecasts potential water levels for Lake Eğirdir. The study concludes that ML models effectively assess the impact of meteorological droughts on lake water levels and can predict forthcoming water level changes.

 

### Keywords

Meteorological drought; standardized precipitation index; lake level; machine learning; long short-term memory

 

## 12.1 Introduction

Hydrological drought occurs with the continuation of meteorological drought, which occurs when precipitation falls below the mean in a period time and decreases in groundwater and surface water resources ([Gumus, 2017](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib21)). Agricultural drought occurs when the decreasing water resources potential cannot meet the plant water needs ([Dai et al., 2020](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib12)). In this context, it is of great importance to accurately determine the severity, magnitude, and duration of a possible meteorological drought and subsequent hydrological drought to make forward-looking and sustainable planning of water resources in a region.

Standardized precipitation index (SPI) is one of the methods recommended for monitoring meteorological drought at different time scales ([McKee et al., 1993](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib39)). The method is preferred by many researchers because only precipitation data are used ([Alvarez et al., 2024](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib3); [Angelidis et al., 2012](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib4); [Bhunia et al., 2020](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib8); [Latifoğlu & Özger, 2023](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib29); [Młyński et al., 2024](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib40); [Moreira et al., 2006](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib42); [Pande et al., 2023](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib46); [Sakellariou et al., 2024](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib50); [Šebenik et al., 2017](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib52)). [Kumar et al. (2009)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib45) conducted a meteorological drought analysis with the SPI method using long-term precipitation data for the state of Andhra Pradesh in India. They stated that the cumulative probability distribution function of precipitation in the region deviated from the normal distribution in the lower and upper ranges and that different probability distribution functions should be tried. [Abeysingha and Rajapaksha (2020)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib1) analyzed drought with the SPI method in 3-, 6-, and 12-month periods using precipitation data from 54 meteorological stations for Sri Lanka and applied the Mann Kendall trend test to the SPI series. They detected an increasing trend in drought. [Mahmoudi et al. (2021)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib36) conducted a study to determine the most appropriate probability distribution for SPI values when the precipitation data for 63 stations in Iran is zero. [Masoudi and Taheri (2024)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib38) created drought risk maps in drought analysis using precipitation data between 1990 and 2019 for Fars, Iran. The maps showed that the risk of drought increased from southeast to northwest. [Rezaiy and Shabri (2024)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib48) calculated 3-, 6-, 9- and 12-month SPI series for Herat Province of Afghanistan using precipitation data between 1970 and 2019. They decomposed SPI series using Ensemble Empirical Mode Decomposition (EEMD) and made drought prediction with Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average (ARIMA). They stated that EEMD-ARIMA models are more successful than ARIMA.

Natural or artificial lakes are important surface water resources affected by climate change. It also contributes greatly to the regional economy in terms of enabling fishing and recreation activities, meeting the need for drinking and irrigation water, softening the climate, and increasing agricultural productivity. In this regard, it is important to examine the differences in lake water level, which is one of the hydrological drought indicators due to climate change and meteorological drought ([Erkoç, 2023](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib17); [Mtilatila et al., 2020](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib43); [Wang et al., 2018](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib60); [Zhang et al., 2015](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib64)). [Mtilatila et al. (2020)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib43) evaluated the trends of meteorological and hydrological droughts for the lake and river basins. Drought trends to increase in the region due to increasing temperatures and decreasing precipitation. [Liu et al. (2020)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib34) proposed storing excess flow during flood periods to prevent hydrological drought for the Dongting Lake area in China with the innovative flood control scheme.

Machine learning (ML) is algorithm systems based on processing the information given as input with different functions and producing solutions by self-learning. They are established to obtain the most successful prediction with different algorithms and to predict time series data such as drought in hydrology ([Dai et al., 2024](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib13); [Fan et al., 2024](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib18); [Hancock & Khoshgoftaar, 2020](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib22); [Ibrahim et al., 2020](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib24); [Sanjeetha et al., 2021](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib51); [Zhang et al., 2024](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib63)). [Huang et al. (2019)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib23) estimated reference evapotranspiration with category boosting (CatBoost), Random Forest (RF), and support vector machine (SVM) algorithms, using meteorological data from five stations in South China between 2001 and 2015. They said that the CatBoost algorithm gives faster and more accurate results than others. [Kumar et al. (2023)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib27) tried different ML algorithms to predict Garudeshwar Basin stream flows. Among these, they stated that the CatBoost algorithm was successful in modeling hydrological time series. [Saber et al. (2022)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib49) used LightGBM and CatBoost algorithms to model flash flood susceptibility in the Wadi System in Egypt. They stated that LightGBM gave more successful results for 445 flash flood areas. [Wang and Qian (2023)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib59) proposed CatBoost for river network classification. They usedSHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) to increase the success of the model and achieved reasonable success.

[Belayneh and Adamowski (2013)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib7) tried different ML algorithms with artificial neural networks (ANNs), support vector regression (SVR), and wavelet analysis (WA) to predict the meteorological drought determined by SPI for the Awash River Basin in Ethiopia. They stated that WA-ANN methods showed successful performance together in estimating SPI values in a 3-month period. [Citakoglu and Coşkun (2022)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib10) used ANN, Adaptive Neuro Fuzzy Inference System, SVR, Gaussian Process Regression, and k-nearest neighbors (kNN) algorithms to predict the values in *t*+1 month after calculating the SPI series in 1-, 3-, and 6-month periods with the precipitation data of the Sakarya Meteorology station in Turkey. They combined these algorithms with various preprocessing techniques and stated that different hybrid approaches were successful in each period. [Mokhtar et al. (2021)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib41) calculated meteorological drought in the Tibetan Plateau in China with the Standardized Precipitation Evapotranspiration Index (SPEI) in 3- and 6-month periods. They used various ML algorithms to estimate SPEI values. They compared the success of ML algorithms in different input combinations for meteorological variables. [Dash et al. (2024)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib14) used logistic regression, Decision Tree (DT), RF, and SVM algorithms for precipitation prediction and stated that RF model gave the best prediction.

Long short-term memory (LSTM) has a structure that uses the output obtained in the previous step as the input of the current step and organizes the flow of information by keeping it in memory for a short time. It is frequently used in modeling and forecasting time series. This method has achieved high success in estimating nonlinear data. In recent years, the method has been often preferred in hydrology for future predictions ([Baykal et al., 2023](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib6); [Latifoğlu & Nuralan, 2020](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib28); [Swagatika et al., 2024](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib55)). [Xu et al. (2024)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib61) stated that the Cauchy mutation and adaptive weight factor (CABES) method increased the success of LSTM in estimating annual flow values of Xiajiang hydrological station in the Ganjiang River Basin, China. [Kadam et al. (2024)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib25) made a drought assessment with 6-month SPI values using recurrent neural network, gated recurrent unit (GRU), and LSTM deep learning methods for the Marathwada region in India. They stated that the model based on LSTM with 6-month SPI is the most successful model for the region. [Li, Liu et al. (2024)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib31) used LSTM, GRU, and temporal convolutional network deep learning methods to predict the level changes of Polang Lake, located in the middle parts of the Yangtze River in China. They preferred Bayesian model average and Monte Carlo methods to increase the prediction accuracy of the models and stated that GRU gave the best estimations. [Li, Shu et al. (2024)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib32) compared LSTM andbidirectional long short-term memory (BiLSTM) methods in a different study for Polang Lake level prediction. While both methods were found to be successful in short-term forecasts, BiLSTM was found to be more successful in long-term forecasts.

In this study, the effects of meteorological droughts on Eğirdir Lake water level changes, that is, hydrological drought, were examined with ML algorithms. For this purpose, firstly, SPI series were calculated for meteorological drought in a 12-month period using precipitation data between 1976 and 2019 from Eğirdir, Senirkent, Uluborlu, Yalvaç, and Isparta meteorological stations. ML models were developed to estimate the water level of Eğirdir Lake with only SPI values during dry periods as input. Then, the next 10-year (2020–30) SPI values of each station were estimated with LSTM and the possible change of Eğirdir Lake water level was examined with the most appropriate ML model selected. When analyzing the existing studies in the literature, it was seen that ML studies on the prediction of lake water level from meteorological drought were limited. In addition, estimating lake levels using only the dry periods of the SPI series and forecasting possible level changes with LSTM also reveal the novelty of the study.

 

## 12.2 Materials and methods

 

### 12.2.1 Study region

Lake Eğirdir is the second largest freshwater lake in Turkey, located within the borders of Isparta Province, formed by tectonic and karst effects. It is located between 35°37′41″ and 38°16′55″ northern latitudes and 30°44′39″ and 30°57′43″ eastern longitudes ([Fig. 12–1](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0010)). The lake, which has steep rocks and a flat and shallow bottom, has a shoreline length of 150 km. Another of the most important features of Lake Eğirdir is its connection with the sea. The waters coming to Kovada Lake through the 22 km Kovada canal were discharged from the Kovada Valley to Aksu Stream and from there to the Mediterranean via karst paths. For the last 20 years, the waters of the lake have been flowing to the Karacaören I and II Dams built in the Çandır Plain.

![Figure 12–1 The study region. Map lines delineate study areas and do not necessarily depict accepted national boundaries.](images/F000055f12-01-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 12–1](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#Bf0010) The study region. Map lines delineate study areas and do not necessarily depict accepted national boundaries.

The most important water inputs feeding the lake are the springs from the bottom of the lake. At the same time, there are many large and small streams and streams descending from the surrounding mountains and fed by rainwater. The most important of these are the Pupa Stream, which comes from Yalvaç district and flows into the lake from Gelendost district, passing through Akçay, Uluborlu and Senirkent districts, Değirmen Stream descending from Hoyran Plain, and Aksu Stream, which connects to the lake with a channel from Aksu Stream. Eğirdir Lake meets the drinking water needs of Isparta province and surrounding settlements. It is also used as irrigation water in the plains of Isparta, Gönen, Uluborlu, Senirkent, Yalvaç, Gelendost, and Eğirdir ([URL-1](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib57)).

 

### 12.2.2 Standardized precipitation index

SPI is a widely used method developed by [McKee et al. (1993)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib39) to monitor meteorological drought. Abnormalities in dry or wet events at any time scale in a region can be determined by SPI, which depends on only precipitation parameter. It is the index value (SPI) obtained by dividing the difference of the precipitation value (*X*[*i*]) from the mean (*X*[mean]) for the specified period by the standard deviation (*σ*<mml:math><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi></mml:math>![Equation](images/F000055si1.png)) ([Eq. 12.1](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#eqn1)).

${SPI} = \\left( \\frac{x\_{i}{- x}\_{mean}}{\\sigma} \\right)$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">SPI</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>x</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mean</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>&#963;</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:math> (12.1)

![Equation](images/F000055si2.png)(12.1)  

### 12.2.3 Machine learnings algorithms

ML uses a variety of computational methods to generate knowledge from data. Data scientists note that there is no best, one-size-fits-all algorithm for solving a problem. The type of algorithm to be used to obtain the best model may vary depending on the problem type and the number of variables. ML is broadly classified into four groups: supervised (in case of smaller amount of data and clearly labeled for training), unsupervised (usually for large datasets), semisupervised (where unlabeled data are available and retrieving labeled data are exhausting), and reinforcement learning (maximizing cumulative reward concept in an environment of software agents) ([Mahesh, 2020](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib35)). In this study, kNN, SVR, adaptive boosting (AdaBoost), RF, Extra trees (ET), gradient boosting regressor (GBR), eXtreme gradient boosting (XGBoost), and CatBoost algorithms were used.

 

#### 12.2.3.1 k-nearest neighbors

kNN is a nonparametric ML algorithm proposed by [Shakhnarovich et al. (2008)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib53). It relies on data training to predict outcomes. The term closest to the output using the training data is measured by distance. For a data point, the kNN algorithm determines points that are close and similar to the training data and averages the values of these points ([Lee et al., 2021](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib30); [Twumasi & Twumasi, 2022](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib56)).

 

#### 12.2.3.2 Support vector regression

Proposed by [Cortes and Vapnik (1995)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib11), SVR is an ML technique where a model learns the significance of a variable to characterize the relationship between input and output ([Zhang & O'Donnell, 2020](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib62)). It is a statistical learning algorithm that allows reaching a universal solution with a small number of examples. This algorithm is a powerful regression approach for estimating linear and nonlinear systems and has been widely used in different studies to solve estimation problems ([Malik et al., 2021](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib37)). Moreover, having high prediction accuracy increases generalization ability ([Awad et al., 2015](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib5)).

 

#### 12.2.3.3 Adaptive boosting

AdaBoost is one of the ensemble algorithms obtained by combining weak learning algorithms such as DTs to create a stronger learning algorithm. During training of the model, a weight *w*[*i*] is assigned to each sample and this weight is recalculated iteratively. At each iteration, AdaBoost changes the weight *w*[*i*] to reduce the error by reaching training samples with higher error in the prediction value, so that each subsequent regression tree has a reweighted training data set ([Drucker, 1997](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib15)).

 

#### 12.2.3.4 Random forest

RF is an ML algorithm used to solve classification and regression problems that combines the output of multiple randomly generated DTs. It is an alternative technique that provides improved prediction success than general classification and regression tree models ([Vrontos et al., 2021](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib58)). Random decision forests in this ensemble learning method, which makes class (classification) or number (regression) predictions according to the type of problem by creating many DTs in the training phase for classification, regression, and other tasks, eliminates the problems of overfitting DTs to training sets ([Lee et al., 2021](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib30)).

 

#### 12.2.3.5 Extra tree algorithm

ET, one of the ensemble learning methods that belongs to the family of DT-based models, separates the nodes with randomly selected cutoff points, unlike other tree-based combination methods. It uses the entire learning sample when evolving trees to minimize bias ([Geurts et al., 2006](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib20)). When creating a DT, the dependency relationship of extracted data samples is used ([Kumar et al., 2022](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib26); [Sharaff & Gupta, 2019](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib54)). The DT selects the best features based on Gini index given in [Eq. (12.2)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#eqn2).

$\\mathit{gini}\\left( D \\right) = 1 - \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{m}P\_{i}^{2}$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="italic">gini</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mi>D</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>m</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:msubsup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>P</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msubsup></mml:mrow></mml:math> (12.2)

![Equation](images/F000055si3.png)(12.2) 

where *m* denotes the number of output labels and *P*[*i*] represents the probability of belonging to the *i*th output label in the *D* dataset ([Du et al., 2023](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib16)).

 

#### 12.2.3.6 Gradient boosting regressor

GBR, an integrated model having higher performance and stability, is a technique that extends the boosting algorithm for solving the regression problem proposed by [Friedman (2001)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib19). It uses negative gradients of the loss function, such as least squares and least absolute deviation, to solve the minimum value. GBR, which can handle well the noisy and unclean data, support different loss functions, and has powerful prediction capability for nonlinear data, is widely used in research. It prevents the problem of overfitting during the DT learning phase by stopping the growth of the tree as soon as possible ([Li et al., 2018](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib33)).

 

#### 12.2.3.7 eXtreme gradient boosting

XGBoost by developed by [Chen and Guestrin (2016)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib9), is as an improved version of gradient boosting. It has the added benefit of parallel tree boosting and uses a gradient boosting framework. The algorithm overcomes overfitting and achieves higher performance, and additionally uses parallel operations, which reduces computation time. Many weak learners are combined using XGBoost to predict the output with a boosting method ([Naghibi et al., 2020](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib44)).

 

#### 12.2.3.8 Category boosting

CatBoost, a gradient DT algorithm that can handle categorical features better, uses the entire data set for training. Thus it provides categorical characteristics with minimal loss of information ([Prokhorenkova et al., 2018](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib47)). It can combine all categorical features into one new feature. Additionally, it overcomes deviations in the solution with its sequential boosting feature and provides faster results ([Huang et al., 2019](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib23)).

 

## 12.3 Results and discussion

In this study, the effect of meteorological droughts on Eğirdir Lake water level was examined with ML algorithms. For this, firstly, SPI values were calculated for meteorological drought in a 12-month period using precipitation data from Eğirdir, Senirkent, Uluborlu, Yalvaç, and Isparta meteorological stations between 1976 and 2019 ([Fig. 12–2](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0015)). Secondly, ML models were developed to estimate the water level of Lake Eğirdir by using only SPI values during dry periods as input. Then, SPI values for the next 10 years (2020–30) of each station were forecasted with LSTM and the possible change of Eğirdir Lake water level was examined with the most appropriate ML model.

![Figure 12–2 Time series of 12-month standardized precipitation index values.](images/F000055f12-02a-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 12–2 Time series of 12-month standardized precipitation index values.

When [Fig. 12–2](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0015) was examined, a total of 281 months were dry at Eğirdir station, 265 months at Senirkent station, 237 months at Uluborlu station, 217 months at Yalvaç station, and 241 months at Isparta station. While in October 2007, for Eğirdir, Senirkent, and Yalvaç stations the lowest SPI values were obtained as −2.55, −2.03, and −3.24, respectively, they were found as −2.78 and −2.82, respectively, in Uluborlu and Isparta stations in November 1990. At the same time, an SPI value of −2.38 was obtained at Senirkent station in November 1990, and −2.86 at Isparta station in February 2000. The common dry period of all stations was determined as 127 months. ML models were developed using kNN, SVR, AdaBoost, RF, ET, GBR, XGBoost, and CatBoost algorithms. In the models, the dry values of these five stations in the study region were used as input, and the lake water level in these dry months was estimated. The determination coefficient (*R*²) and root mean square error (RMSE) of the developed ML models are shown in [Table 12–1](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#t0010). The calculation of *R*² and RMSE values are given in [Eqs. 12.3](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#eqn3) and [12.4](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#eqn4), respectively.

$R^{2} = 1 - \\frac{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N}\\left( {X\_{i({measured})}{- X}\_{i({model})}} \\right)^{2}}{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N}\\left( {X\_{i{({measured})}} - X\_{i({mean})}} \\right)^{2}}$<mml:math><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mi>R</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">measured</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">model</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">measured</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mean</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac></mml:math> (12.3)

![Equation](images/F000055si4.png)(12.3) 

${RMSE} = \\sqrt{\\frac{1}{N}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N}\\left( {X\_{i({measured})} - X\_{i({model})}} \\right)^{2}}$<mml:math><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">RMSE</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:msqrt><mml:mrow><mml:mfrac><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:mfrac><mml:munderover><mml:mo>&#8721;</mml:mo><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>=</mml:mo><mml:mn>1</mml:mn></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>N</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:munderover><mml:mrow><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mfenced close=")" open="("><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">measured</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msub><mml:mo>&#8722;</mml:mo><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>X</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi>i</mml:mi><mml:mo>(</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">model</mml:mi><mml:mo>)</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:mfenced></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msqrt></mml:math> (12.4)

![Equation](images/F000055si5.png)(12.4) 

where *N* shows the total number of data, *X*[*i*(measured)] is the measured lake water level value, and *X*[*i*(model)] is the result of the ML model. *X*[mean] is the mean amount of measured lake water level values.

In order to increase model performance and prevent overfitting, hyperparameter tuning of the model developed with the CatBoost algorithm were made. Optuna was used as the framework recommended by [Akiba et al. (2019)](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#bib2) due to its scalability and usability in different areas. *R*² and RMSE values for the testing set of the improved CatBoost model after hyperparameter tuning were found to be 0.75 and 0.37, respectively. These values 1 and 0.000025 were obtained for the training set. It was observed that the *R*² value for the testing set of the CatBoost model increased. Hyperparameter values were given in [Table 12–2](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#t0015).

Scatter diagrams for the training and testing sets are given in [Fig. 12–3](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0020). As can be seen from the scatter diagrams, the distribution of estimated and measured values was around the 45-degree line and does not have large deviations.

![Figure 12–3 Scatter diagrams of CatBoost model for training and testing sets.](images/F000055f12-03-9780443219054.jpg)

[Figure 12–3](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#Bf0020) Scatter diagrams of CatBoost model for training and testing sets.

Additionally, feature importance was calculated to understand the effect of input parameters on the CatBoost model ([Fig. 12–4](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0025)). Examining [Fig. 12–4](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0025), it was figured out that the SPI values of the Isparta meteorological station have the highest effect on the water level estimation of Lake Eğirdir, while the SPI values of the Uluborlu station have the lowest effect.

![Figure 12–4 Feature importance chart for CatBoost model.](images/F000055f12-04-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 12–4 Feature importance chart for CatBoost model.

Then, forecasting for the next 10 years were made with LSTM using the SPI values of Eğirdir, Senirkent, Uluborlu, Yalvaç, and Isparta meteorological stations. For this, 80% (409 months of data) of the SPI values between 1976 and 2019 (517 months of data) were used to train LSTM models, and the remaining 20% (108 months of data) was used for testing the models. To determine the LSTM models that give the best results, different units, activation functions, and optimizers were tried and *R*² values were compared. The parameters and *R*² values of the best LSTM models developed for each station are given in [Table 12–3](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#t0020) and the time series in [Fig. 12–5](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0030).

Table 12–3

 The most appropriate hyperparameters and *R*² values for long short-term memory models. 

 
| Station  || Parameters |
| --- | --- |
| Units || Activation || Optimizer || *R*² |
| Eğirdir || 396 || tanh || adam || 0.82 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Senirkent || 460 || relu || adam || 0.80 |
| Uluborlu || 12 || tanh || rmsprop || 0.80 |
| Yalvaç || 364 || relu || rmsprop || 0.75 |
| Isparta || 460 || relu || adam || 0.74 |

![Figure 12–5 Time series of long short-term memory models for each station.](images/F000055f12-05a-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 12–5 Time series of long short-term memory models for each station.

As can be seen from [Table 12–3](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#t0020), the highest success was obtained at Eğirdir station with an *R*² value of 0.82, while the lowest *R*² value was obtained as 0.74 at Isparta station among the LSTM models. While the *R*² value at Senirkent and Uluborlu stations was 0.80, the *R*² value at Yalvaç station was 0.75.

As seen in [Fig. 12–5](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0030), LSTM models correctly forecasted the increases and decreases in SPI values at all stations. After the LSTM models were developed, SPI values for the next 10 years were used as input in the best ML model developed in the previous step, and the lake water levels for the next 10 years were forecasted. The water levels of Eğirdir Lake between 1976 and 2019 are given in [Fig. 12–6](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0035), and the change in Eğirdir Lake water level for the next 10 years is given in [Fig. 12–7](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0040).

![Figure 12–6 Eğirdir Lake water level change between 1976 and 2019.](images/F000055f12-06-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 12–6 Eğirdir Lake water level change between 1976 and 2019.

![Figure 12–7 Eğirdir Lake water level change for the next 10 years.](images/F000055f12-07-9780443219054.jpg)

Figure 12–7 Eğirdir Lake water level change for the next 10 years.

When [Fig. 12–6](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0035) was examined, minimum lake levels were seen as 915.81 m in October 1994, 916.25 m in December 2008, and 916.20 m in December 2018. When these minimum water levels were compared with the driest values seen in November 1990 at the Senirkent, Uluborlu, and Isparta stations given in [Fig. 12–2](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0015), a serious decrease in the lake level was observed approximately 48 months later. At the same time, there was a delay of approximately 12 months between the driest values seen at Eğirdir and Yalvaç stations in October 2007 and the 916.25 m lake level seen in December 2008.

When the possible lake level estimates for the next 10 years given in [Fig. 12–7](#Ch012_227-243_B9780443219054000055.xhtml#f0040) were examined, it was thought that the lake level of 916.94 m seen in August 2021 was due to the dry period seen in all stations between 2017 and 2018. Also, it was shown that the average water level of Eğirdir Lake was approximately 917.30 m in the 1976–2019 observation period, and is approximately 917.50 m for the next 10 years.

 

## 12.4 Conclusions

In this study, ML algorithms were used to examine the relationship between meteorological drought and lake water level. First, 12-month SPI values of Eğirdir, Senirkent, Uluborlu, Yalvaç, and Isparta stations between 1976 and 2019 were calculated. According to the calculated SPI values, a common dry period of 127 months was obtained for these five stations. Then, SPI values for the 127 months were used as input in ML models developed with kNN, SVR, AdaBoost, RF, ET, GBR, XGBoost, and CatBoost algorithms, and Eğirdir Lake water levels were estimated at these months. When the results of developed models were examined, it was determined that the CatBoost algorithm gave the best performance. In order to increase the success of the developed CatBoost model and prevent overfitting, hyperparameter adjustment was made with Optuna. Thus the success of the model increased. In addition, to better understand this model, feature importance values were calculated and the effects of the inputs on the model result were examined. According to feature importance, it has been observed that the most effective station on the CatBoost model developed for Eğirdir Lake water level estimation was Isparta and the influence of the others is also noteworthy.

Secondly, SPI values of each station were obtained for the next 10 years by using LSTM to estimate the possible Eğirdir Lake water level. When the results of the most appropriate LSTM models obtained for each station were examined, it was understood that the calculated and forecasted values were similar in the testing set. Then, SPI values over the all period were used as input in the best LSTM models for each station, and SPI values were forecasted for the next 10 years. Also, SPI values for the next 10 years were used as input in the best ML model and possible water levels of Lake Eğirdir were forecasted. Lake Eğirdir water levels between 1976 and 2019 and the ML model forecasting values of the next 10 years were compared. The comparison revealed that ML models forecasted the average lake water level with a difference of 0.20 m. This study demonstrated that ML models have been successfully used to assess the impact of meteorological droughts on lake water levels and can forecast possible next water level changes. In conclusion, it has been observed that the effect of meteorological drought on the Eğirdir Lake water level is generally between 2 and 4 years. It is thought that this period is sufficient to create early warning systems against possible hydrological drought and to carry out optimum lake management studies.
